[[P1031]]SECTION VI

CHAPTER XXVI

[[Tūntrik|Tāntrik]] doctrines and [[Dharmasastra|Dharmaśāstra]]

In H. of Dh. vol. II. p. 739 while dealing with the worship of Durgā, who is also worshipped as [[Sakti|Śakti]] (cosmic power or energy ), it was stated that the influence of [[Sākta|Śākta]] worship has [[boon|been]] great throughout India and a promise was given that a brief treatment of [[Sāktiem|Śāktism]] would follow in a later volume. It is now time to deal with Śāktas and Tantras, which exercised some influence over the Purāṇas and directly and through the Purāṇas over Indian religious ritual and practices in the medieval ages.

There is a vast literature on Tantras, published and unpublished. There are Buddhist Tantras, Hindu [[Trantras|Tantras]] and Jaina Tantras. There is a philosophical or spiritual side in some of the Tantras which has not been much studied except by Arthur Avalon, B. Bhattacharya and a few others. In the popular mind Tantras [[bave|have]] been associated with the worship of [[Sakti|Śakti]] ( Goddess [[Kāli|Kālī]] ), with mudrās, mantras, [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]], the five makāras, the Dakṣiṇamārga and the Vāma-mārga, and magic practices for acquiring supernatural powers. All that can and will be attempted here is to trace briefly the origin of [[Sāktism|Śāktism]] and Tantra and point out some of the ways in which tantra has entered into Hindu ritual directly and [[indireotly|indirectly]] through the Purāṇas.

The Amarakośa states that tantra means ’ principal matter or part’, ‘siddhānta’ ([[i. e,|i. e.]] doctrine or śāstra ), a loom or [[paren phernalia|paraphernalia]]. But it does not state that tantra means a certain peculiar class of works. Therefore, the inference is not altogether unjustifiable that in its time works bearing the peculiar [[charaoter|character]] of what are called Tantras were either not composed or had not at least attained great vogue. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 71. 9 the word ‘[[ |]]tantra’ [[ooours|occurs]] and appears to mean a loom. ’ These ignorant1 men do not move about lower down ( in this world ) nor in a higher

[[P1032]]world, being neither (learned) brāhmaṇas nor [[some-extracting|soma-extracting]] priests; these resort to speech (of a vile kind) and with that vile speech they engage in handling ploughs and [[looms!|looms’.]] The [[Atharveveda|Atharvaveda]] (X. 7. 42) (’tantram-eke [[yurati|yuvati]] virūpe [[abhya krāmam|abhyā-krāmam]] vayataḥ [[san-mayūkham’|ṣaṇ-mayūkham’]] ) employs the word tantra in the same sense and so does the Tai. Br. II. 5. 5. 3 in a closely similar passage. Pāṇini ([[V.| V.]] 2. 70 ) derives the word ’tantraka’ ([[ |]]a cloth that is recently taken away from a loom ) from ’tantra’. The [[Ap. Sr.|Āp. Śr.]] employs the word2 tantra in the sense of ‘procedure containing many parts’. The [[sān. Sr.|Śāṅ. Śr.]] applies the word tantra to what being once done serves the purpose of many other actions. The Mahābhāṣya on Pāṇini IV. 2. 60 and Vārtika [[‘sarvasāder dvigośca lah’cites ‘garvatantrah’|‘sarvasāder-dvigośca laḥ’ cites ‘sarvatantraḥ’]] and ‘dvitantraḥ’ as examples meaning ‘one who has studied all the tantras’ or ‘one who has studied two tantras’, tantra probably meaning ‘[[siddbānta|siddhānta]]’. In Yāj. I. 228 ([[’tantram va vaisva.devikam’|‘tantraṁ vā vaiśva-devikam’]]) the word tantra is employed in the sense in which the commentary on the [[Sān. Sr.|Śāṅ. Śr.]] takes it. The 15th [[adhikarapa|adhikaraṇa]] of [[Kautilya’s|Kauṭilya’s]] Arthaśāstra bears the title ‘Tantrayukti’ (vide J. O. R., Madras, vol. 4, 1930, p. 82 ff) meaning the main canons or propositions or principles of exposition [[cf|of]] a śāstra. Caraka (Siddhisthāna, chap. 12. 40-45) also speaks of ‘36 tantrasya yuktayaḥ’ and [[Susruta|Suśruta]] ([[ |]]Uttara-tantra, chap. 65 ) names 32 tantrayuktis. In [[Bphaspati|Bṛhaspati]] and [[Katyāyana|Kātyāyana]] and in the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] the word tantra is employed in the sense of ‘doctrine’ or ‘śāstra’. [[Sabara|Śabara]] in his bhāṣya on Jaimini XI. [[I.|1.]] 1 remarks that when [[and|any]] thing or act, once done, becomes useful in several3 matters, that is called ’tantra’. [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] in several passages of his bhāṣya on the [[Vedanta.’|Vedānta-]]sūtras speaks of the Sāṅkhya system as [[sānkhya-tantra|sāṅkhya-tantra]] and of the [[Purvammāmgā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] as [[‘prathama-tantra ‘|‘prathama-tantra’.]]4 In the Kālikā-

Here the पञ्चरात्र is called सात्वततन्त्र. ‘यत्सकृत्कृतं बहुनामुपकरोति तत्तन्त्रमित्युच्यते यथा बहूनां ब्राह्मणानां मध्ये कृतः प्रदीपः ।’ शबर’s भाष्य on जै. XI. 4. 1.]]

[[P1033]]purāṇa (87. 130) the works of [[Usanas and Bphaspati on Rajaniti ( the science of statecraft)|Uśanas and Bṛhaspati on Rājanīti ( the science of statecraft )]] are called tantras and in [[92.2|92. 2]] the Viṣṇudharmottara-purāṇa is called tantra. In all these cases the peculiar meaning attached to the word tantra in medieval times does not occur.

It is difficult to [[determina|determine]] the exact5 time when the word tantra came to [[bo|be]] employed in the sense in which it is used in the [[80-called|so-called]] tantra literature nor is it possible to decide what people first introduced tantra principles and practices or where they first arose. M. M. Haraprasad Shastri was inclined to hold that the principles and practices of tantra came to India from [[out side|outside]] and he relies mainly on a verse in the Kubjikāmatatantra6

in three out of the eight mss. on which the edition is based. Prof. Choudburi further asserts that the guru’s position ‘in tantra’ is neither Vaidika nor Paurāṛika. Here he is wrong. The Vidyāsūkta in Nirakta (II. 4) and the passage of the Svetāśvatara Upanisad quoted in bote 1540 make the Vaidika position of the guru clear. As for the Paorānika position of guru, vide the Lingaparāṇa (quoted in note 1718 below) and Devi bhāgavata (XI. 1. 49.Gurur-brahma’ &c. in the same pota) and the Vorse ‘यस्य देवे पर भक्तिर्यथा देखे तथा शुरौ तस्यैते कथिता पर्याः प्रकाशन्ते महात्मन: चेताया 34. VI, 23 and S . 392, 6.|I. H. Q. Vol. IX p. 358 f. n. ‘गच्छ त्वं भारते वर्षे अधिकाराय सर्वतः । पीठोपपीठक्षेत्रेषु कुरु सृष्टिमनेकधा ॥’. Vide H. P. Sastri’s Cat. of Palm-leaf mss. in Nepal Durbar Library (Calcutta, 1905), Preface p. LXXIX, for the verse in Kubjikāmata, the ms. of which is written in late Gupta characters (i. e. about 7th century A. D.). Dr. B. Bhattacharya appears to hold the same opinion (p. 43 of the work cited in the preceding note). Arthur Avalon in Mahānirvāṇatantra (3rd ed. of 1953 p. 560) holds that tantra was brought into India from Chaldea or Śākadvīpa. In ‘Modern Review’ for 1934 pp. 150–156 Prof. N. N. Choudhuri tries to prove that Indian tāntricism has its origin in the Bon religion of Tibet. He relies on the Tibetan tradition of Asaṅga having introduced Tāntricism in India. But that tradition is mainly based on Taranath’s History of Buddhism. Lama Taranath was born in 1573 A. D. (1575 A. D. according to some) and completed his history in 1608 i. e. he wrote about twelve hundred years after Asaṅga. Prof. Choudhuri further relies on the colophon to Ekajaṭāsādhana (in Sādhanamālā No. 127, आर्यनागार्जुनपादैर्भोटेषु उद्धृतमिति). But this sentence is dropped in three out of the eight mss. on which the edition is based. Prof. Choudhuri further asserts that the guru’s position ‘in tantra’ is neither Vaidika nor Paurāṇika. Here he is wrong. The Vidyāsūkta in Nirukta (II. 4) and the passage of the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad quoted in note 1540 make the Vaidika position of the guru clear. As for the Paurāṇika position of guru, vide the Liṅgapurāṇa (quoted in note 1718 below) and Devībhāgavata (XI. 1. 49 ‘Gurur-brahmā’ &c. in the same note) and the verse ‘यस्य देवे परा भक्तिर्यथा देवे तथा गुरौ । तस्यैते कथिता ह्यर्थाः प्रकाशन्ते महात्मनः ॥’ श्वेताश्व० उप. VI. 23 and अग्निपु. 392. 6.]]

[[P1034]]which says ‘go thou to the country of [[Bharata|Bhārata]] for [[exorotsing|exercising]] dominance on all sides and bring about new creation in [[varioua|various]] ways in [[pithas, upapithas|pīṭhas, upapīṭhas]] and kṣetras’. With all respect to that great scholar it has to be said that the passage does not affirm that tantra principles were then [[unknowe|unknown]] in India and were first introduced in [[Bbārata|Bhārata]] after that verse was uttered. That passage could very well have been uttered even when tantra practices had already taken hold of people’s minds in India and the reference to [[pithas aad ksetras|pīṭhas and kṣetras]] ( in the verse ) clearly indicates that what is meant is only a record of an existing fact, just as the Purāṇas speak in a prophetic vein about what is past. It is possible that a few mystic practices of [[Rulācāra|Kulācāra]] or Vāmācāra owe their origin to foreign elements or sources. But the one [[Verse|verse]] on which M. M. H. P. Shastri relied is far too inadequate for [[proung|proving]] this. The Rudrayāmala ( ed. by Jivananda, 1892) contains a panegyric of the [[Atharvayeda|Atharvaveda]] ( 17th [[patala,|paṭala,]] verse 4), saying that all gods, all beings (on land, in water and air ), [[ali|all]] sages, [[Kamavidyā and Mahavidyā|Kāmavidyā and Mahāvidyā]] dwell in it; verses 10-17 dwell upon the mysterious [[Kundalini,|Kuṇḍalinī,]] verses 31 ff dilate upon Yogic practices and six cakras in the body, verses 51-53 mention Kāmarūpa, [[Jalandhara, Purnagiri, Uddiyāna,|Jālandhara, Pūrṇagiri, Uḍḍiyāna,]] and a few [[otherg 89 Kalika pithas,|others as Kālikā pīṭhas,]] Bagchi in ‘Studies in Tantra’ pp. 45-55 ) adduces some evidence of foreign elements in the tāntrik doctrines. The Rudrayāmala7 (17th [[Patala,|Paṭala,]] [[verges|verses]] 119-125 ) states that [[Mabāvidyā|Mahāvidyā]] appeared to sage [[Vasiṣtha|Vasiṣṭha]] and asked him to go to Cinadeśa and Buddha, who is said to have taught [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]] the [[K’aula|Kaula]] path, Yoga practices for the attainment of siddhis ( extraordinary powers) and directed him to make use of five makāras ( madya &c.) in his [[sūdhanā|sādhanā]] for becoming a perfect Yogin. All this shows that [[pithas|pīṭhas]] flourished in India when the [[Rudrayāmāla|Rudrayāmala]] was composed, that Tāntrik practices had grown in China or Tibet and that Buddha was deemed to have taught those practices, which is a libel and a vile travesty of Buddha’s noble teaching.

“तसो सुनिषः श्रुत्वा महाविपासरस्वतीम् । जगाम चीनभूमौ च यत्र हुएः प्रतिष्ठति “ब उवाच। वसिष्ठ शृणु पक्ष्यामि कुलमार्गमहत्तमम् । पेन विज्ञान(a)माण समरूपी भवेक्षणात्।

असा कुलं समाश्रित्य सर्वसिद्धीपरो भव मासेमाकर्षण सिद्धिदिमासे वाक्यतिभवत् । “शाकि विना शिवोऽशकः किमन्ये जब्तुद्धयः इत्युक्ला दरूपी च कारयामास साधनम् ।

[[P1035]]Magic spells are found in plenty in the [[Atharvayeda|Atharvaveda]] and some mystic words or syllables are used even in the Ṛgveda; e. g. the word [[‘yaṣat,|‘vaṣaṭ’]] occurs in Ṛg. VII. 99. 7, VII. 100. 7 and other verses and the word svāhā8 occurs over a dozen times in the Ṛgveda (e. g. in I. 13. 12, V. 5. 11, VII. 2. 11). A sleep-inducing spell occurs in Ṛg. VII. 55. 5–8,9 which verses occur also in the Atharvaveda IV. 5. 6, 5, 1, 3 and which spell [[waa|was]] probably employed by a purohita to put to sleep some noble man complaining of [[sleepless11888|sleeplessness]] at night. Some Western scholars have held that this hymn is a lover’s charm for [[&|a]] clandestine meeting with his lady-love. But the whole hymn [[ban|has]] hardly any words of love in it and the author is not able to accept that theory as proved. [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] X. 145 is a hymn to be employed against a co-wife, the first verse of which is ‘I dig up this herb, which is a most powerful [[crespor,|creeper,]] by which (a woman) injures her co-wife and by which she secures her husband ( to herself alone )’.

The Ṛgveda frequently mentions magicians who appear to have been mostly non-Aryans described as adeva (godless), anṛtadeva ( worshipping false gods ), śiśnadeva (lecherous, [[Rs.|Ṛg.]] VII. 21. 5, X. 99. 3). For reasons of space, it is not possible to go into great details. Tāntrik works describe the six [[cruel.|cruel]] acts which will be dealt with later on. In the Vedic times it was supposed that some wicked people could by charms and [[incantan tions|incantations]] bring about the death of men and animals or make [[thema|them]] ill. Two long hymns ([[VI.|VII.]] 104 and X. 87 both containing 25 [[Verses)|verses)]] are enough to show how the [[Rgyeda|Ṛgveda]] people were afraid of black magic. Both hymns are full of the words ’ Yātudhāna’ (one who employs black magic) and ‘rakṣas’ (devil or evil spirit), the word yātu being the same as [[’“jadu’|‘jādu’]] (employed in Marathi and other regional Indian languages). There were female evil spirits also [[oalled|called]] [[‘pisaoi’|‘piśācī’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 133. 5 ‘O Indra! destroy the reddish very powerful [[pisāoi|piśācī]] and kill all evil spirits )’. A few verses from the Ṛgveda may be [[tradslated|translated]]

या! “मोठेशया पोशया नाहीस्तल्याशीवरी। सियो या पुण्यगन्धास्ता सर्वाः स्थापयामसि । R. VII. 35. 5-8.|सस्तु माता सस्तु पिता सस्तु श्वा सस्तु विश्पतिः । ससन्तु सर्वे ज्ञातयः सस्त्वयमभितो जनः ॥ य आस्ते यश्च चरति यश्च पश्यति नो जनः । तेषां सं हन्मो अक्षाणि यथेदं हर्म्यं तथा । … प्रोष्ठेशया वह्येशया नारीस्तल्पशीवरीः । स्त्रियो याः पुण्यगन्धास्ताः सर्वाः स्वापयामसि ॥ ऋ. VII. 55. 5–8.]]

[[P1036]]here. ‘May I (Vasiṣṭha) die this very day if I [[bo &|be a]] practitioner of black magic or if I have scorched the life of any person; may he, who falsely called me a practitioner of black [[magio,|magic,]] lose [[bis|his]] ten sons; may Indra kill with a terrible weapon him who called me [[yūtudhāna,|yātudhāna,]] though I am not so and who, being himself a rakṣas, declares himself to be [[purs;|pure;]] may he, being most wretched, fall below all beings (Rg. VII. 104. 15–16); O Maruts! may you spread in different places among the people, and wish to seize the devils and pound to dust the devils (rakṣasaḥ) who [[Assuming|assuming]] the form of birds fly about at night and who, when the sacrifice is shining, produce deadly obstacles ( ibid. verse 18 ); O Indra! kill the male practitioner of black magic and also the female ( magician) that destroys with wiles; may the ( devils ) worshippers of foolish deities perish with their necks cut off; and may they not be able to see the sun rising (Rg. VII. 104. 24); O Agni, split the skin of the yātudhāna, may thy [[destruc. tiye|destructive]] bolt kill him by its heat; O [[Jātapedas /|Jātavedas!]] shatter his joints, may some carnivorous beast longing for flesh seek ( devour ) the broken ( yātudhāna ); O Agni! shatter the yātudhānas by your heat, and the rakṣas by your glow and destroy the worshippers of foolish gods ([[mūradevūn)|mūradevān)]] and, shining towards those that feed on the lives of men ([[asulgpah),|asutṛpaḥ),]] shatter them’ (Rg. X. 87. 5 and 14).

• In the Āp. Gṛ. (III. 9. 5–8) it is said that the plant used by the co-wife is called [[Pathā|Pāṭhā]] and the hymn (Rg. X. 145 ) is employed for securing domination over the husband and for harming a co-wife. Rg. I. 191 is a charm against various poisons. In the Atharvaveda there are numerous [[hymds|hymns]] styled ‘śatrunāśana’ (destructive of enemies) e. g. II. 12–24, III. 6, IV. 3 and 40, V. 8, VI. 6, 65–67 and 134. Atharva II. 11 is styled ‘Kṛtyā-dūṣaṇa’ (counteracting black magic). A [[fow|few]] of the typical verses may be cited10 here. ‘Employ [[magio|magic]] spells against him, who hates us and whom we hate; attain (i. e. dominate) him who is superior and surpass him who is (our) equal’; ‘O Soma! strike in the mouth with your thunder-bolt him who speaks evil of us that speak what is good and may he, being crushed, run away’. The [[Sukranitisārs|Śukranītisāra]] (ed. by G. Oppert, 1882 ) provides that the Tantras are the Upaveda of the

[[P1037]]Atharvaveda. Atharva III. 25 and VI. 130 are spells respectively employed by a man and a woman to soften the heart of the person loved; Atharva II. 30 and 31 are charms for driving away or destroying worms that cause diseases and V. 36 is a charm against [[pisācas|piśācas]] (goblins ).11 The sound ‘phaṭ’ occurs in Vāj. S. In the [[Ap. Sr. Sātra|Āp. Śr. Sūtra]] phaṭ is employed in offering Soma stalks in [[abhi cūra|abhicāra]] ( employment of spells for a malevolent purpose ). Phaṭ is a sound frequently employed in the worship of Devī in Tantra works. But no direct connection or line of evolution from the Atharvaveda to the Tantras can be traced. The [[Tattvasangraha|Tattvasaṅgraha]] of [[śāntarakṣita|Śāntarakṣita]] (705–762 A. D.) connects even Buddha with [[magio|magic]] practices. It says ‘all wise [[mon|men]] declare that it is dharma from which results worldly prosperity and the highest beatitude. Seen results such as intelligence, health, rulership are produced by properly observing the rules about mantras, yoga and the like declared by him (i. e.12 by Buddha)’. But one cannot place implicit reliance on any writer’s statement made more than a thousand [[yean|years]] after the event or person referred to in it. There are, however, stories in the Pali sacred books about the cultivation of magic powers among Buddha’s own disciples, [[o. g.|e. g.]] the story of [[Bharadvāja 1672|Bhāradvāja]]13 who rose in the air for [[&|a]] bowl carved out of very fragrant

Some take it to mean part in contradistinction to निःश्रेयस (which means मोक्ष or अस्तत्व).|यतोऽभ्युदयनिष्पत्तियतो निःश्रेयसस्य च । स धर्म उच्यते तादृक् सर्वैरेव विचक्षणैः ॥ तदुक्तमन्त्रयोगादिनियमाद्विधिवत्कृतात् । प्रज्ञारोग्यविभुत्वादि दृष्टधर्मोऽपि जायते ॥ तत्त्वसंग्रह p. 905; कमलशील (pupil of शान्तरक्षित) comments ‘तेन भगवतोक्तश्वासौ मन्त्रयोगादिनियमश्चेति विग्रहः । योगः समाधिः । आदिशब्देन मुद्रामण्डलादिपरिग्रहः ।’. The first verse appears to have been based on the वैशेषिकसूत्र I. 1. 1–2 ‘अथातो धर्म व्याख्यास्यामः । यतोऽभ्युदयनिःश्रेयससिद्धिः स धर्मः ।’. The word अभ्युदय has been variously interpreted by the commentators of Kaṇāda’s sūtra, but generally अभ्युदय means ‘wordly happiness or prosperity’; compare भरत’s नाट्यशास्त्र ‘विवाहप्रसवावाहप्रमोदाभ्युदयादिषु । विनोदकरणं चैव वृत्तमेतत्प्रकीर्तितम् ॥’ chap. IV. 263 (G. O. S.). Some take it to mean स्वर्ग in contradistinction to निःश्रेयस (which means मोक्ष or अमृतत्व).]]

[[P1038]]sandalwood. Further, there is a story of miraculous powers possessed by all the members of the family of a layman called [[Mendaka|Meṇḍaka]] (viz. himself, his wife, son and daughter-in-law ) in Mahāvagga VI. 34. 1 ff. ([[8.|S.]] B. E. vol. [[XVI.|XVII.]] pp. 121 ff). [[Honey|Here]] again we have to remember that there is nothing in the [[Tipitaka|Tipiṭaka]] or any early Buddhist document to prove that Buddha or his first disciples had anything to do with [[mudrūs,|mudrās,]] mantras and [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] and that neither Yuan Chwang nor I-tsing refers to any Tantras, though both of them refer to the Buddhist [[mona stories|monasteries]] as centres of Buddhist culture (vide Dr. De in N. I. [[a.|A.]] vol. I. pp. 1 ff). In the introduction to [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] (vol. II. [[LXVIII.)|p. LXVIII.)]] Dr. Bhattacharya relies upon the words [[‘Sugatom padistam’|‘Sugatōpadiṣṭaṁ’]] and ‘Sugataiḥ’ occurring on pp. 334–335 of the [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] for holding that Buddha himself must have promulgated some mantras. There are two weighty objections [[this|viz.]] ‘Sugataiḥ’ does not always mean Buddha, but means also ‘followers of Buddha’ and secondly, just as most Hindu Tantras are dialogues between Śiva and [[Parvati,|Pārvatī,]] so later Buddhist writers might have easily said that they [[aro|are]] quoting the Buddha; the same objection applies to [[Kamalaśila’s romarks|Kamalaśīla’s remarks]] quoted by Dr. Bhattacharya, [[43|as]] [[Kamalasila|Kamalaśīla]] and his teacher [[tourisbed|flourished]] about 1200 years after Buddha.

The question whether Buddhist Tantras were prior to Hindu Tantras or vice versa is difficult to decide. It appears probable that both arose nearly about the same time. Vide ‘The [[Sāktas’|Śāktas’]] by E. A. Payne pp. 72–74 for discussion of views. In the [[Sādbanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] (a Vajrayāna work consisting of 312 small works composed according to Dr. Bhattacharya from the 3rd century [[A, D.|A. D.]] to 12th century A. D.) four [[pithas|pīṭhas]] (chief centres) of Vajrayāna14 are mentioned viz. Kāmākhyā, Sirihaṭṭa (or [[Srthatta ),|Śrīhaṭṭa ),]] Pūrṇagiri and [[Uddiyana.|Uḍḍiyāna.]] The first two are [[reaper|respec-]]

[[P1039]]tively identified with [[Kamakhya or Kamarūpa|Kāmākhyā or Kāmarūpa]] ( three miles from Gauhati) and modern Sylhet. The exact situation of the other two is a controversial matter. M. M. [[E.|H.]] P. Shastri identified [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] (which is most frequently mentioned as a [[pitha|pīṭha]] ) with Orissa. His son Dr. B. Bhattacharya thinks it most probable that Vajrayāna [[Tāntrioism|Tāntricism]] arose in [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] (p. 46 of Intro. to B. E.). Dr. Bagchi in ‘Studies in the Tantras’ pp. 37-40 furnishes good grounds for holding that [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] was near Swat [[valloy|valley]] in N. W. India and Grousset ‘In the footsteps of Buddha’ pp. 109-110 holds the same view. The [[Bārbaspatya Sūtra|Bārhaspatyasūtra]] (ed. by F. W. Thomas) names eight [[sāktaksetras|Śāktakṣetras]] (III. 123–124). In his Intro. to [[Bādhanamala|Sādhanamālā]] (vol. II. p. LXXVIII) Dr. Bhattacharya holds that the Hindu tantras were introduced on the model of the Buddhist [[tantrag.|tantras.]] But Winternitz (in ‘History of Indian Literature’, Eng. tr., vol. II. p. 401) states that this view of Dr. Bhattacharya is contrary to the facts and the present writer agrees with this view.

Though Dr. Bhattacharya admits that Buddhism and Jainism exploited Hindu gods in the earlier period, he asserts (on p. 147 of his Intro. to B. E.) that ‘it is possible to declare without fear of contradiction that the Buddhists were the first to introduce the Tantras in their religion and that the Hindus borrowed them from the Buddhists in later times’. It is no honour to ancient Hindus to be called the pioneers in the practice of black [[magio.|magic.]] But scholars have to seek truth irrespective of the question of honour or dishonour. Very weighty arguments are advanced by Valleé Poussin (in E. R. E. vol. XII, p. 193), Winternitz and Payne (on [[’|‘Śāktas]] p. 73 ) for the opposite view and the present author [[agroos|agrees]] with them. Hundreds of works were translated from Sanskrit into Tibetan and Chinese. The [[traffio|traffic]] of borrowing has been all one way from India to Tibet and China. Vide a paper on ‘China’s debt to India’ by Prof. Liang Chi Chao in [[Viśvabhārati|Viśvabhāratī]] Quarterly, vol. II, for 1924-25 pp. 251–261, where it is stated that 24 Hindu scholars from A. D. 67 to 789 came to China, besides 13 from Kashmir and that the Chinese scholars that went to India for study from 265-790 A. D. numbered 187 of which the names of 105 can be ascertained.

[[P1040]]There is hardly any evidence of the translations of Chinese or Tibetan works into Sanskrit. Besides, the three great Chinese travellers never refer to the study of Buddhist Tantras in India. Watters [[On|on]] ‘Yuan Chwang’s Travels in India ’ vol. I. p. 360 [[narratos|narrates]] a story from the pilgrim’s life that when he left Ayodhyā in a boat and proceeded east down the Ganges, thugs that looted the boat decided to sacrifice him to Durgā but that the Chinese pilgrim was saved by a hurricane which put the thugs in terror, who released him and treated him with [[Teveronce.|reverence.]] Vide also ‘In the Footsteps of Buddha’ by René Grousset pp. 133-135 for this incident. We see that there is evidence of the prevalence of Tāntrik and Śākta worship in India long before the 7th century A. D. There is hardly any evidence of any Buddhist Tāntrik work before 650 [[A, D.|A. D.]] except [[perbaps|perhaps]] the [[Guhyasamajatantra|Guhyasamājatantra]] and [[Manjusrimūlakalpa,|Mañjuśrīmūlakalpa,]] both of which contain late elements. In this way, presumption and chronology are both against borrowing by Hinduism from Buddhist Tibetan or Chinese Tāntrik works. Vide ‘Tibet past and present’ by Sir Charles Bell (1924) pp. 23, 25, 29, Sardar K. M. Panikkar’s work ‘India and China’ (1957) p. 70, [[‘Intro duction|‘Introduction]] of the alphabet in Tibet’ by M. M. Dr. Satischandra who holds that it was borrowed from Magadha in 7th Century A. D., that show that a written script based on an Indian alphabet as prevalent in Kashmir was first introduced about 640 A. D., that the Tāntrik Buddhist Padmasambhava was summoned from [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] by a Tibetan king [[Ti-son Do-tson|Ti-son De-tson]] (749-786 A. D.) on the recommendation of [[Santarakṣita|Śāntarakṣita]] Bodhisattva and was induced to settle in Tibet. Bunjiu Nanjio’s ‘Catalogue of [[Tripitaka’|Tripiṭaka’]] (Oxford, 1883), appendix II. p. 445 No. 155 shows that Amoghavajra translated many works between 746 and 771 A. D., died in 774 A. D. and that it was under his influence that Tantra doctrines gained currency in China. It is clear from the works of Bāṇa as detailed later on that worship of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] with [[wide|wine]] and flesh was prevalent in India long before 600 A. D., that [[Śriparvata|Śrīparvata]] was famed for its tāntrik siddhis, that [[sivasamhitas|Śivasaṁhitās]] existed, that japa of mantras a crore of times in a cemetery was [[gupposed|supposed]] to confer siddhis, that [[he|the]] 14th of the dark half of a month was deemed the proper tithi for japa and black magic. Therefore, it is most likely that [[Sākta|Śākta]] or Tāntrik [[dootrines|doctrines]] were taken to China and Tibet from India and not vice versa. Prof. P. V. Bapat in ‘2500 years of Buddhism’ (pp. 360-376) follows (at p. 363) Dr. B. Bhattacharya and tries to prove that Tibetan Tāntrism is earlier than Hindu [[Tantrism,|Tāntrism,]] but his [[argumento|arguments]]

[[P1041]]like those of Dr. Bhattacharya do not [[oarry|carry]] conviction at all. Dr. A. S. Altekar in his paper on Sanskrit Literature in Tibet ( ABORI. [[Vol.|vol.]] 35 pp. [[54-66|54–66]] ) shows how Buddhism entered into Tibet in the reign of [[Strong-Tsan-Gampo|Sroṅg-Tsan-Gampo]] ([[637-693 A, D.|637–693 A. D.]] ), that about 750 A. D. [[ |]]Padmasambhava from Orissa and Vairocana from Kashmir were pioneers and how about 4500 works were translated into Tibetan.

Even Dr. Bhattacharya admits that Buddhist Tantras in outward appearance resemble in a marked degree Hindu Tantras (p. 47 of Intro. to B. E.), but he contends that in the subject [[inatter,|matter,]] the philosophical doctrines and religious principles there is little similarity. As Buddhism did not believe in Hindu gods they do not speak about [[Sakti or Sāktism.|Śakti or Śāktism.]] But just as in Hindu Tantras there is the male principle Śiva and the female principle of [[Devi,|Devī,]] the Buddhists postulate Prajñā (which is feminine) and Upāya (masculine) as two principles and invest them with the same roles as those of [[Siya and Davi but reyorsed|Śiva and Devī but reversed]] in character. They had to graft on the idea of [[Sunyata|Śūnyatā]] the ideas underlying the concepts of [[Siya and Devi or Sakti.|Śiva and Devī or Śakti.]] The subject matter is very similar as regards the goal and means ( Yoga [[&o|&c]] ) and the procedure of mantra, guru, [[mandala|maṇḍala]] &c. is the same. The most important and early works of Buddhist Tāntric cult, the [[Prajñopāya-viniscayasiddhi and Jõānasiddhi|Prajñopāya-viniścayasiddhi and Jñānasiddhi]] are not earlier than the 8th century A. D., when [[Sāktism and Tantrismo had both boon|Śāktism and Tāntrism had both been]] long established in India.

The word ‘[[Sākta |Śākta]]’ means one who is a worshipper or devotee of ‘[[Sakti’|Śakti]]’ (cosmic power or energy ). It appears that long before the 8th century A. D. this cult had spread in almost all parts of India, particularly in Bengal and Assam. [[Sakti|Śakti]] under different names (such as Tripurā, [[Lohita, Sdaśikā, Kāmeśvari|Lohitā, Ṣoḍaśikā, Kāmeśvarī]] ) was conceived to be the primordial principle of all activity in the universe and is generally worshipped under the name of [[Devi.|Devī.]] The [[Devīmābātmya|Devīmāhātmya]] is one of the chief works of the [[Saktas|Śāktas]] and has been described above on pp. [[155-156.|155–156.]] The chief characteristics of the [[sākta|Śākta]] cult are the [[doctrinc|doctrine]] that God or Deity is one and is to be conceived as the mother and also the [[destroyor|destroyer]]15 and that there is a special form of ceremonial

(Vepk, ed.)|शवारूढां महाभीमां घोरदंष्ट्रां हसन्मुखीम् । चतुर्भुजां खड्गमुण्ड-वराभयकरां शिवाम् ॥ मुण्डमालाधरां देवीं ललज्जिह्वां दिगम्बराम् । एवं सञ्चिन्तयेत्कालीं श्मशानालयवासिनीम् ॥ कालीतन्त्र in शाक्तप्रमोद (Venk. ed.)]]

[[P1042]]worship which sometimes assumed debased and revolting forms. [[Dari|Devī]] has been eulogised in other Purāṇas also as in [[Vamana (18-19), Doyl-bhagavata (III. 27), Brahmānda (that oontains the Lalitāmāhātmaya|Vāmana (18–19), Devī-bhāgavata (III. 27), Brahmāṇḍa (that contains the Lalitāmāhātmya]] in 44 chapters), Matsya (13. [[24-54,|24–54,]] where 108 names of [[Devi|Devī]] and 108 places of her worship are set out), Kūrma (I. 12). In the last Purāṇa (Kūrma I. 12 ) [[Devi|Devī]] is called [[Mahamahiṣamardini|Mahāmahiṣamardinī]] (98), [[Anahata, Kundalini|Anāhatā, Kuṇḍalinī]] (128), Durgā, [[Katyayani, Candi, Bhadrakali|Kātyāyanī, Caṇḍī, Bhadrakālī]] (143 and 148 ) and it is stated that the śāstras opposed16 to the Veda and [[Smrti|Smṛti]] that are popular among people such as Kāpāla, Bhairava, [[Yamala, Vāma, Arbata|Yāmala, Vāma, Ārhata]] were propagated by [[Devi|Devī]] for deluding the world and were based on ignorance. Vide also Brahmapurāṇa ([[181.48-52|181. 48–52]] ) for names of [[Devi|Devī]] and for the proposition that [[Devi|Devī]] when worshipped with offerings of wine, flesh and other edibles becomes pleased and grants men’s desires. [[Bhadrakāli|Bhadrakālī]] is [[com. paratively|comparatively]] an ancient name. In the [[Sāó. Gr.(SBE|Śāṅ. Gṛ. (SBE]] vol. XXIX p. 86 ) it is provided that an offering is to be made to [[Sri|Śrī]] at the head of the bed on which the householder sleeps and at the foot of the bed to [[Bhadra-kāli,|Bhadra-kālī,]] while Manu provides that, in the daily [[baliharana,|baliharaṇa,]] bali ([[offering )|offering)]] is to be offered to [[Bhadrakali|Bhadrakālī]] in the south-west.

The Tantras and [[Sākta|Śākta]] works have much in common, the main point of difference being that in the [[Sākta|Śākta]] cult [[Devi|Devī]] (or Śakti) is worshipped as the highest, while Tantras (which include also Buddhist and Jaina works) are not restricted to the worship of [[Devi or Sakti,|Devī or Śakti,]] but may be agnostic, Vedāntic or Sāṅkhya in their philosophical outlook. Dr. B. Bhattacharya (in Intro. to [[Gubyasamājatantra|Guhyasamājatantra]] p. XXXIV and in [[Intro, to Sadhananālā|Intro. to Sādhanamālā]] vol. II, p. XIX) states that, for a work to be called a real Tantra, there must be the element of [[Sakti|Śakti]] in it. But this is plainly rather an over-statement. The Vāyupurāṇa [[enume. rates|enumerates]] the [[Sākta|Śākta]] among six darśanas ( philosophio points of view) as in note 17

Even the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] speaks of the [[Saktis|Śaktis]] of the great gods of the Vedic pantheon. But the [[Sakti or Saktis|Śakti or Śaktis]] are of the God

[[P1043]][[nimself|himself]] and not a separate creative principle and sometimes [[Sakti|Śakti]] is meant as part of the poet, [[priøst|priest]] or sacrificer ( as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 31. 18, I. 83. 3, IV. [[22, 8,|22. 8,]] X. 25. 5). The word ‘Śakti’ occurs in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] about a dozen times in the singular as well as in the plural, five times with Indra,18 once with Aśvins ([[Rg. II. 39.7),|Ṛg. II. 39. 7 ),]] twice with [[pitps (I 109.3,|pitṛs ( I. 109. 3,]] VI. [[75.9)|75. 9 )]] and once with gods in general (X. 88. 10, who are said to have created Agni with their powers). Sometimes, the word ‘Māyā’ is used with regard to Indra instead of the word Śakti. ‘O Indra! I long for your great friendship and powers ([[saktih|śaktīḥ]] )…understand that you are our great protector; I proclaim your ancient and recent deeds, O Indra endowed with powers ([[Saktivah|Śaktīvaḥ]])!……; ‘Indra assuming19 many forms by his powers (māyābhiḥ) repairs ([[to many saorifices), ton|to many sacrifices), ten]] hundred horses are yoked to his chariot’. In these passages there is no question of [[worship ping sakti or faktis|worshipping śakti or śaktis]] of the god praised. The more frequent word, however, is [[‘Saci’ (‘sacibhiḥ’|‘Śacī’ (‘śacībhiḥ’]] occurring 36 times and [[‘sacyā’|‘śacyā’]] 12 times). The word [[‘Sacipati’|‘Śacīpati’]] (lord of [[Saci|Śacī]] or power) occurs sixteen times in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] and has been applied in all places to Indra, except once in [[Rg. VII, 67.5|Ṛg. VII. 67. 5]] (where it is applied to [[Asvins).|Aśvins).]] It cannot be said that in the [[Rgveda Saci’|Ṛgveda ‘Śacī’]] is the wife of Indra (as it is said in later mythology), since the plural is more frequent than [[Saoi|Śacī]] in the singular and since [[Aśving|Aśvins]] also are called [[‘sacipati’.|‘Śacīpati’]] once. Similarly, the word [[‘Sacīvaḥ|‘Śacīvaḥ’]] occurs eleven times, in nine of which it is addressed to Indra but it is once applied to Agni ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 21. 4) and once to Soma ([[Rg. IX. 87.9).|Ṛg. IX. 87. 9).]] The ideas associated with the words [[’. Sakti’ and Saci’|‘Śakti’ and ‘Śacī’]] are those of creation, protection, valour, and bounty. In [[Rg. I 56. 4|Ṛg. I. 56. 4]] Indra’s power is called [[‘Devi tayiṣi’|‘Devī taviṣī’]] but the word [[Saci|Śacī]] does not occur in that verse. There is a sublime hymn ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 125 ) of the power of Vāk ( speech), wherein [[Vak|Vāk]] is said to associate with Rudras, [[Adityas,|Ādityas,]] Vasus, all gods and is

[[P1044]]declared to support Mitra and Varuṇa, Indra and [[Agai|Agni]], the [[Asyins|Aśvins]], Soma, [[Tvaṣtr|Tvaṣṭṛ]], Pūṣan and Bhaga. [[Vak|Vāk]] is said to stretch the bow for Rudra in order that the destructive [[enerny|enemy]] of brahma (prayer or God Brahmā) may be killed, that Vāk stands [[occupy. ing|occupying]] all worlds and that her body touches [[leavon|heaven]], that it is beyond heaven and the earth, that Vāk stands so vast by its greatness’. Vāk becomes the principle of all energy. [[Acoording|According]] to the [[Nighanṭu|Nighaṇṭu]] (I. 11), menā, gnāḥ and [[saci|śacī]] are three of the 57 words meaning ‘Vāk’. In Tai. S. V. [[1.7.2|1. 7. 2]] the metres are called [[’.gnās’|‘gnās’]]. [[Rg. I. 164, 41|Ṛg. I. 164. 41]] is an [[enigmatio|enigmatic]] description of [[Vak|Vāk]] explained in Nirukta XI. 40. It should be noticed that, just as [[Dovi|Devī]] or [[Sakti|Śakti]] is associated with Śiva in later literature, so are [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]], Varuṇānī, [[Agnāyi|Agnāyī]], [[Rodasi|Rodasī]] associated with Indra, [[Varupa|Varuṇa]], [[Agai|Agni]] and Maruts respectively as wives. ‘I invoke [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]],20 [[Varupāni|Varuṇānī]] and [[Agnāyi|Agnāyī]] for my welfare and for [[drink iog|drinking]] Soma’; ‘May the women, the wives of gods, partake of the offering, viz. [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]], [[Agnayi|Agnāyī]], the brilliant ([[wife)|(wife)]] of the [[Asyins|Aśvins]], [[Rodasi|Rodasī]]; may [[Varuṇāṇi|Varuṇānī]] listen (to our laud); may the goddesses partake (of offerings) at the time (appropriate) for women’. It must be said, however, that these goddesses ([[deviḥ|devīḥ]]) play a very subordinate part in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]]. No direct connection can be traced between these Vedic goddesses and the later [[concep tion|conception]] of [[Devi|Devī]] or [[Sakti|Śakti]]. [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]] is invoked for protection in [[Rg. I 22. 12|Ṛg. I. 22. 12]], II. 32. 8, V. 46. 8, X. 86. [[11-12|11–12]]. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 46. 8 [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]] and three others are called Devapatnīs and ‘gnās’. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 61. 8 it is said that the gnās, the wives of gods, [[wovo|wove]], when Indra struck the demon [[Abi|Ahi]], a song of worship. The word ‘gnā’ occurs 20 times in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] in the nominative, objective, [[instru mental|instrumental]] and locative and is an Indo-European word for wife (Greek has it). Vide Nir. III. 21 [[whera|where]] ‘menā’ and ‘gnā’ occur. In the Kenopaniṣad Umā [[Haimavati|Haimavatī]] (daughter of Himavat) tells of Brahma to the gods Agni, [[Vayu|Vāyu]] and Indra (III. 12). In the Śvetāśvataropaniṣad it is said ’they ([[brahma Yādins|brahmavādins]]) endowed with meditation and Yoga saw Śakti (power) abiding as non-different from God and concealed (from [[com|com-]]

[[P1045]]prehension) by its own attributes (or by sattva, rajas and tamas).’ The same [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] (in VI. 8) speaks of Brahma as possessing the highest [[Sakti|Śakti]]21 in various forms and this text is quoted by Śaṅkarācārya on Vedāntasūtra II. 1. 24. In the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on Vedāntasūtra II. 1. 30 and in the sūtra itself Brahma is said to be endowed with all powers. Vide also [[Svetasva.|Śvetāśva.]] IV. 1. The [[Nārāyanopaniṣad|Nārāyaṇopaniṣad]] contains an invocation of [[Durgā-devi|Durgā-devī]]22 ‘I approach as a refuge the blazing goddess Durgā, brilliant like fire, luminous owing to tapas, that is resorted to for (yielding) the rewards of religious actions; O goddess possessed of excellent [[mighti|might!]] adoration to your power’. [[Raghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]]23 [[avera|avers]] that Tantra cult is based on Śruti as can be seen from the Upaniṣads called [[Rāmapūrvottara- tāpaniya|Rāmapūrvottara-tāpanīya]] and [[Nṭsimbapūr vottara-tāpaniya|Nṛsiṃhapūrvottara-tāpanīya]]. Similarly, Bhāskararāya in his commentary Setubandha on Vāmakeśvaratantra mentions several [[Upa niṣads|Upaniṣads]] as treating in detail of the bhakti of Mahātripura-sundarī and interprets [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 47. [[4’catvari|4 ‘catvāri]] [[im’|īm’]] as referring to [[Kadividya,|Kādividyā.]] But all these Upaniṣads seem to be purposely composed to bolster up the tantras that had come to be looked down upon and they

[[P1046]]are mostly mentioned by late [[medievel|medieval]] writers like [[Raghaven bhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] and [[Bhaskararārya.|Bhāskararāya.]] There are two great hymns addressed to Durgā in the Mahābhārata,24 viz. in [[Virātaparya|Virāṭaparva]] (chap. 6) by [[Yudhisthira|Yudhiṣṭhira]] and the other in [[Bhiṣmaparya|Bhīṣmaparva]] (chap. 23) by Arjuna, but they are discarded as apocryphal in the Poona [[oritical|critical]] edition. The Gangadhara stone Inscription of Viśvavarman of 480 of the Mālava era (424 A.D.) refers to [[Mātra|Mātṛs]] (Mother [[Goddesses )|Goddesses)]] and Tantra.25 The [[Br, S.|Bṛ. S.]] 57. 56 mentions the groups of [[Mātpe.|Mātṛs.]] The [[Výddha-Hārita-smrti|Vṛddha-Hārīta-smṛti]] [[rocommonds|recommends]] that the householder should not enter places of [[Saira,|Śaiva,]] Bauddha, Skānda and [[Sākta|Śākta]]26 cults. The Viṣṇupurāṇa27 (one of the earliest among extant Purāṇas) speaks of the whole world as that of Viṣṇu, who is the highest Brahma and is endowed with

TYP a Pere V. 1. 86. This verse occurs in

M ay 181. $2 ead the preceding three verses which contaia the games of Dargi are the same in both,|एतत्सर्वमिदं विश्वं जगदेतच्चराचरम् । परब्रह्मस्वरूपस्य विष्णोः शक्तिसमन्वितम् ॥ विष्णुपु V. 7. 60; सुरामांसोपहारैश्च भक्ष्यभोज्यैश्च पूजिता । नृणामशेषकामांस्त्वं प्रसन्ना संप्रदास्यसि ॥ विष्णुपु. V. 1. 86. This verse occurs in ब्रह्मपुराण 181. 52 and the preceding three verses which contain the names of Durgā are the same in both.]]

[[P1047]][[Sakti|Śakti]], enumerates some of the names of Durgā as [[Ārya|Āryā]], Vedagarbhā, Ambikā, Bhadrā, [[Bhadra-kāli|Bhadra-kālī]], [[Ksomadā|Kṣemadā]], Bhāgyadā and winds up by stating that when Durgā is worshipped with offerings of wine, flesh, various kinds of foods, she, being pleased, would fulfil all desires of men. In the [[Kādambari|Kādambarī]] of [[Bāṇabhatta|Bāṇabhaṭṭa]] there is a long description of the temple of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] at a few days’ journey from [[Ujjayini,|Ujjayinī,]] where there was an old [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] devotee, in which the following points deserve to be noted, viz. offerings of the heads of animals, lion as vāhana, the slaughter of Mahiṣāsura, the doctrines of Pāśupatas written down on small books of palm leaves containing jugglery, tantra and mantras, Durgā-stotra written on a piece of cloth, ruined temples of the [[Mātrs|Mātṛs]] and description of the [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] devotee as knowing thousands of wonderful stories about [[Sriparvata.|Śrīparvata.]] [[Bāpa|Bāṇa]] describes at length what queen [[Vilāsavati,|Vilāsavatī,]] pining for a son, began doing to placate all Gods viz. sleeping in the shrines of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] where guggulu was being incessantly burnt, taking auspicious baths on nights of dark 14th in public squares where magic circles had been drawn by great magicians, visiting temples of [[Mātra,|Mātṛs,]] wearing amulets inside which were pieces of birch leaves on which mantras had been written with yellow pigment, and, when delivery was [[dear|near]], her bed was rendered holy with various herbs, roots and [[yantrag|yantras]] (figures or diagrams). In the [[Harsacarita|Harṣacarita]] (III) there is a reference to magic circles and to human sacrifices in the description of the [[Saiya|Śaiva]] ascetic [[Bhairavā cārya|Bhairavācārya]] who had all the [[Śaiva-samhitas|Śaiva-saṃhitās]] by heart, who performed the japa of a mahāmantra called [[Mahākālahṭdaya &|Mahākālahṛdaya a]] crore of times in a cemetery and wanted the help of [[Puṣpabhāti|Puṣpabhūti]] (an [[ancostor|ancestor]] of Emperor [[Harsa )|Harṣa)]] for perfection in that mantra to be achieved by subduing a [[vetala|vetāla]] and who ultimately attained to the position of [[Vidyadhara|Vidyādhara]] and rose into the starry firmament. In the last [[Introductory verge|Introductory verse]] of the Harṣacarita the Emperor [[Harga|Harṣa]] is, by means of puns, called the Śrīparvata in yielding all Siddhis (supernatural powers, or fulfilment) according to the wishes of all [[supplianta.|suppliants.]]28 [[Those|These]] descriptions from the works of Bāṇa (first half of 7th century) show [[bow|how]] even long before the 7th century A. D. the worship of [[Candi|Caṇḍī]], with flesh and the [[Sakta|Śākta]] or Tāntrik paraphernalia of mantras, siddhis, [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] and yantras had gripped the minds of all Indian people, great

[[P1048]]and small, rich and poor. In the [[Mālatimadhava (Aot|Mālatīmādhava (Act]] V) we have a gruesome picture of human sacrifice to [[Camunda.|Cāmuṇḍā.]] In the same drama [[Saudamini|Saudāminī]] is described as observing the vows of a Kāpālika on Śrīparvata and as having secured supernatural powers by means of mantras. [[Sriparvata|Śrīparvata]] is mentioned as a holy place of Śiva and [[Dovi|Devī]] in Vanaparva 85. [[19.-20.|19–20.]] The [[Vāsavam datta|Vāsavadattā]] of Subandhu (p. 87 of Hall’s ed.) speaks of [[Sriparvata|Śrīparvata]] as ‘Sannihita-mallikārjunaḥ.’ Later on a few passages from Sanskrit and Prakrit literature will be cited to show how the teaching of Tāntrik practices led to [[grost|great]] moral debasement and revolting orgies in the name of religion.

The literature on Tantras was vast (vide ‘Principles of Tantra’ ed. by A. Avalon, part 1 pp. [[390-392|390–392]] for a long list of Tantras). Both Hindu and Buddhist writers composed numerous works on Tantra and a very large number of subjects came to be included in Tantra works. Buddhist and Hindu Tantras are alike in some respects but they differ in the topics discussed, philosophical doctrines and some religious principles and practices. Tāntra works were introduced into Tibet, Mongolia, China, Japan and South East Asia. Originals of many of the Sanskrit Tāntrik works are now not available, but the [[trang lations|translations]] of some of them in Tibetan are available.29 It is [[aid|said]] that even now if proper search is made, three hundred works on Tantra may be discovered (vide Dr. B. Bhattacharya in vol. X. of Śrī [[Rāmavarma|Rāmavarmā]] Institute of Research at Cochin p. 81 ).

It is difficult to give a general definition of Tantras. The word ‘Tantra’ is derived usually from ‘Tan’ to spread and [[’trai’ (to saye).|‘trai’ (to save).]] ‘It spreads (dilates upon) [[Dacy|many]] matters including the tattvas and mantras and affords protection;

[[P1049]]therefore it is called Tantra ‘.30 They can only be [[desoribed|described]] but one matter is included in almost all of them, [[vig,|viz.]] the five ‘Makāras’. Often they contain an amalgam of religion, philosophy, superstitious dogmas, rites, astronomy, astrology, medicine, prognostications. They [[resomble|resemble]] the Purāṇas in several respects. The Buddhists deified many important [[per sonages|personages]] of Buddhism and took over in course of time certain Hindu deities like Gaṇeśa and [[Sarasvati.|Sarasvatī.]] Tantras are classified by comparatively later works into three groups, Viṣṇukrānta, Rathakrānta and Aśvakrānta and 64 Tantras are assigned to each of the three groups (vide Tāntrik Texts, vol. I. ed. by Arthur Avalon, Introduction pp. II–IV); but these numbers seem to be fictitious. The same Tantra is put in two classes by certain works. The Kulārṇava-tantra (III. 6–7) speaks of five [[ūmnāyas|āmnāyas]] (East, West, South, North and ūrdhva) as the paths to [[Moksa.|Mokṣa.]] The Paraśurāma-kalpasūtra31 I. [[%|2]] does the same. Besides, Tāntrik worshippers are divided into three classes viz. Śaiva, Śākta and Vaiṣṇava. Bagchi states (‘Studies in [[Tantras’p.|Tantras’ p.]] 3) that Tāntrik literature is classified into Srotas (which are three) [[Pitha|Pīṭha]] and [[Amnāya.|Āmnāya.]] The Saundaryalaharī, ascribed by some to the great Advaita teacher Śaṅkarācārya, refers to sixty-four Tantras (in verse 31 which begins [[‘catuṣṣaṣtyā tantraih’|‘catuṣṣaṣtyā tantraiḥ’]] which, it is said therein, were declared by God [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] for deluding32 the world. Several Hindu and Buddhist Tantras

[[P1050]]have been published and we have now a fair idea of what the numerous Tantras must have been like. Some of the published Hindu Tantras are Kulārṇava, Tantrasāra, Nityotsava, [[Parasuramakalpa-sūtra,|Paraśurāmakalpa-sūtra,]] Pārānandasūtra, [[Prapancasāra,|Prapañcasāra,]] [[Mantra mahodadhi|Mantramahodadhi]] of [[Mahidhara,|Mahīdhara,]] Mahānirvāṇatantra, Rudrayāmala, Vāmakeśvaratantra, Śāradātilaka (about 11th century A. D.). Besides, there are works like the Tantrāloka and [[Malinlvijaya vārtika|Mālinīvijayavārtika]] of [[Abhinava-gupta|Abhinavagupta]] of Kashmir [[Tantrism.|Tāntrism.]] They stand somewhat apart from the works enumerated above. Among the published Buddhist tantras [[are;|are:]] Advayavajrasaṅgraha, [[Aryaman juśrimūlakalpa,|Āryamañjuśrīmūlakalpa,]] [[Guhayasamāja-tantra|Guhyasamāja-tantra]] (probably 6th century A. D.), Jñānasiddhi of Indrabhūti (717 A. D.), [[Nispan. nayogāvali|Niṣpannayogāvalī]] of Abhayākaragupta (composed between the last quarter of 11th and first of 12th century A. D.), [[Prajñopāya viniscaya-siddhi|Prajñopāyaviniścaya-siddhi]] of [[Anangavajra ( about|Anaṅgavajra (about]] 705 A. D.), [[Ṣat-cakra nirūpaña|Ṣaṭ-cakranirūpaṇa]] (1577 A.D.), [[Sadhanamala|Sādhanamālā]] (containing 312 small works supposed to be from 3rd to 12th century A. D.). Of the Buddhist Tantras [[Aryamañjusrimūlakalpa|Āryamañjuśrīmūlakalpa]] and Guhyasamāja tantra33 are the oldest according to Dr. B. Bhattacharya (Intro. p. [[XXXVIII,|XXXVIII.]] to Guhya-samājatantra). Most of the above works have been published by Arthur Avalon ([[Sir.|Sir]] John Woodroffe) and in the Gaikwad Oriental Series. Some of the Hindu Tantras contain sublime philosophical views derived from the Upaniṣads and the Gītā or from the Sāṅkhya and Yoga and the final goal according to them is [[Mukli|Mukti]] (liberation from the

[[P1051]]cycle of births and deaths) for all [[mon|men]] but to be secured by following the path laid down by the Tantras. As the number of published Hindu Tantras is considerable, reference will be made mainly to a few viz. Kulārṇava, Pārānandasūtra, [[Prapancasāra,|Prapañcasāra,]] [[Māhānirvāṇatantra,|Mahānirvāṇatantra,]] [[Vāmakeśyaratantra|Vāmakeśvaratantra]] ([[Anan.|Ānan.]] ed.), [[Sakti. sangamatantra,|Śaktisaṅgamatantra,]] [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] and in the case of the Buddhist tantras to [[Aryamañjuśrīmūla-kalpa,|Āryamañjuśrīmūla-kalpa,]] [[Guhyasa mājatantra,|Guhyasamājatantra,]] [[Prajõopāyaviniscayasiddhi,|Prajñopāyaviniścayasiddhi,]] [[Jhānasiddhi,|Jñānasiddhi,]] [[Sadhanamāla,|Sādhanamālā,]] [[Sekodde satikā.|Sekoddeśaṭīkā.]] The purpose of most Buddhist Tantras is to indicate a short path for attaining [[Buddha-hood|Buddhahood]] through Yoga practices and they introduce the element of [[Sakti|Śakti]] for [[Yogio|Yogic]] practices and for securing miraculous powers (called siddhis). In the History of Dharmaśāstra not much need be said about Buddhist Tantras except for comparison and stress will have to be laid on the Hindu Tantras alone. The philosophical aspects of Tāntrik culture may be studied in the Paraśurāmakalpasūtra, the [[Vāmakesvara-tantra,|Vāmakeśvara-tantra,]] Tantrarāja, the works on Kashmir [[Saivism,|Śaivism,]] works of [[Bhaskararāya,|Bhāskararāya,]] Bhāvanopaniṣad. This last is a late work dignified with the title of Upaniṣad, as it deals with bhāvanā and summarises the [[Vāsanāpatala|Vāsanāpaṭala]] of Tantrarājatantra (vide [[Intro,|Intro.]] to the latter p. 3). There are also Vaiṣṇava [[Tantras|Tāntras]] like the [[Gautamiya-tantra|Gautamīya-tantra]] (D. C. ms. No. 1120 of 1886-1892) and [[Kramadipikā|Kramadīpikā]] of [[Kesava|Keśava]] (who was a successor of Nimbārka) with the commentary of Govinda Vidyāvinoda (published in the Chowkhamba S. series), which are not referred to in this work from considerations of space. Vide Agnipurāṇa 39. 1-7 for the names of twenty-five [[Vaiṣnava|Vaiṣṇava]] Tantras dealing with the establishment of Viṣṇu image and other matters and [[Māheśvara tantra|Māheśvaratantra]] 26. 16-20.

The Hindu tantras which are supposed to have embodied [[dia logues|dialogues]] between Śiva and [[Devi|Devī]] or Skanda or Bhairava and rarely others as in [[Dattatreyatantra|Dattātreyatantra]] (D. C. ms. No. [[96%|962]] of 1887-91) endeavour to show that they [[bane|base]] themselves on the Vedas, Āgamas, Smṛtis and Purāṇas, that there is an easier and quicker way to the final goal of [[moksa|mokṣa]] and they often quote Vedic [[Verses.|verses.]] For example, in the [[Kulārnava,|Kulārṇava,]] Śiva says to [[Devi|Devī]] ‘I churned the great ocean of Vedas and [[Againas|Āgamas]] with the churning handle of (correct) knowledge. I [[know|knew]] the essence of these and took out the Kuladharma,34 that the Kaulaśāstras are [[autho.|autho-]]

[[P1052]]ritative like Vedic texts and should not be nullified by [[ratiooina tion.|ratiocination.]] The same Tantra further asserts ‘one who has studied the four Vedas but is ignorant of Kuladharma is inferior to [[& candāla,|a cāṇḍāla,]] while a [[capdala|cāṇḍāla]] who knows Kuladharmas is superior to a brāhmaṇa. If all dharmas such as sacrifices, pilgrimages and vratas are put on one side and Kuladharma on another side, Kaula (dharma) is superior.’35 It is, therefore necessary to understand what is meant by Kula or Kauladharma. The [[Guhya-samaja 169t|Guhya-samāja]]36 states that Guhya means the three viz. body, speech and mind and ‘samaja’ means ‘coming together’, that Kula may comprehend five matters or three or 101 and that Guhya (as defined) is trikula.’ God [[Śhaṅkara 16s deolared|Śaṅkara]]37 declared five tattvas, viz. wine, flesh, fish, mudra (hand and finger poses or the woman helper of a yogin) and sexual intercourse, that are [[aots|acts]] that become the means for the attainment of the position of a vira and that the mantra of Sakti does not confer perfection unless one follows the practices of Kula; therefore a person

[[P1053]]should be devoted to the Kula practices whereby he would attain to the [[sadhana|sādhanā]] of [[Sakti;|Śakti;]] wine, flesh, fish, mudra and sexual intercourse—these are declared to be the five tattvas in the procedure of the worship of [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] In another place the [[Mahanirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]]38 says that the individual soul, [[prakrti,|prakṛti,]] space, time, [[alkasa,|ākāśa,]] earth, water, fire and [[Vayu-these|Vāyu—these]] are called ‘Kula’ and that way of life whereby one looks upon all these as brahma without distinction is called Kulācāra, that confers the four goals viz. dharma, artha, kāma and mokṣa. The [[Śaktisangama tantra|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra]] states that Kula means the upāsakas ([[worshippers )|worshippers)]]39 of [[Kāli.|Kālī.]] The Kulārṇava states [[*Kula|‘Kula]] means gotra and that springs from [[Sakti|Śakti]] and Śiva; that man is called Kaulika who knows that [[mokea|mokṣa]] is secured from that (i. e. [[Sakti|Śakti]] and Śiva). Śiva is called ‘Akula’ and [[Sakti|Śakti]] is called ‘Kula’; those who contemplate on Kula and Akula are the wise kaulikas.” Various other definitions are given in Guhyasamāja ([[Ist|1st]] [[patala|paṭala]] p. 6), in the Preface p. VIII of the [[Saktisangama-tantra, Tarakhanda|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra, Tārākhaṇḍa.]] But the same Tantra clinches the matter by declaring that “Śakti is known as Kula; her worship and the like are described ; that should be known as ‘Kulācāra’, which is difficult of attain- ment even for gods. Worship, done with these alone viz. wine, flesh, fish, mudrā and sexual intercourse, is known as Kulācāra.” The Pārānanda-sūtra40 provides that the highest self is one,

[[P1054]]that there are seven Lords [[(Isvara )|(Īśvara)]] viz. [[Brahma,|Brahmā,]] Viṣṇu, Śiva, Sūrya, [[Ganesa,|Gaṇeśa,]] Śakti and Bhairava, that individual souls are countless, that there are three mārgas [[(patha)|(paths)]] viz. Dakṣiṇa, [[Vama and Uttard,|Vāma and Uttara,]] each succeeding one being superior to each preceding one, that [[Dakṣiṇa-marga|Dakṣiṇa-mārga]] is the one declared in the Veda, [[smrtis|smṛtis]] and purāṇas, that the Vāma (way) is declared by the Veda and [[Agamas,|Āgamas,]] while the third (Uttara) is the one declared by the words of the Veda and of the Guru and that the Guruvākya is that of one’s guru who is himself [[Jiran-mukta|Jīvan-mukta]] and who gives instruction as to [[&|a]] mantra. That sūtra further provides that the Vāmācāra is of two kinds, madhyama (middling) and [[utlama|uttama]] (best), that [[uttana|uttama]] is the one which is concerned with wine, sexual intercourse and hand poses, while madhyama is one where all five, wine, flesh, fish, [[mudra|mudrā]] and maithuna are resorted to. It should be noticed that the Tantras themselves designate the use of five makāras in worship as Vāmācāra and not their orthodox partisans of Yoga, as Heinrich Zimmer alleges in ‘The art of Indian Asia’ vol. I. p 130. Pārānandasūtra41 prescribes that the disciple has to undergo [[dikṣā|dīkṣā]] (consecration) from a qualified guru, who instructs the disciple as to the mantra, who holds a mouthful of water in his own mouth and passes it into the mouth of the disciple that accepts the mantra while he [[gulpa|gulps]] down the water. This procedure applies if the guru is a brāhmaṇa, but if the guru is a [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] he should recite the mantra in the right ear of the disciple. The [[Tantraraja-tantra|Tantrarāja-tantra]] provides that the guru should wait for 1, 2, 3, 4 or 5 years according as the intending disciple belongs to the four varṇas or to a mixed caste, should test [[bis|his]] qualities and devotion and then [[com municate|communicate]] to him the mantra; otherwise, both guru and disciple would come to grief ([[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] Texts, [[vol VIII, II, 37-38).|vol. VIII, II. 37-38).]] Most works on Tantra provide that the knowledge conveyed by the guru and worship with five makāras must be kept secret and if made public falling in hell is the result. Vide [[Parasurama kalpasūtra 1 12|Paraśurāma-kalpasūtra I. 12]] and [[Saktigangama-tantra.|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra.]]42 After undergoing [[dikṣā|dīkṣā]] and receiving the mantra the disciple has to follow the orders

[[P1055]]of the guru till the former has [[&|a]] vision of the deity.43 Guru is higher than all other men, mantra is higher than guru, the deity is higher than the mantra and the highest self is higher than the devatā. In order to attain siddhis the guru is to be served by disciples with devotion in all ways. There is only one way viz. bhakti (devotion) for those (disciples) that long for worldly pleasures, heaven or mokṣa, as the Śruti says ’there is no other way’. [[Jivan-mukti|Jīvan-mukti]]44 [[means to have a vision of the Deity worshipped’ and one who is liberated, though living, is not tainted by his acts, whether meritorious or otherwise’, This|means ’to have a vision of the Deity worshipped’ and ‘one who is liberated, though living, is not tainted by his acts, whether meritorious or otherwise’. This]] doctrine closely follows what is said in some of the Upaniṣads about the man who has realised brahma [[that he shakes|that ‘he shakes]] off [[puṇya and papa|puṇya and pāpa]] and having cast aside the body he reaches the world of brahma; he does not return [[i.e.|i. e.]] he never again undergoes [[samsāra’.|saṁsāra’.]] One should strive for that stage. Therefore one who has obtained correct knowledge should become a bhakta (devotee). All these, [[viz.one in distress, the seekor|viz. one in distress, the seeker]] after knowledge, one who seeks some desired object and one who has obtained correct knowledge, are noble, but the man, who has knowledge about God, when he becomes a devotee, reaches the world of the Highest Self, as the Vedic words say ‘one who knows brahma reaches the Highest’. With all this sublime philosophy as the background the Pārānandasūtra frankly provides that the guru, after completing the worship up to the offering of a handful of flowers and having offered into [[firo somo|fire some]] food, should make a collection of makāras, should again come to the place for worshipping the Deity and offer food into fire, should hand over to the neophyte a bowl for drinking wine,

He who giving up the ordinances of śāstra acts as he pleases does not attain siddhi in this world, [[por|nor]] heaven nor the Highest goal [[( mokṣa).|(mokṣa).]] A worshipper should drink wine only up till his eyes do not begin to roll and up till [[bis|his]] mind does not become unsteady; to drink beyond that stage is bestial’. The [[Paranandasātra|Pārānandasūtra]] (pp. 70-71) describes the procedure of a festival (Utsavavidhi) among [[Tantrikas.|Tāntrikas.]] The mantra is [[‘Isvarātman, tava dasoham’|‘Īśvarātman, tava dāsoham’]], which may be given to even a cāṇḍāla or may be accepted from a cāṇḍāla. They [[aro:|are:]] ‘I take this holy nectar, which is a [[medioine ( antidote)|medicine (antidote)]] for [[samsāra,|saṁsāra,]] which is a [[meang|means]] of cutting off the [[sparos|snares]] by [[whioh|which]] the paśu (in man) is bound and which is [[deolared|declared]] by Bhairava’ (this when taking the first [[i. o.|i. e.]] wine); ‘I take this mudrā which is [[‘ucohista’|‘ucchiṣṭa’]] of the Lord (i. e. which has been first offered to God), which destroys the torments of the heart, which produces joy and which47 is enriched with other food materials’ (this when taking mudrā); [[“I|‘I]] take this divine young woman who has drunk wine, that always makes the heart full of bliss and that brings about my [[sādhana’|sādhanā’]] (when taking one of the women that are brought [[together ).|together).]]

The Hindu Tantra works present two sides, one philosophical and spiritual, the other popular, practical and more or less [[magioal,|magical,]] which relies on mantras, mudrās, [[mandalas,|maṇḍalas,]] nyāsas, cakras and yantras as physical means to realize one’s identity with the Supreme Power or Energy by concentration and as conferring extraordinary powers on the devotee. This may be illustrated by reference to two typical tantras, the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] and the [[Mahanirvāṇa-tantra.|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra.]] The [[Mahānirvana-tantra,|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra,]] though it speaks of the five makāras as means of upāsanā and though it states that when the great Tantra is understood, the Vedas, [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] and śāstras are hardly48 of any use, [[puta|puts]] forward the striking conception in IV. 34-47 that Parameśvara is one and to be described as sat, cit and [[ananda,|ānanda,]] that He is one without [[&|a]] second, is beyond the [[gunas|guṇas]] and is to be known from the [[Vedanta|Vedānta]] texts. It further on says that the best mantra is [[‘om sac gidekam brahma’(III. 14),|‘om sac-cidekam brahma’ (III. 14),]] that those who perform the upāsanā of the Highest Brahma do not require other means of worship [[(sādbanā);|(sādhanā);]] by [[stioking|sticking]] to this mantra man becomes brahma. In the 4th chap. however, the [[Mahāpariniryāṇa|Mahāparinirvāṇa]] starts by saying that Durgā is the highest [[praksti|prakṛti]] of Paramātmā, she has various names such as [[Kāli,|Kālī,]] [[Bhuvanośvari,|Bhuvaneśvarī,]] Bagalā, [[Bhairavi,|Bhairavī,]] [[Ohhin namastakā,|Chinnamastakā,]] that she is [[Sarasvati,|Sarasvatī,]] [[Lakṣmi|Lakṣmī]] and [[Sakti,|Śakti,]] that she [[aggumes|assumes]] various forms for securing the purpose of her devotees and for the destruction of [[demong.|demons.]] In the Kaliyuga perfection cannot be attained without following kula practices, which lead

[[P1058]]to the knowledge of brahma and the man who possesses [[know ledge|knowledge]] of brahma is a [[liborated|liberated]] soul, though living (he is [[Jivanmukla).|Jīvanmukta).]] Then there is high praise (IV. 10 ff) of [[Dev!|Devī]] who is spoken of as the primordial [[Sakti (adyā paramā sakti )|Śakti (ādyā paramā śakti)]] and all gods including Śiva himself derive their powers from this Highest [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] A rather astounding statement is made in49 the words ‘as there was partaking of wine and the rest in Satya, Tretā and [[Dyāpara|Dvāpara]] yugas, one should do the same in Kali-yuga, but in accordance with the kula way, and that Kali does not affect those who give to the truthful Yogin the five tattvas (wine &c.) sanctified according to the kula way’. Then [[&|a]] mantra of ten syllables is declared ‘[[hrim srim krim parama. svari 170$|hrīm śrīm krīm parameśvari]]50 [[syābā’|svāhā’]], by merely listening to which a man becomes [[jivanmukta.|jīvanmukta.]] Then by various combinations of the [[mystio|mystic]] syllables with [[Parameśvari|Parameśvarī]] and Kālikā twelve mantras are produced (V. 18). But the mantras do not confer siddhi unless the kulācāra way is followed viz. the five tattvas [[madya’&o.|‘madya’ &c.]] [[ara|are]] offered (V. 22-23). Then a [[Gayatri Mantra|Gāyatrī mantra]] is set out (V. 62-63) as [[adyayai vidmabe Paramośvaryai dhimabii tan-nah Kali pracodayat il’,|‘adyāyai vidmahe Parameśvaryai dhīmahi tan-naḥ Kālī pracodayāt॥’,]] which is to be muttered thrice daily. The [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] tattvas, [[Prakrti,|Prakṛti,]] Mahat, [[Abankara|Ahaṅkāra]] &c. are welded on to the worship of Śakti and the Vedic mantra [[Hainsaḥ śuoiṣad’|‘Haṁsaḥ śuciṣad’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IV. 40. 5) with the Tāntrik [[bija Hrim|bīja Hrīm]] (V. 197). The Tantra gives directions for the sanctification of flesh (V. 203-208, where [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 22. 20 [[’tadvisnoḥ paramam padam’|‘tadviṣṇoḥ paramam padam’]] is employed), of fish (V. 209-210) where the mantra [[‘Tryambakam’ (Rg. VIII,|‘Tryambakam’ (Ṛg. VII.]] 59. 12) is employed, of mudrā (V. 211-212 where the mantras [[‘Tad Viṣpo” paramam’|‘Tad Viṣṇoḥ paramam’]] and ’tad-viprāso’ [[Rg. I. 22.20 21,|Ṛg. I. 22. 20-21,]] are employed) to be offered to [[Devi.|Devī.]] The [[Mabanirvāṇatantra|Mahānirvāṇatantra]]

[[P1059]](18th century) being composed after [[sāktism|Śāktism]] had come into great [[ridioule|ridicule]] and obloquy is rather sober,51 It says that women of good birth should simply smell wine and not drink it, while householder [[sūdhalas|sādhakas]] should drink only as much as is contained in five cups, since by drinking too much [[mon|men]] of good family incur the loss of siddhi, and should drink only so long as the wine quaffed does not make their eyes roll or [[doos|does]] not make their mind confused. As to the last tattva (maithuna) the sādhaka was to confine himself to the woman he chooses as his Śakti (VI. 14) and if his wife is alive he is not to touch another woman with a vicious intent, otherwise he would go to hell.52 In keeping with its desire to present respectability along with [[tantrik practicos,|tāntrik practices,]] the Mahānirvāṇa devotes chapter VIII. to the duties of varṇas and āśramas, the duties of the king, the duties of servants in general, provides53 that persons of all varṇas are to marry within the [[varpa|varṇa]] and dine with [[persong|persons]] of the [[game|same]] varṇa, except when engaged in [[Bhairavi|Bhairavī]] cakra and Tattva-cakra (VIII. 150), when men of all varṇas are like the best brāhmaṇas, and no consideration of the castes of the participants arises nor any question about ucchiṣṭa. It prescribes that no one has the adhikāra to engage in the performance of Tattvacakra unless he is [[&|a]] sādhaka endowed with knowledge of brahma. In that cakra the tattvas (wine and the others) should be collected and placed in front of the [[Devi,|Devī,]] the mantra [[‘Hamgaḥ’ (Rg. IV, 40.5)|‘Haṁsaḥ’ (Ṛg. IV. 40. 5)]] should be recited over all tattvas and the tattvas should be offered to the Highest Self with the [[verge.|verse]] [[‘Brahmārpanam brahma havir’ (Bhagvadgitā|‘Brahmārpaṇam brahma havir’ (Bhagvadgītā]] IV. 24 = Mahānirvāṇa VIII. 214)

[[P1060]]and all the [[sadhakas|sādhakas]] should engage in drinking and eating.54 Chap. IX enumerates and describes ten [[sainskāras|saṁskāras]] from [[garbhadhāpa|garbhadhāna]] to marriage for [[membors|members]] of the three varṇas and nine for [[sūdras|śūdras]] (omitting [[upanayanı|upanayana]]), wherein [[Vodic mantrag|Vedic mantras]] are prescribed as in the Dharmasūtras and [[smrtis.|smṛtis.]] [[One interest ing|One interesting]] item is what is called [[Saira|Śaiva]] marriage, which is of two kinds, one entered into according to the rules of cakra and the other lasting for life. It is further provided that in [[Śaiva marriage1713|Śaiva marriage]]55 no question of [[rarna|varṇa]] or age arises and that if a man has children from a wife married in the [[brūhma|brāhma]] form and also [[ohildren|children]] of a Śaiva marriage, it is only the former that take as heirs and the latter are entitled only to food and raiment ([[IX 261-264|IX. 261-264]]). Chapters X, XI, XII of the [[Mabānirvāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] deal respectively with [[śrūddhas|śrāddhas]], [[prāyaścitlas|prāyaścittas]] for [[sids|sins]] and [[ryjarahāra.|vyavahāra.]]

It is necessary now to turn to the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] which belongs to about the 11th century A. D. That work is divided into 25 [[patalas|paṭalas]] and contains over 4500 [[verse.|verses.]] It presents in the [[begin. ning|beginning]] a somewhat abstruse and involved philosophy. It says that Śiva is both [[nirguna|nirguṇa]] and [[saquna,|saguṇa,]] the former being different from [[Prakrti,|Prakṛti,]] the latter being [[8ssociated|associated]] with [[Praksti.|Prakṛti.]] Then it describes the order of evolution and manifestation as [[follows:|follows :—]] From the [[Saguna|Saguṇa]] Parameśvara described as [[“Sac-cidananda vibhava’|“Sac-cidānanda-vibhava”]] [[Sakti|Śakti]]56 proceeds; from the latter arises [[nāda (para )|nāda (para)]] and from nāda arises bindu (para) which is divided into three viz. bindu (apara), [[nāda (apara )|nāda (apara)]] and [[bija;|bīja;]] the first is [[indentified|identified]] with Śiva, [[bija being Sakti and rāda|bīja being Śakti and nāda]] being the coming together of the two (Śiva and [[Sakti).|Śakti).]] [[Sakti|Śakti]] creates the worlds, she is [[sabda-bhahma|śabda-brahma]] ([[I. 56 )|I. 56)]] and is called [[parāsakti|parāśakti]] (I. 52) and [[para deyatā|paradevatā]] (I. 57). She flashes like lightning in the [[adhāra-oakra.|ādhāra-cakra.]]57

[[P1061]][[Sakti|Śakti]] assumes the form of [[Kuṇdalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] in the human body. From [[Sambhu|Śambhu]] in the form of bindu arise in order [[Sadasiva, Isa. Rudra,|Sadāśiva, Īśa, Rudra,]] Viṣṇu, Brahmā; from the avyakta bindu arise in order the mahat-tattva, [[abankāra|ahaṅkāra]] and the other tattvas mentioned in the [[Sāökbya|Sāṅkhya]] system. [[Sakti|Śakti]] is all-pervading and yet more subtle than the subtlest, she is the [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] coiled like a serpent and manifests herself in the form of the fifty letters of the Sanskrit alphabet (from ‘a’ to ‘kṣa’).

Before proceeding further some explanation of the six cakras which form an important constituent subject of several tantras is needed. There are said to be six cakras (centres) in the human body viz. [[Adhāra|Ādhāra]] or [[Mūlādhāra ( at|Mūlādhāra (at]] the base of the spine), [[Svadhisthāna ( near|Svādhiṣṭhāna (near]] the generative organ), [[Manipūra ( near|Maṇipūra (near]] the navel), [[Anahata (near the beart),|Anāhata (near the heart),]] [[Visuddha ( near throat),|Viśuddha (near throat),]] and [[Ajõā|Ājñā]] (between the brows). Besides these, there is [[Brahma randhra|Brahmarandhra]] figured as the pericarp of the thousand-petalled lotus

[[P1062]]within the crown of the head. The [[oakres|cakras]] are often [[indentified|identified]] with the nerve plexuses of modern physiology, but the [[descrip tions|descriptions]] in Sanskrit works of the [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] and the cakras are meant to refer not to the gross body but rather to what is situated in the subtle body that vanishes when a man dies. The idea of the relevant Sanskrit texts is that the [[Kundalini Sakti (‘Kundalin’|Kuṇḍalinī Śakti (‘Kuṇḍalin’]] [[means & serpent)|means a serpent)]] is asleep in the [[Mūlādhara-oakra|Mūlādhāra-cakra]] coiled like a serpent and has to be roused by the practices of Yoga and deep meditation.58] The [[Sāradātilaka anks|Śāradātilaka asks]] in an eloquent stanza the [[sādbaka|sādhaka]] to meditate upon the [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] which when roused passes from the Mūlādhāra-cakra by means of the [[Susumnā-nadi|Suṣumnā-nāḍī]] (which is in the [[contre|centre]] of the spinal column) through all the six cakras, unites with Śiva in the Sahasrāra (thousand-petalled) cakra and then returns to [[Mlūlādbāra.|Mūlādhāra.]] Each of the six cakras is said to have a certain number of petals [[viz, 4,|viz. 4,]] 6, 10, 12, 16, 2 (50 in all) in order from [[Mūlādbāra|Mūlādhāra]] to [[Ajñā|Ājñā]] (vide Rudrayāmala, 17th [[Patala,|Paṭala,]] verses 55-56). The letters of the alphabet also are 50 [[(from ‘a’ to * kṣa’)|(from ‘a’ to ‘kṣa’)]] and they are assigned in groups to the six cakras as follows : ha and kṣa to [[Ajña,|Ājñā,]] 16 vowels to [[Visuddha|Viśuddha]] in the throat, letters ka to [[tha|ṭha]] (12 in all) to [[Anahata,|Anāhata,]] letters [[Da to Pha (10) to Mapipūra,|ḍa to pha (10) to Maṇipūra,]] ba to la (6 in all) to [[Svādbiṣthāna,|Svādhiṣṭhāna,]] letters va to sa (4) to Mūlādhāra. Some [[tantrag|tantras]] state the colour of each of the six cakras and identify them with the five elements and the mind. These speculations of the Yoga and Tantras are
