Vide षट्चक्रनिरूपण verse 53 also, for the stream of nectar flowing over कुण्डलिनी in सहस्रारपद्म. Vide मन्त्रमहोदधि IV. 19-25, ज्ञानार्णवतन्त्र (24. 45-54), महानिर्वाणतन्त्र V. 113-115, for the number of petals in cakras, their colours, the letters assigned to each and their identification with the five elements and mind and सौन्दर्यलहरी verse 9 ‘महीं मूलाधारे…सहस्रारे पद्मे सह रहसि पत्या विहरसे ॥’ for identifying the five elements and the mind with the six cakras. In ‘Sarasvatībhavana Studies’ Vol. II, pp. 83-92 Pandit Gopināth Kavirāja describes the system of cakras according to Gorakṣanātha. The रुद्रयामल (36. 6-168) sets out 1008 names of कुण्डलिनी, all of which begin with the letter क.]]

[[P1063]]developments of the ancient Upaniṣad theories briefly noticed [[below,|below.]]59]

Letters form words and words form mantras, that are the power incarnate of [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] Then the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] describes [[āgana, maṇḍapa, kuṇda, mandala, pithas|āsana, maṇḍapa, kuṇḍa, maṇḍala, pīṭhas]] (on which images of gods are to be placed), [[diksa|dīkṣā]] (initiation), [[prāṇapratiṣthā|prāṇapratiṣṭhā]] (vivifying images), production of sacrificial fire. The [[sāradā tilaka|Śāradātilaka]] (I. 109 and V. 81-91), the Varivasyārahasya ([[II..80),|II. 80),]] the Paraśurāmakalpasūtra (I. 4, [[‘Ṣat-trimsat tattyāni visvam’)|‘Ṣaṭ-triṁśat tattvāni viśvam’)]] and other Tāntrik and Āgamic works enumerate 36 [[tattyag|tattvas]] (including those of the [[sānkhya|sāṅkhya]] system). From [[chap. VII. to XXIII|chap. VII to XXIII]] mantras of different deities, their formation, use and [[resulta,|results,]] abhiṣekas and mudrās are described. Yantras are dealt with in chap. XXIV and Yoga in chap. XXV. It must be said to the credit of the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] that it treats of only mantras and mudrās and hardly anywhere treats of the other makāras. The [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] has been profusely quoted as an authoritative Tantra by medieval Dharmaśāstra writers like [[Govindananda,|Govindānanda,]] Raghunandana, [[Kamalakara, Nilakantha, Mitramiśra and orthers.|Kamalākara, Nīlakaṇṭha, Mitramiśra and others.]] In J. G. J. R. I. vol. III. pp. 97-108 M. M. [[Gopinath|Gopīnāth]] Kavirāja contributes a learned paper on nāda, bindu and kalā and takes great pains to elucidate these and hopes that his exposition will make the meaning of these words clear (p. 103 ). But the author feels grave doubts whether most readers will find the meanings clear.

Many tantras speak of the five [[makāres|makāras]] as the means of worshipping [[Devi,|Devī,]] as enabling a man to possess miraculous

[[P1064]]powers and as leading to final liberation. The [[Kularnava stater * the great|Kulārṇava states ’the great]] Bhairava has [[proscribed|prescribed]] that in the Kaula system [[Biddhi|Siddhi]] (perfection) results from those very substances by (resorting to) which (ordinarily) men incur sin’. That means that the Kaula system eradicates poison by poison or, to employ modern terminology, its principles are like those of Homeopathy.60

The Tantras seem to be not unaware that in prescribing the five makāras as leading to mukti (final liberation) they were playing with fire. The [[Kulārṇave|Kulārṇava]] itself remarks (II. 117-119 and 122) ‘If by merely drinking wine a man were to attain siddhi (miraculous powers, perfection), then all wretched drunkards may attain siddhi. If, by merely eating meat a holy goal were to be secured then all meat-eaters in the world would be holy men. If by mere intercourse with a woman (called [[sakti)|śakti)]] [[moksa|mokṣa]] was to result, then all men in the world may attain liberation. To follow the path (of Kula) is indeed more unattainable than walking on the edge of a sword, than clinging to a tiger’s neck, than holding a serpent (in one’s hand)’. The Kulārṇava prefaces the preceding dicta by the words ‘Many, who are devoid of traditional knowledge and who profane (the [[sāstra|śāstra]]) by false ideas, imagine that the Kaulika [[dootrine|doctrine]] is this and that, relying on their (poor) intellect’ (II. 116).

The [[Devibhāgavata|Devībhāgavata]] provides (XI. 1. 25) that so much of tantra as is not opposed to the Veda is undoubtedly [[authorita. tive ( vedāvirodhi cot tantram tat pramāṇam na samsayaḥ),|authoritative (vedāvirodhi cet tantram tat pramāṇaṁ na saṁśayaḥ),]] but what is opposed to Veda is not authoritative.

There appears to have been great rivalry between the Hindu and Buddhist tantras. The [[Saktisangama-tantra,|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra,]] one of the most popular and most exhaustive works on tantra, states that [[Dovi|Devī]] manifests herself for the destruction of Bauddha and other heretical sects, for the removal of the confusing admixture of

[[P1065]][[different culty,|different cults,]] for the establishment of the (true) [[oult,|cult,]] for the protection of what the brāhmaṇas stand for and for the perfect attainment of mantra-śāstra.61 The Bauddha tantras, on the other hand, were not slow to retaliate.

It would not be entirely out of place if [[& fow|a few]] words were devoted to the Bauddha Tantras, particularly of the Vajrayāna. It has already been shown above (pp. 943–44 note: 1516) that all Buddhists, whether of the [[hinayāna’|‘hīnayāna’]] or mahāyāna type, were required to observe strict rules and regulations such as those of [[pancasilas|pañcaśīlas]] and of taking refuge with Buddha, Dharma and Sangha and of [[daśasllas|daśaśīlas]] (for monks). The goal of [[nirvana|nirvāṇa]] (particularly under Mahāyāna doctrines) could be reached only after a very long time or after several births. As luxuries viz. flesh, fish, wine and women had been banned, the general mass of the people and probably monks also were tired of the strict mode of life and long waiting for the goal. Buddhist Tantras like the Guhyasamāja (that belongs to the Vajrayāna school) provided an easy process by which liberation and even [[Buddha.. bood|Buddha-hood]] could be secured in a short time and even in62 one life and averred that Bodhisattvas and Buddhas attained the seat of dharma by enjoying all objects of pleasure as they63 pleased. The word Vajra means both ‘diamond’ and ’thunderbolt’. The first meaning appears to have been principally meant in the Guhyasamāja but the 2nd meaning also must have been [[some times|sometimes]] intended. Vajra denotes anything which is hard like a diamond. In the Guhyasamājatantra the word Vajra, either singly or in compounds, [[Occurs|occurs]] hundreds of times. [[Kaya (body), Vak|Kāya (body), Vāk]] (speech) and citta (mind) are called [[’trivajra’ (Gulya,|‘trivajra’ (Guhya,]] pp. 31, 35, 36, 43). Numerous other things64 are called ‘vajra’,

a tala rallat i preto, attrave I. 17-19.|आविर्भवति देवेशि दिव्योद्घारतः शिवे । बौद्धपाषण्डनाशार्थं संप्रदायार्थमेव च । संप्रदायसङ्कराणां विनाशार्थमिहेश्वरि । संप्रदायस्थापनार्थं ब्राह्मण्यरक्षणाय च । मन्त्रशास्त्रस्य सिद्ध्यर्थमाविर्भवति पार्वति । शक्तिसङ्गम०, कालीखण्ड I. 17-19.]]

[[P1066]]such as the [[sunya|śūnya]] (the Absolute of the [[Madhyamaka|Mādhyamaka]] school) and [[ako Vijñans|also Vijñāna]] (consciousness ), which is the [[sele|sole]] reality according to [[tho|the]] [[Yogāoāra|Yogācāra]] school, and the [[Mabāsukha (Bliss )|Mahāsukha (Bliss)]] added by the [[Śaktas.|Śāktas.]] It also means the male organ in the mystic language of [[Saktas.|Śāktas.]] Though the original Buddhist rules insisted on [[ahimga,|ahiṁsā,]] Guhyasamāja permitted several kinds of flesh, such as that of elephants, horses, dogs and even human65 flesh. Early Buddhism insisted upon truthfulness and continence ([[brahma cerya);|brahmacarya);]] vajrayāna, being an innovating revolt, allowed the killing of all animals, speaking untruth, intercourse with women (including even incest with the mother, sister66 and daughter) and [[appropriating:wealth|appropriating wealth]] not donated by any one. This was called vajramārga (the path of vajra), which is said to have been the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of all Buddhas.

The Prajñopāya (I. 20 ) describes the [[stato|state]] reached by the Vajrayāna method as follows. ‘It is neither duality [[por 100, duality,|nor non-duality,]] it is full of peace, beneficent, present everywhere, to be realised by one’s own self, steady (unchanging), undisturbed and full of [[Prajina|Prajñā]] (wisdom) and [[Upaya|Upāya]] (activity with [[compas.|compassion)]]

[[P1067]]sion) and it further provides (V. 22-23) that ‘by those who hanker after liberation perfection of wisdom must be sought (resorted to or adored) in all ways. This perfection of wisdom is everywhere present assuming the form of woman’. [[Prajña|Prajñā]] was linked up with an intense state of emotion called Sukha or Mahāsukha (great bliss). “It being of the nature of endless bliss is designated mahāsukha; it is beneficent all round, most eminent and conduces to complete enlightenment’ (Prajñopāya. I. 27) and ’this all Buddha knowledge which is by its nature to be experienced by one’s inmost self is called Mahāsukha (great bliss) since it is the most eminent of all pleasures’ (Jñānasiddhi VII. 3). The word Prajñā is feminine in [[gendør|gender]] and therefore some Vajrayāna writers identified Prajñā with woman; by [[erotio|erotic]] symbolism and far-fetched analogies the cult of woman was started.

Dr. H. V. Guenther has published a book styled ‘Yuganaddha’ propounding the Tāntrik view of life based only on Buddhist Tantras and endeavours to prove in that work (of about 190 pages) that the Buddhist Tāntrikas try to restore life in its entirety, which is neither an indulgence in passions nor a rejection and escape, but a complete reconciliation to the hard facts of life, that the sexual aspect of the Tantras is but the corrective against the one-sided intellectualism and rationalism of mere philosophy which is unable to cope with the problems of everyday life and that the symbol of Yuganaddha points to the unique harmony and interpenetration of masculinity and [[feminity,|femininity,]] of blunt truth and symbolic truth, of intellect and humanity. It is not possible even to summarize this work or criticize it here. The core of the doctrine of the Vajrayāna tantras is found in the passages quoted in notes 1720-21, [[1723-84.|1723-24.]] The argument is: according to these Tantras perception of wholeness is the most joyous of all human experiences and man’s experience will not be full but only partial if he has [[dio|no]] experience of [[feminity|femininity]] i. e. of everything [[fomale.|female.]] He may [[expertendo feminity|experience femininity]] through all female members of his family. [[Thorefore,|Therefore,]] it is not to be wondered at, says Dr. Guenther, that ’this [[er. perionoe|ex-perience]] so often has an incestuous character’. Then he offers (on pp. [[106-11%)|106-112)]] a lengthy explanation of what he means which, the [[prosont|present]] author has to [[confoss,|confess,]] is not quite clear to his [[moderato|moderate]] mental abilities. Dr. Guenther appears to be steeped in all the latest theories of modern psychologists like those of Freud and tries to expound that Buddhist [[author|authors]] of the 8th century A. D. [[Like Anangavafra and Indrabhu had plumbed the depths at the|like Anaṅgavajra and Indrabhūti had plumbed the depths of the]]

[[P1068]]psychical life on the lines of modern psycho-analysis. Granting for a moment all that Dr. Guenther says about bi-sexuality, about sexual partnership being the best expression for the most intimate relation between two opposites, about woman being for the [[malo|male]] a material object and a goddess, the present writer feels that one question appears to have been not satisfactorily answered or explained, viz. why did not the Buddhist Tāntrikas simply exhort the sādhaka to understand the emotions, [[view points|view-points]] and value of woman as a mother, sister, wife, daughter or as a woman in general and why did they frequently and blatantly harp upon sexual intercourse of even an incestuous kind as a quick method of realizing the goal ?

The [[Guhyagamāja-tantra|Guhyasamāja-tantra]] puts forward a quick and short method for realising Buddhahood and for the attainment of miraculous powers (siddhis) through the processes of Yoga. The siddhis are said to be of two kinds, Sāmānya (ordinary,67 such as becoming invisible) and Uttama (highest i. e. attaining Buddhahood). Four means for securing ordinary siddhis are mentioned and they are called [[Vajra-catuska.|Vajra-catuṣka.]] It is further provided that the best [[ṣiddhi|siddhi]] is acquired by the nectar of knowledge due to the six [[angas|aṅgas]]68 of Yoga (and by no other way) viz. [[Pratyābāra,|Pratyāhāra,]] Dhyāna, Prāṇāyāma, Dhāraṇā, Anusmṛti and [[Samādbi.|Samādhi.]] It is worthy of note that the first three [[angas|aṅgas]] of yoga mentioned in the [[Yogagātra|Yogasūtra]] viz. yama, niyama69 and [[asana|āsana]] are omitted and a new one, Anusmṛti, is added. Yama could not be included because to the Guhyasamāja it did not matter if the sādhaka ate flesh, or indulged in sexual intercourse or told lies, while in Yogasūtra yamas are ahiṁsā, [[salya|satya]] (truthfulness), asteya (not appropriating anything that does not belong to one), brahmacarya (sexual purity) and aparigraha (non-acceptance of gifts). [[Niyames|Niyamas]] could not be [[inoluded|included]] because among the five

[[P1069]]niyamas are svādhyāya (Veda study) and ([[Isvaraprapidhāna )|Īśvarapraṇidhāna)]] devotion to or surrender to God. Many Bauddhas reviled the Veda and acknowledged no Supreme Deity. The Guhyasamāja introduced Yogic practices for quickly securing Buddhahood. The idea of allowing flesh and sexual intercourse seems to have been that the Yogin is to be indifferent as to what he does so long as he is striving for the goal of Buddhahood, for development of his psychic life and that he may disregard all social [[conyon tions|conventions]] and rules.70 Another innovation of the Vajrayāna was the introduction of upāsanā of [[Sakti|Śakti]] for liberation through Yoga. The Guhya-samāja provides that if even after making efforts for six months, the sādhaka has no realisation, [[ho|he]] may repeat his [[offorts|efforts]] for three times more and if even after that he has no enlightenment he should resort to [[Hathayoga|Haṭhayoga]] and then he would attain correct knowledge through Yoga. A further innovation was the theory of the five Dhyāni-buddhas,71 emanating from the Bhagavān, who represent the five Skandhas or fundamental principles of which the whole creation is composed and each of whom was associated with a female [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] The teaching of the Guhyasamāja is that if psychical power and miraculous siddhis are to be developed females must be associated with those who undertake Yogic exercises to achieve their [[enda.|ends.]] Thus the prophecy of the founder of Buddhism pronounced, when yielding to the pressure and entreaties of his favourite disciple [[Ananda|Ānanda]] for allowing women to be members of the sangha and to be nuns, that on account of this innovation his system would [[atand|stand]] fast only for five hundred years, though otherwise it would have lasted for a thousand years, was literally fulfilled (vide Cullavagga

e to and copil challenge the worldly enjoymente|भक्ष्याभक्ष्यविनिर्मुक्तः पेयापेयविवर्जितः । गम्यागम्यविनिर्मुक्तो भवेद्योगी समाहितः ॥ ज्ञानसिद्धि I. 18; गम्यागम्यादिकल्पं नात्र कुर्यात् कदाचन । मायोपमादियोगेन मोक्षार्थं सर्वमेव हि ॥ वज्रोपाय० p. 23 v. 29.]]

[[P1070]]X. 1. 6 in Vinaya Texts, vol. III, S. B. E. XX. p. 325). If we accept 483 [[B. O.|B. C.]] as the date of Buddha’s [[Parinirvana ( as|Parinirvāṇa (as]] many scholars hold) or 477 B. C. (as A. Foucher [[bolds)|holds)]] 500 years therefrom would bring us to the first century A. D. and it is clear that by a century or two after that time much of Buddha’s teaching appears to have been almost completely swamped by doctrines of Mahāyāna and Vajrayāna Tantras. By a strange irony of fate Buddha’s ‘dharma-cakra-pravartana’ came to be transformed into ‘adharma-cakra-pravartana’ by many of his so-called followers of the [[Vajrayana.|Vajrayāna.]] In the [[Mabā-parinibbana autta|Mahā-parinibbāna-sutta]] V. 23 (S. B. E. vol. XI. [[p. 91 )|p. 91)]] the Buddha was very strict and warned bhikkhus not to see [[bhikkhunis,|bhikkhunīs,]] not to talk to them if they could not avoid seeing them, and to be wide awake if [[&|a]] nun talked to them. Buddha severely rebuked one of his disciples for showing his miraculous powers (vide p. 1037 and [[2. 1672|n. 1672]] above), but [[Guhyagamāja|Guhyasamāja]] and other Buddhist tantras make provision for endowing the [[sādbaka|sādhaka]] with [[miraculoug|miraculous]] powers, such as causing rainfall in case of drought ([[Guhya Bamāja|Guhyasamāja]] p. 84), killing an enemy by magical rites over an effigy of the enemy (ibid. p. 96). Besides, the Guhyasamāja knows the [[gix|six]] cruel or terrible magic acts (called [[‘satkarmāṇi),|‘ṣaṭkarmāṇi),]] viz. [[Sānti|Śānti]] (rite for averting disease or black magic), [[vaśikarana|vaśīkaraṇa]] (bewitching women and men and even [[gods ),|gods),]] stambhana (stopping the movements or actions of others), [[vidveṣana|vidveṣaṇa]] (creating enmity between two friends or two persons who love each [[other ),|other),]] [[uccātana|uccāṭana]] (making a person or enemy flee from the country, town or village), [[mārana|māraṇa]] (killing or causing permanent injury to living beings). The [[Guhyasamaja|Guhyasamāja]] mentions these six ([[putting * Akarṣaṇa ’ for ‘vidveṣana’)|putting ‘ākarṣaṇa’ for ‘vidveṣaṇa’)]] respectively at pp. 168, 165, 96, 87 ([[Akarṣaña),|Ākarṣaṇa),]] 81, 130. Vide [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] pp. 368-369 for the same and for the shape of [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] and times for each of the six cruel rites. Even the rather [[sobor Saradātilaka-tantra mentiong|sober Śāradātilaka-tantra mentions]] these six (23. [[12%),|122),]] defines them (23. 123-125), provides that Rati, [[Vāṇi ( speech or Sarasvati),|Vāṇī (speech or Sarasvatī),]] Ramā, [[Jyoṣths,|Jyeṣṭhā,]] Durgā and [[Kāli|Kālī]] are respectively the six deities of these six cruel acts and must be worshipped at the commencement, that six periods of ten ghaṭikās from [[suprise|sunrise]] are respectively appropriate to these six and so also are certain seasons (23. 126-139). It is most astounding that the [[Prapaõoasāra|Prapañcasāra]] (23. 5) [[asoribed|ascribed]] to the great advaita teacher [[Saṅkardoārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] describes at length a mantra called Trailokya-mohana for accomplishing the [[aboy si|above six]] cruel acts.

[[P1071]]Both Hindu and Buddhist [[Tantrag1730|Tantras]]72 lay great emphasis on the importance and qualifications of the guru. The Bauddha Tantras have the highest praise for the guru. The [[Jūlnasiddhi (13.9-12) proscribes|Jñānasiddhi (13. 9–12) prescribes]] high qualifications and the [[Prajiopāyavini soaya-siddhi (III, 9-16 )|Prajñopāyaviniścaya-siddhi (III. 9–16)]] contains a grand eulogy of the guru, identifying him with Buddha and calling him omniscient and [[80|so]] on. The Advayasiddhi of [[Laksmiðkarā|Lakṣmīṅkarā]] (about 729 A. D.), who started the astounding doctrine that one should offer worship to one’s own body wherein all gods reside, says that in the three worlds there is none higher than the ācārya. In the [[Nityotgava|Nityotsava]] of Umānanda-nātha, pupil of [[Bhāgurānanda-nātha (i. . Bhāskararāya|Bhāsurānanda-nātha (i. e. Bhāskararāya]] as he was called before he took [[diksa)|dīkṣā)]] the guru Bhāskararāya is praised in the following hyperbolical [[terms 1731|terms]]73 ’to whom no part of the earth was unseen (owing to his yogic sight), there was no king who was not his slave, to whom no śāstra was unknown; why use more words, whose form was the highest [[Sakti|Śakti]] herself.’ But the [[Jõāna-siddhi|Jñāna-siddhi]] and [[Kulārpava (XII. 128 ) ward|Kulārṇava (XIII. 128) warn]] against gurus who falsely pretend to know the truth and give instruction in dharma from greed for money. The [[Kulārpava ( ullāsas XI and XII)|Kulārṇava (ullāsas XII and XIII)]] are devoted to the [[quali fications|qualifications]] and greatness of guru. The [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] also sets out the qualifications of the tāntrika guru (II. [[142–144 )|142–144)]] and of the disciple [[(:III. 145–152).|(III. 145–152).]]74 The guru among other matters ‘must know the essence of all Āgamas and the principles and meaning of all śāstras, he must be one whose words come out true, who has a quiet mind, who has profoundly studied the Veda and its meaning, who follows the path of Yoga and whose bearing is as beneficent as that of a deity.’ Among the many requirements about a disciple,75 one is that he must keep secret the mantra and [[pājā|pūjā]] imparted by his guru. The disciple places the guru’s feet on his head and surrenders his body, wealth and even life to

aanut Th: 1 Iotro, to HRACIE VOL. II. p. LXIV-LXV.|आचार्यात्परतरं नास्ति त्रैलोक्ये सचराचरे। यस्य प्रसादात्प्राप्यन्ते सिद्धयोऽनेकधा बुधैः ॥ Intro. to साधनमाला vol. II, p. LXIV–LXV.]]

[[P1072]]the guru. The necessity of a guru for the [[aoquisition|acquisition]] of [[esotorio|esoteric]] philosophy is [[stronged|stressed]] by the Upaniṣads also. [[For, example|For example,]] the [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]]76 says ’this knowledge cannot be obtained by ratiocination, it can be understood well only when expounded by another’; the [[Chandogy& (IV.9.3)|Chāndogya (IV. 9. 3)]] says ‘Sir, I have heard from men like you that only knowledge that is learnt from a teacher leads to real good’. The [[Lingapurana1735|Liṅgapurāṇa]]77 and others say that guru is identical with Śiva and the rewards of devotion to Śiva and to the guru are the same. The [[Kularnava (XI.46)|Kulārṇava (XI. 46)]] emphasizes that the order of the succession of gurus, the [[Āgamus,|Āgamas,]] Āmnāya, mantra and [[practiceg-all|practices—all]] these when learnt from the lips of a guru become fruitful and not otherwise. The [[Prapanca. sāra|Prapañcasāra]] provides ’the disciple should consider in his mind that guru, devatā and mantra are one and should repeat a hundred times the mantra that he received by the favour of the guru.’

The Vedānta system requires for its understanding high intellectual and moral attainments and could be followed by only a few gifted souls. It is claimed that Tantras provide [[&|a]] method which assists [[mon|men]] of ordinary intellect and which utilizes visual and physical processes for the attainment of spiritual experience, for the development of [[psychio|psychic]] forces and for the quick attainment of liberation by [[suoh|such]] practices as the repetition of mantras, mudrās, [[nyasa,|nyāsa,]] [[mandalas,|maṇḍalas,]] cakras and yantras. The respect for the guru sometimes reached [[amongat Tāntrik writors|amongst Tāntrik writers]] to extreme and disgusting lengths as the note below will [[indicate.1736|indicate.]]78

[[P1073]]The teaching of Tāntrika texts about the five makāras must have created [[&|a]] very unhealthy and debased state among all classes, particularly the lower orders of Society. The centuries from the 7th to the 12th A. D. were the peak period of Tāntrik works and cults, both Hindu and Buddhist. In [[&|a]] certain cult of the Vajrayāna the gurus wore a blue [[yesture.|vesture.]] A story is narrated of a guru (a monk) of the [[sammitiya|sammitīya]] sect who went dressed in a blue dress to a courtezan. He did not return to the monastery at night. When asked in the morning by his disciples why he put on a blue dress, he explained the great spiritual merit of the blue-coloured dress. Since then his followers began to wear blue dress and in their book called [[‘Nilapatadarsana’|‘Nīlapaṭadarśana’]] it is written ’the god Kāmadeva (Eros) is a jewel, a courtezan is [[&|a]] jewel, wine is a [[jowel;|jewel;]] I bow to these three jewels; other so-called jewels are three glass beads’. It should be remembered that for devout Buddhists, Buddha, Dharma and Sangha are three Ratnas (jewels). The followers of the [[Nilapatadarśana|Nīlapaṭadarśana]] deemed these last three to be as worthless as glass beads. Vide Bhikṣu [[Rahula Sāṅkstyāyana’s|Rāhula Sāṅkṛtyāyana’s]] paper ‘On Vajrayāna or [[Mantrayana ‘|Mantrayāna’]] in J. A. vol. 225 (1934) p. 216 where this is narrated. [[Valge|False]] gurus must have gone about deluding people with rosy prospects of bliss and liberation by the easy method of drinking [[wino,|wine,]] eating flesh and free association with all sorts of women. The Indian literature of those centuries is full of condemnation and caricatures of the methods of Tāntrik worship by means of wine, flesh and sexual laxity. A few instances may be cited here. In the [[prākrit|Prākrit]] play called [[Karpūramañjarl of Rajasekhara|Karpūramañjarī of Rājaśekhara]] (about 900 A. D.), a character called [[Bhairavananda 1737|Bhairavānanda]]79 who was

[[P1074]][[zupposed|supposed]] to possess wonderful powers is introduced and he [[saya|says]] (while representing that he was somewhat tipsy) ‘owing to the favour of our guru we know nothing about mantras or tantras or meditation. We drink wine, dally with women and yet, being devoted to the Kula path, we reach [[mok:a.|mokṣa.]] A fierce strumpet is given dīkṣā and made a lawful wife, wine is drunk and flesh is consumed, our food is got by begging for alms, our bed is a piece of hide. To whom would the Kauladharma not appear attractive ? Even gods headed by Viṣṇu and [[Brahma|Brahmā]] declare that liberation is attained by meditation, Veda study and performance of Vedic sacrifices; only one God, the husband of Umā, perceived that mokṣa [[(oan be attained)|(can be attained)]] by dalliance with women and by wine’. The Yaśastilaka-campū (composed in 959 A. D.) after referring to the Dakṣiṇa and Vāma paths of [[Saivāgama|Śaivāgama]] quotes a [[verge|verse]] of the great poet [[Bhasa|Bhāsa]]80 “a person should drink wine, look at the face of the woman dear to him, wear [[&|a]] dress naturally charming and free from being odd; may the adorable Śiva be long-lived, who (first) discovered such a path to mokṣa!” The [[Daśavatāra-carita of Kṣomendra|Daśāvatāra-carita of Kṣemendra]] (3rd quarter of 11th century A. D.) states in one verse what the Tāntrik gurus and their followers did “Gurus declare that liberation follows by the drinking of wine from the same goblet by various craftsmen such as washermen, weavers, workers in hides, [[kāpālais,|kāpālikas,]] in the procedure of cakrapūjā, and by dalliance with women without the least scruple and by always leading a life of festivities.”81 The [[Rāja-tarangini|Rāja-taraṅgiṇī]] (middle of 12th

[[P1075]]century A. D.) has several references to the [[Tantrikas|Tāntrikas]] and their doings. In V. 66 [[Kalhana|Kalhaṇa]] says that during82 the reign of Avantivarman of Kashmir, siddhas (persons possessed of [[mira culous|miraculous]] powers like [[animā)|aṇimā)]] such as [[Bhatta Kallata|Bhaṭṭa Kallaṭa]] were born for the benefit of the world. In describing the effects of the rule of a good king [[Yasaskara (1939–948|Yaśaskara (939–948]] A. D.), [[Kalhana 1741|Kalhaṇa]]83 remarks that during his reign were not seen housewives figuring as divinities at the rite of [[gurudikṣā|gurudīkṣā]] and shaking their heads for detracting from the eminent character (attributed to) of their husbands.’ King [[Kalasa|Kalaśa]] of Kashmir [[(1063-1089|(1063–1089]] A. D.) became a pupil of [[Pramadakantha,|Pramadakaṇṭha,]] son of [[Amarakantha|Amarakaṇṭha]] who was a good [[brahmana,|brāhmaṇa,]] but Kalaśa, who by nature was evil-minded, was instructed in evil practices by his guru [[Pramadakantha|Pramadakaṇṭha]] and the latter made the former ignore the distinction between women who are approachable and who are not. In this connection, [[Kalhana|Kalhaṇa]] bitterly84 bewails “what other unscrupulousness of this guru (of [[Kalasa)|Kalaśa)]] should be described by me, when, leaving aside all scruples, he committed incestuous intercourse with his own daughter.” This establishes that in the 11th century A. D.

tāntricism and bence occasions for women being gurus did not arise.|नादृश्यन्त च गेहिन्यो गुरुदीक्षोत्थदेवताः । कुर्वाणा भर्तृशीलश्रीनिषेधं मूर्धधूननैः ॥ राजत. VI. 12. This shows that, there being equality of sexes among Tāntrikas, women used to be made gurus in Tāntrik rites. Vide प्राणतोषिणी p. 179 for qualifications of a woman guru and p. 540 for worship of the wife of a guru and of a woman as a guru in her own right. The guru and his predecessors are to be worshipped by disciples as sacrificers. When the latter praised the husbands of the women that had become gurus, they shook their heads in disagreement and thereby impliedly criticized the character of their husbands. Kalhaṇa says that this did not happen in the reign of Yaśaskara, who must have frowned upon the practices of tāntricism and hence occasions for women being gurus did not arise.]]

[[P1076]]in Kashmir there were [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] gurus that literally carried out what the Guhyasamājatantra quoted in note 1724 apparently recommended to Buddhist Yogis. In the drama called [[Moba parajaya|Mohaparājaya]] by [[Yaśabpāla|Yaśaḥpāla]] under king Ajayadeva, successor of [[Kumarapāla,|Kumārapāla,]] among the dramatis personae is a kaula who declares his doctrine to be eating flesh every day, drinking wine without any qualms and allowing the mind free [[scope.1743|scope.]]85 Aparārka quotes a verse which shows that, in the midst of numerous cults, it was difficult to be consistent: “A person may be at heart a Kaula (follower of the Kula doctrine), in outward appearance he may look a Śaiva, and he may follow Vaidika rites in his usual practices. One should live grasping what is essential like the [[cocoanut 1741 fruit”.|cocoanut fruit]]86”. It appears that great scholars and poets had a sneaking admiration for Tāntrika worship. The great writer Vidyāpati of Mithilā appears to be a Vaiṣṇava from his devotional songs, but wrote the [[Saivasarvasvasāra|Śaivasarvasvasāra]] (so he may be called a [[Śaiva ),|Śaiva),]] and wrote also [[Durgābhaktitarangini|Durgābhaktitaraṅgiṇī]] (and thus appears to have been a [[Sākta)|Śākta)]] and composed also a [[Tāntrik 1745 work.|Tāntrik work.]]87 The very first verse of Vidyāpati’s [[‘Puruşa parīkṣā’|‘Puruṣaparīkṣā’]] contains an invocation of [[Adi-Sakti.|‘Ādi-Śakti’.]] Bengal and Assam were the strongholds of [[Sākta|Śākta]] doctrines and even now [[Kāli|Kālī]] worship is still in vogue there, but it was the great Bengal

[[P1077]]king Ballālasena who discarded [[Devipurāṇa|Devīpurāṇa]] as an authority in his encyclopaedic work on gifts called [[Dānasāgara.1746|Dānasāgara.]]88

It is possible that the founders of the Tāntrik or [[Sākta|Śākta]] cult of the five makāras threw defiance at the terrible aspect of God or the Highest Spirit, that rules the destinies of men and things and that made men sometimes suffer terribly even if they led lives of virtue, to do the worst for their disregard of conventional morality and social practices and hoped by their Yoga exercises to attain to high psychical powers and [[bliss. 1747|bliss.]]89 There is another possible motive also. Masses of common people were being drawn towards Buddhism. The founders of Hindu Tāntrik cults wanted to retain them within the Hindu fold. As common people drank wine and ate meat, they were told that they would attain higher spiritual levels even while indulging in meat and drink provided they followed Tāntrik gurus and practices. The idea was that [[Sakti|Śakti]] was all and was for all; bhoga (enjoyment) need not be given up, as man is part of Devi or Śiva. Bhoga should be sublimated, that is all that is required in [[Kaula-śāstra.|Kaulaśāstra.]] The [[Tāntrikas 1748|Tāntrikas]]90 substitute a yoga of enjoyment (bhoga) for the yoga of abstinence and asceticism. When indulging in the left-hand path practices the sādhaka is supposed or held to be destroying the egoistic elements of the soul.

The Mahānirvāṇatantra and a few others endeavour to stem the tide of sexual immorality and promiscuity. For example, Rāmeśvara, the commentator of Paraśurāmakalpasūtra, says that one that has not conquered his senses has no adhikāra for Kaulamārga (p. 153). This is in direct conflict with what even the Mahānirvāṇatantra says that all men from brāhmaṇas to the untouchables have adhikāra for Kula practices. Modern apologists for Tāntrism emphasize that the instructions embodied in the Guhyasamāja, the directions for the mode of life to be led by followers of Vajrayāna, apply only to the Yogis who have attained some degree of yogic perfection. But the obvious

[[P1078]]reply is ‘who is to decide that a particular person had attained the minimum spiritual level except the man himself and, [[pupposing|supposing]] that all the directions were meant for Yogis, why was it necessary to say in a blatant manner and language that a Yogi practising [[Vajrayana|Vajrayāna]] may engage in what common and less sophisticated people deem to be incest? This is not the place to reply to all apologists for ancient and medieval Tāntrik works. But a few must be dealt with because they are liable to create misunderstandings, if allowed to go uncriticized. In his Preface to ‘Principles of Tantra’ Part 2, Sir John Woodroffe [[statos|states]] (p. IX.) that the use of flesh, fish and wine was common in the Vedic age and that the Mahābhārata and some Purāṇas like the Kālikā, [[Mārkandeya|Mārkaṇḍeya]] and Kūrma refer to consumption of wine, meat and fish. This looks like special pleading and is misleading. The question is: was [[sirū|surā]] offered to the gods in every day or periodic sacrifices as an offering in the [[Roveda|Ṛgveda]] or any other Veda. In the Vedic age wine may have been known and even drunk, but what is material to recognize is that there is a difference made between Soma and surā. [[Vida Sat. 176 Br.|Vide Śat.]]91 Br. “Soma is truth, prosperity, light and surā is untruth, [[nigary|misery]] and darkness” (V. 1. 5. 28). While Soma is mentioned hundreds of times in the Ṛgveda, has the 9th [[mandala|maṇḍala]] of the Ṛgveda specially reserved for its praises and was offered to gods, the word [[surū|surā]] occurs only six times in the Ṛgveda and it is nowhere expressly stated that it was offered to any god as [[&|a]] religious offering; on the contrary, in a hymn to Varuṇa, [[burā|surā]] is put forward as leading to the commission of sin just as anger and gambling do (Ṛg. VII. 86. 6 ’na sa [[svo.|svo]] [[dakso|dakṣo]] [[Varuna|Varuṇa]] [[dhrutih|dhrutiḥ]] sā surā manyur-vibhidako [[acittih).|acittiḥ’).]] In [[bis|his]] zeal for the vindication of Tāntrism Arthur Avalon is injudicious enough to misinterpret simple words. In the Intro. to ‘Principles of Tantra’ p. VII [[be|he]] quotes Ṛg. I. 166. 7 “arcantyarkam madirasya [[pitaye”|pītaye”]] and translates “worshipping the Sun before drinking [[madira (wine)."|madirā (wine).”]] The word here is madira (and not ‘madirā’), it is an adjective and means “exhilarating”. The word “Madirā” never occurs in the Ṛg. while “madira” as an adjective, occurs at least sixteen times and qualifies Soma, Indu, [[Amsu,|Aṁśu,]] Rasa or Madhu, generally expressed (and rarely implied). There is no word for “before.” That quarter means “they (the worshippers or Maruts) worship (Indra) who is worthy of praise (and a friend of the Maruts) in order that he may come for

r vatia: HATSTE TOT A: Timu V, 1, 5, 28.|सत्यं वै श्रीर्ज्योतिः सोमोऽनृतं पाप्मा तमः सुरा । शतपथ V. 1. 5. 28.]]

[[P1079]]drinking the exhilarating [[(Soma).|(Soma)’.]] The word madirā (for [[wine )|wine)]] hardly ever occurs in any [[genuing|genuine]] work of Vedic times. Its earliest [[oocurrence|occurrence]] is probably in the [[Mahābbārate.|Mahābhārata.]] Reference is also made by modern apologists to the offering of surā in the [[Sautrāmaṇl iṣti|Sautrāmaṇī iṣṭi]] in honour of [[Indre|Indra.]] But the circumstances are peculiar. Sautrāmaṇī is only one out of numerous sacrifices and the occasions for performing it were rare [[i.e.|i. e.]] it was performed at the end of [[Rājagūya|Rājasūya]] and also at the end of Agnicayana and when a priest who had drunk too much of soma vomited. The most important fact, however, is that the remnants of surā offered in Sautrāmaṇī were not drunk by the priests engaged in it but a brāhmaṇa [[bad|had]] to be hired for drinking the remnants and if no brāhmaṇa could be secured then the remnants were to be poured over an ant-hill (vide H. of Dh. vol. II, p. 1226 for details). The [[Kathakagamhitā 1750|Kāṭhakasaṁhitā]]92 contains an interesting passage “Therefore an elderly person and a youngster, the daughter-in-law and the father-in-law, drink liquor and remain babbling together; thoughtlessness is sin; therefore a brāhmaṇa does not drink surā with the thought that ‘otherwise (if I drink it) I may be tainted by sin’; therefore this is for [[ksatriya;|kṣatriya;]] one should say to a brāhmaṇa that surā, if drunk by [[&|a]] [[ksatriya,|kṣatriya,]] does not harm the latter.” These passages show that not only the priests did not drink surā even in [[Sautrāmapi|Sautrāmaṇī]] but that it had become difficult to hire [[& brāhmapa|a brāhmaṇa]] for drinking it by the time of the [[Kathaka-gamhitā.|Kāṭhaka-saṁhitā.]] The passage of the Vājasaneya Saṁhitā 19. 5 relied on at p. VII (of Intro [[to|to ‘Principles]] of Tantra’ part II) also refers to [[Saytrāmaṇi|Sautrāmaṇī]] and to no other [[gagri fice.|sacrifice.]] The mantra is [[“Brahma kṣatram pavate toja, indriyam surayā - somaḥ suta asuto madāya”|‘Brahmā kṣatraṁ pavate teja indriyaṁ surayā somaḥ suta āsuto madāya’]] and means that: “Soma when mixed with surā becomes a strong drink and leads to [[intoxication."|intoxication.’]] The Chāndogya V. 10. [[9’ (q.|9 (q.]] above p. 1578 n. [[943’)|943)]] counts the drinker of surā among the five grave sinners. [[There fore,|Therefore,]] there is hardly any analogy between the offering of wine in Sautrāmaṇī and the teaching about offering [[wine. to Doyl|wine to Devī]] as provided in the Tantras. Similarly, the reference to the [[Atharyaveda|Atharvaveda]] as containing magical [[rites. does|rites does]] not help at all. Society had advanced far beyond [[that-stage|that stage]] and Manu XI. 63 looked upon abhicāra (i. [[o.|e.]] performance of a [[magio|magic]] rite like Śyenayāga for killing a person) and [[mūlakārmda|mūlakarma]] (i. e. bringing

[[P1080]]under one’s control a person or a woman by herbs and mantras) as a sin, though a [[legger|lesser]] one (an upapātaka). The reference to the Mahābhārata [[(Udyogaparya,| (Udyogaparva,]] 59. 5 ff) is again misleading. Wine was drunk by people in the Mahābhārata times, but not as part of a religious ritual as in the Tantras. Vide pp. 964–966 of H. of Dh. vol. III for a note on ‘intoxicating drinks.’ The reference to Mārkaṇḍeya and other purāṇas is of no avail as evidence, since at least the present author holds that those parts of the Purāṇas were written and interpolated after Tāntrik rites had taken a firm hold on some sections of the Hindu society. The reference to sexual intercourse in the Mahāvrata93 is most misleading. In the Tantras like the Kulārṇava and the [[Guhya. samāja|Guhyasamāja]] it is the sādhaka himself who is to practise maithuna as one of the ways of securing marvellous powers and higher spiritual attainments. But in the Mahāvrata the sexual intercourse is by strangers to the sacrifice (and not by the yajamāna nor by any of the [[priests ),|priests),]] is a purely symbolical act and [[beans 20|bears no]] analogy to a sādhaka himself engaging in maithuna as a religious rite to placate the Devī. Even [[&|a]] late reformist work like the [[Mahānispāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VIII. 174–175) expressly says that those of the five tattvas which a sādhaka can secure, such as flesh and others, should have recited over them the mantra ‘ām, hrīm, krom, [[svābā’|svāhā’]] a hundred times, that he should reflect that every thing is [[&|a]] product of brahma, should close his eyes, should offer those to Kālī and should himself drink and eat them. As the Tāntrik prescriptions of the makāras for attainment of [[mira culous|miraculous]] powers and of liberation had shocked people and [[bad|had]] brought the tantras into great disrepute, later Hindu Tāntrik works like the Śaktisaṅgamatantra (between 1555–1607 A. D.) began to offer symbolic interpretations. They94 say that the

Vide ‘Śakti and Śākta’ pp. 339–340 where madya, māṁsa, matsya and maithuna are interpreted esoterically for a ‘divyabhāva’ following Yoginītantra (chap. VI) and from Āgamasāra. One verse from the former may be quoted: ‘सहस्रारोपरि बिन्दौ कुण्डल्या मेलनं शिवे । मैथुनं परमं द्रव्यं यतीनां परिकीर्तितम् ॥’. Substitutional तत्त्वs are also variously mentioned for पशु, who is the lowest kind of the worshippers of Śakti. The कौलावलीनिर्णय V. 113–123 suggests various substitutes viz. a ब्राह्मण may employ honey in a copper vessel or cow’s milk or the water of cocoanut fruit in a bell-metal vessel; that, in the absence of flesh, garlic and ginger may be used for flesh, milk of buffalo or sheep for fish, roasted fruits and roots for maithuna. It will be, however, noticed in the above and also later on that the explanations do not all completely agree and therefore raise doubts about their veracity.]]

[[P1081]]words ‘[[medya,|madya,]] [[mudrā, maithuna’ &o.|mudrā, maithuna’ &c.]] are not used in [[this|the]] ordinary popular sense but in a special [[esoterio|esoteric]] [[sense. .. For|sense. For]] example, mudrā has several [[sensos,|senses,]] viz. a mixture of jaggery and ginger, or of salt and oil-cake or garlic and tamarind fruit, or wheat and māṣa beans, that madya (wine) is not what is prepared from [[mādbavi (|mādhavī (]]spring [[flower )|flower)]] but it springs from the joyful experience (or rasa) of [[Sakti,|Śakti,]] when effort is made to awaken the [[Kundalini.|Kuṇḍalinī.]] It may be admitted that some Tāntrik works and writers divide men into three classes, paśu (the bestial ones), [[vira|vīra]] (those that have made great progress on the path of spiritual discipline) and daiva (those who are godlike). As regards these three, the five makāras are assigned by some [[apologistio|apologistic]] writers different [[meaninge.|meanings.]] D. N. [[Boge|Bose]] in his work *Tantras, their philosophy and occult secrets’ [[boldly’ asserts|boldly asserts]] (p. 110) that the real significance of the five makāras has been deliberately perverted by vicious people and explains [[(p: 111)|(p. 111)]] that madya is the nectarine stream issuing from the cavity of the brain where the soul resides, matsya means suppression of vital airs, māṁsa means [[’ yow|‘vow]] of silence,’ ‘maithuna’ means ‘meditation on the acts of creation and [[destruction. ..:|destruction.’]]

The Tāntrikas clothe their practices in bombastic and [[high. sounding|high-sounding]] words. The five makāras are called pañca-tattvas, kuladravyas or kulatattvas. Maithuna is generally referred to [[28 parcamatattva,|as pañcamatattva,]] the woman with whom sexual intercourse [[iṣ|is]] to be had or who was at least to be associated with a male in Tantra worship is called [[Sakti|Śakti]] (vide [[Kulārnava VII 39-43|Kulārṇava VII 39–43]] and [[Mahānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] VI. [[18-20)|18–20)]] or [[praksti|prakṛti]] or latā and this special ritual is called ‘Latāsādhana’ (Mahānirvāṇa I. 52). Wine is called [[tirthavāri|tīrthavāri]] (holy water) or kāraṇa (VIII. 168 and VI. [[17.)|17).]] The [[Mahānirvana-tantra,|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra,]] though a reformist work and in certain [[oases|cases]] asking the king to punish drunkards (XI. 113–121),

[[P1082]]waxes eloquent over wine and praises it as [[Tara|Tārā]] in the [[form 1750 of a|form]]95 of a fluid substance, as the saviour of individual souls, as the mother (or producer) of bhoga (enjoyment) and mokṣa ([[libera tion)|liberation)]] and as the remover of misfortunes and diseases and after some further praise of wine winds up by saying that those men who drink wine according to the rules and with a well-controlled mind are gods on earth (XI. 108). For the worship of [[Sakti|Śakti]] the five tattvas are absolutely necessary ([[Mahānirvana-tantra|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra]] V. [[21-24|21–24]] quoted in note 1695 above and96 Kulārṇava V. 69 and 76). Some tantras say that the meaning of tattva differs according as the person concerned is [[Tamasika|Tāmasika]] (a paśu kind of worshipper), Rājasika (a [[vira|vīra]] as he is called), sāttvika (a divya, a person who is godlike). Madya means according to various Tāntrik Texts real wine as well as a substitute like cocoanut water or any other liquid; it also means that intoxicating [[know ledge|knowledge]] that comes of Yoga practices, whereby the worshipper becomes senseless as regards the external world. [[Maisa|Māṁsa]] is the [[Bot|act]] whereby the sādhaka surrenders himself and his action to Lord [[Śiva|Śiva.]] Matsya (fish, of which the first part ‘mat’ means [[‘mino’)|‘mine’)]] is that psychical state by which the worshipper [[sympa thizes|sympathizes]] with the pleasure and pain of all beings. Maithuna is the union of the [[Sakti Kundalini|Śakti Kuṇḍalinī]] (the woman inside a man’s body) in the [[Mūlādbāra-cakra|Mūlādhāra-cakra]] with the supreme Śiva in the [[Sahasraracakra|Sahasrāracakra]] in the highest centre of the brain and is the stream of the sweet juice that drops from the [[Sabasrāra.|Sahasrāra.]] [[Preparar tion|Preparation]] of hemp (called vijayā or [[‘bhang’)|‘bhāṅg’)]] is madya for some. The [[Mahāniryāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] says (VIII. 170 and 173) that ‘madhura-traya’ may be substituted for wine and for ‘Maithuna’ meditation on the [[foot|feet]] of the [[image of ) Devi|image of Devī]] and japa of the desired mantra may be substituted. The [[Kaulavallnirṇaya|Kaulāvalīnirṇaya]] (III. 111) boldly states that if a man, after partaking of [[vijaya (bhāng) ongages|vijayā (bhāṅg) engages]] in meditation, he sees directly before him the form of the goddess as described in the Dhyānamantra. Some Tantras like the

P Purgi Hentafogo XI, 105.|सुरा द्रवमयी तारा जीवविस्तारकारिणी । जननी भोगमोक्षाणां नाशिनी विपदां रुजाम् ॥ महानिर्वाण० XI. 105.]]

[[P1083]][[Kaulajñananirṇaya|Kaulajñānanirṇaya]] and [[Bhaskarariya|Bhāskararāya]]97 in his commentary on [[Lalitigahasranama|Lalitāsahasranāma]] say that when [[Kundalini in|Kuṇḍalinī is]] roused by [[&|a]] yogin and it reaches the [[thousand-patalled|thousand-petalled]] cakra [[(Sahasrara )|(Sahasrāra)]] from [[whioh|which]] (that has on its pericarp the moon) drops [[neotar|nectar]] which is figuratively spoken of [[aa|as]] madya (vide notes 1716, 1752). The [[Kularnava|Kulārṇava]] at first asserts (I. [[105-107)|105–107)]] ‘Mukti does not result from the study of the Veda nor by the study of śāstras, it results from (correct) knowledge alone, that the āśramas are not a means of [[moksa|mokṣa]] nor are the [[darsanas|darśanas]] the means, nor all the śāstras, that it is knowledge alone that is the cause, that it is the knowledge imparted by the teaching of the guru that confers mukti, all (other) vidyās are mockery.’ Then in the Vedāntic strain it affirms (I. [[111-112 )|111–112)]] “two words (respectively) lead to bondage or liberation viz. (this is) ‘mine’ or ’nothing is [[mine ‘.|mine’.]] A person falls in bondage by the thought ’this is mine’ and becomes liberated by realising ’nothing is mine’ and that that is proper action that does not lead to bondage and that is real lore that leads to [[liberation."|liberation.’]] After these high thoughts the same Tantra (II. [[22-23|22–23]] and 29) proceeds to Kaula doctrine. ‘If a man is a yogin he does not (ordinarily) enjoy life, while one who enjoys life does not know yoga; but the Kaula doctrine combines both yoga and bhoga and is superior to all (other doctrines); in the Kaula doctrine bhoga turns into yoga directly, what is sin (acc. to ordinary people) becomes meritorious, [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] turns into mokṣa. Kaula knowledge enlightens him whose mind is purified by the mantras of Śaiva worship, Durgā worship [[&c. “|&c.’]]

The [[Kulārnava|Kulārṇava]] appears to be in two minds to ordinary men like the present writer. While in one breath as shown in note 1718 it frankly recommends the drinking of wine and eating of flesh to the followers of Kaula doctrine, it also endeavours to give an esoteric meaning to the makāras as follows (V. [[107-112):|107–112):]] Frequently reaching the brahmarandhra from the mūlādhāra, there arises the bliss of the union of the [[Kundalini-Sakti|Kuṇḍalinī-Śakti]] with the moon in the form of Intelligence (cit, [[Śiva );|Śiva);]] the person who is intent on tasting the nectar oozing from the lotus in the

[[P1084]][[orown|crown]] of the head is said to drink sudhā ([[neotar,|nectar,]] wine); other people drink [[nere|mere]] wine. When the adept98 in Yoga cutting the beast (ego) doing good or evil actions with the sword of knowledge (of reality) makes his mind merge in the Highest, he is said to be eater of pala (the Highest, flesh). The (Yogin) who restraining the several senses by his mind concentrates them on the Self, becomes ‘matsyāśī’,99 others are only killers of living beings. The [[Sakti|Śakti]] (the woman associated with a [[sādhaka )|sādhaka)]] of a beastly man is unenlightened but that of the Kaulika is enlightened; he who honours (or resorts to) such a [[Sakti|Śakti]] is really a worshipper of [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] When a man is filled through and through with the bliss due to the union of the Highest [[Sakti|Śakti]] with the Self ([[Śiva ),|Śiva),]] that is called Maithuna [[(copulation );|(copulation);]] all other men are merely licentious persons.

The apologists for unpopular Tāntrik practices offer explanations of the five ‘makāras’ more or less on the lines of the [[Kulār-anava.|Kulārṇava.]] For example, in his Introduction to the ‘Principles of Tantra’ (part II) Arthur Avalon (Sir John Woodroffe) gives (pp. [[CVII-CVIII )|CVII–CVIII)]] an esoteric explanation of the word ‘drinking’ used in such a verse as that in the Pārānandasūtra, ‘Having drunk again and again, having fallen upon the earth, then having got up and again drunk (wine) there is no [[rebirth.’ 1758|rebirth.’]]100

[[P1085]]He explains ‘Being thus awakened [[Kundalinl|Kuṇḍalinī]] enters the great road to liberation (mukti), that is, the [[Sushumna|Sushumnā]] nerve, and penetrating the centres one by one, ascends to the [[Sahasrara|Sahasrāra]] and there coming in blissful communion with the Lord of Lords, again descends down through the same passage to the Mūlādhāra Chakra. Nectar is said to flow from such communion. The sādhaka drinks it and becomes supremely happy. This is the wine called Kulāmṛta, which a sādhaka of the spiritual plane drinks……In reference to a sādhaka of the spiritual ([[ādhyātmika )|ādhyātmika)]] class the Tantra says [[‘Pitvā pitvā…vidyate’.|‘Pītvā pītvā…vidyate’.]] During the first stage of [[Şat-cakra-sādhanā|Ṣaṭ-cakra-sādhanā]] the sādhaka cannot suppress his breath for a sufficiently long time at a stretch to enable him to practise concentration and meditation in each centre of Power. He cannot therefore detain [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] within the Sushumnā longer than his power of Kumbhaka permits. He must consequently come down upon the earth i. e. the Mūlādhāra, which is the centre of the element earth, after having drunk of the heavenly ambrosia. The sādhaka must practise this again and again and by constant practice, the cause of rebirth i. e. vāsanā (desire) is [[removed."|removed.’]] This explanation sounds very profound and highly psychical, but it is not at all convincing. Similarly, the present author wonders how many writers on Tantras and how many Tāntrikas understood the theory of sublimation put forward in ‘Tantras as a way of realization’ (Cultural Heritage of India, Vol. IV. pp. [[233–235 )|233–235)]] by way of explaining the insistence on five makāras. The first question is: why was it necessary to employ vulgar language to describe a state of profound bliss? Supposing the explanation of madya offered by Woodroffe is accepted, what is the explanation about offering flesh and fish? One cannot easily get out of [[‘matsyāśi’|‘matsyāśī’]] and [[‘māmsāśi’|‘māṁsāśī’]] the esoteric sense required by the apologists. The [[Kulārnava,|Kulārṇava,]] the Pārānanda-sūtra and several

[[P1086]]other works almost always employ the words madya, [[mānga|māṁsa]] and fish in the ordinary [[sen86.|sense.]] They rarely, if at all, drop the hint that words like matsya and māṁsa are being used in an esoteric sense and proceed to deal with realities of madya and flesh. The Kulārṇava quotes (II. 126) the verse of Manu IX. 93 (surā vai malam-annānām &c.), provides for the preparation of the three kinds of surā (V. [[15–21 ),|15–21),]] and says (V. 30) that surā is the 12th kind among intoxicants and that there are eleven other kinds of intoxicants made from jack-fruit, grapes, dates, sugar-cane &c. (V. 29). In XI. 22–35 the Kulārṇava dilates upon the etiquette to be observed in drinking wine as part of Kaula practice. It proceeds (V. 44) to divide flesh into three classes viz. of animals that fly in the sky (birds), of [[acquatic|aquatic]] animals and of those that move on the earth. The101 Svacchandatantra (a work of great authority in Kashmir [[Saivism )|Śaivism)]] prescribes that various kinds of fish and meat and other food that can be licked and drunk should be poured in front of Śiva (image) and one should not stint for money. Quotations from Pārānanda-sūtra (notes 1698, [[1703-4 )|1703–4)]] make it perfectly clear that the words madya, [[māmsa,|māṁsa,]] and maithuna are employed therein in the ordinary sense. Pārānandasūtra (on pp. [[80-81,|80–81,]] paragraphs [[69-70|69–70]] and pp. [[82-83|82–83]] para. [[76-80 )|76–80)]] describes such obscene details of the sādhaka’s sexual intercourse that it is not possible to set them down here. The ordinary worship of [[Devi|Devī]] was elaborate enough, containing as it did 16 upacāras (vide p. 164 note 420 above). Where was the necessity of bringing in wine, meat and maithuna as an absolutely necessary part of the worship of [[Devi ?|Devī?]] The Kulārṇava and other tantras praise the Vedas, employ vedic mantras and [[quote 1760|quote]]102 Upaniṣad and Gītā passages. For example, Mahānirvāṇa V. 197 ff employs Vedic mantras (as noted on p. 1058) for sanctifying the five makāras, the three mantras ‘Āpo hi [[stha’|ṣṭhā’]] Ṛg. X. 9. 1–3 in Nāmakaraṇa (ibid. IX. [[150-152 ),|150–152),]] ‘Tac-cakṣur’ Ṛg. VII. 66. 16 in Niṣkramaṇa (ibid. IX. [[163 ),|163),]] ‘Viṣṇuryonim’ Ṛg. X. 184. 1 in Garbhādhāna (ibid. IX. [[96 ),|96),]] ‘Ayam te yonir’ Ṛg. III. 29. 10 in the worship of Agni (ibid. IX. [[21 )|21)]] and the verse ‘Kālī Karālī’ (Muṇḍakopaniṣad I. 2. 4)

[[P1087]]in the worship of the seven tongues of Agni. The [[Mabānirvāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VIII. In spite of all this lip service to the Veda the tantras ignore the great moral dangers involved in their persistent teaching of five makāras and also pay no heed to the impressive advice of the Gītā (III. 21) that whatever a great or honoured man does or holds as authoritative is followed by all common people. There are several late medieval works about the Kaula cult that speak of drinking wine, eating flesh and maithuna in the vulgar sense as means of [[Devi|Devī]] worship and yet assert that by doing so mukti (liberation) would be secured. Two verses from a ms. of a tāntrik work called Kaularahasya (containing one hundred verses) will bring home to the reader how common people understood the cult of the [[makāras.1761|makāras.]]103

Prof. Heinrich Zimmer in the ‘Art of Indian Asia’ (vol. I pp. 129–130) observes ‘such living forms are suggested to the Indian artist by a dynamic philosophy that is intrinsic to his religious and philosophical tradition, for the worship of the [[lifeforce|life-force]] pouring into the universe and maintaining it, manifesting itself no less in the gross matter of daily experience than in the divine beings of religious vision constitutes the very foundation of Indian religious life. According to this doctrine which was particularly influential in the great periods of Indian art, release from the bondage of our normal human imperfection can be gained not only through the [[worldnegating|world-negating]] methods of asceticism (yoga) but equally through a perfect realization of love and its sexual enjoyment (bhoga). According to this view which has been eloquently expressed in the so-called Tāntric symbols and rituals of both the Hindu and Buddhist traditions, there is intrinsically no antagonism between yoga and bhoga. The role played by the guru, the spiritual guide and teacher in the stern masculine disciplines of yoga, is

The initiating [[women play|woman plays]] the part of [[Sakti|Śakti]] while the male initiate assumes that of [[Siva|Śiva]] and both attain together to the realisation of the immanence within themselves of the consubstantiality of the Goddess and the God.’ It has already been stated (p. 1054) that Prof. Zimmer is wrong in thinking that the Tāntrik rites were systematically disparaged as ‘vāmamārga’ by the Indian partisans of Yoga. He is also wrong (p. 130) when he says that ’throughout the first [[millenium|millennium]] A. D. they (Tāntric rites) were a basic element of normal Indian [[experi ence.’|experience.’]] There is really very little evidence to substantiate this claim. He makes this sweeping generalisation as an art-critic and historian of Indian Art from the few tāntric sculptures on the Purī and other temples in Orissa and some other places in India. Vide the well-known work ‘Bengal Lancer’ by F. Yeats-Brown (London, Golancz, 1930) pp. [[23:-237,|235–237,]] where the superintendent of the temple of Jagannātha justifies the obscene frescoes on the ground that until a man is master of his gross body he cannot see the Godhead and feels sure that future ages will look on Tāntrik psychology with understanding.

One or two very peculiar notions and practices of the Tāntrikas may be briefly mentioned here. The offering of flesh, wine and mudrā to the devatā with the recital of three [[bijas|bījas]] ‘āṁ, hrīm, krom’ and the mantras ‘om Ānandabhairavāya [[Damah’|namaḥ’]] and ‘om Ānandabhairavyai [[namah’|namaḥ’]] was technically called [[suddhi|śuddhi]]104.
