Virs only in Trata and Dvāpara and these two were 000-existent in Kali and Pasu-bbāva romained lo Kali (VI. 10 and 21).|The कुलार्णव (17. 25) defines वीर as ‘वीतरागमदक्लेशकोपमात्सर्यमोहतः । रजस्तमोविविक्तत्वाद्वीर इत्यभिधीयते ॥’. In spite of these requirements of high qualities the रुद्रयामल (in 28. 31–36) provides that a वीर should honour the beautiful wife of another (or ‘his own wife or another’s’) wearing charming ornaments whose body is suffused with erotic passion and who has become gay with wine ‘अथ वीरो यजेत्कान्तां परकीयामथापि वा ।…मदनानलतप्ताङ्गीमासवासवमन्दविह्वलाम् ॥’ &c. The महानिर्वाण० I. 57 refers to the three categories of साधक viz. पशु, वीर and दिव्य, the last being defined as दिव्यश्च देवताप्रायः शुद्धान्तःकरणः सदा । द्वन्द्वातीतो वीतरागः सर्वभूतसमः क्षमी ॥ (ibid. I. 55). The Tantras contain conflicting views on these three bhāvas. The Kālivilāsa-tantra says that men of divya type existed only in Satya-yuga and Tretāyuga, Vīra only in Tretā and Dvāpara and these two were non-existent in Kali and Paśu-bhāva remained in Kali (VI. 10 and 21).]]

[[P1089]]was like swallowing poison, that the person doing so would suffer from diseases for a long time, would soon die prematurely and that wine was to be drunk even by one who has attained some perfection only up till the mind has reached an ecstatic state of absorption (in thoughts of [[Dovi)|Devī)]] and that if he drinks beyond that stage he at once becomes a [[sinnor|sinner]] (vide also Kulārṇava [[VIL.|VII.]] 97–98 for the last proposition).

A most [[rovolting|revolting]] rite from the view-point of all [[unsophisti cated|unsophisticated]] people is what is called [[‘oakra-pājā’|‘cakra-pūjā’]] (worship in a circle). An equal number of men and women without distinction of caste and even near blood relations secretly meet at night and sit in a [[oircle|circle]] (vide [[Kaulāvallnirṇaya|Kaulāvalīnirṇaya]] VIII. 76). The Goddess is represented by a yantra (diagram). There is a leader of the cakra. The regulations were that only persons who had attained the status of Vīra (defined above in n 1763) were to be admitted106 and paśus (ordinary men with beastly passions not curbed) were to be excluded. What assurance was there that the leader of the cakra himself possessed the noble qualities mentioned in the [[vorse|verse]] quoted above and would choose only men possessed of similar qualities ? The women assembled cast their [[bodicos|bodices]] in [[&|a]] receptacle and each of the assembled men [[gocurod|secured]] a female companion for that night by lot [[i. o,|i. e.]] by taking a bodice out of those contained in the receptacle. The practice of

[[P1090]]Śrīcakra must have given rise to great obloquy and unpopularity for the Tāntrikas. Therefore, the Kulārṇava107 advises that cakrapūjā should be sub rosa. ‘What happens at Śrīcakra, whether good or bad, should never be uttered (in public); this is the order (of God); one should never give out information about what happens at Cakrapūjā’. The 18th century Mahānirvāṇatantra, which is reformist in character, states that, since in the Kali age (in which people are weak and the influence of sinful age is very strong), for the last tattva (i. e. maithuna) one’s own wife is alone to be the Śakti because in that case no fault can be found or some substitute like red sandalwood paste may be employed. In the author’s youth whispers floated about that in some towns in Mahārāṣṭra cakrapūjā was practised, that even great Pandits thought that their learning was due to the favour of Devī, that they, though very orthodox in other respects, took once a year in Devīpūjā a thimbleful of wine as prasāda. The learned Achyutarāya108 Modak of Nasik composed at Nasik a work called ‘Avaidikadhikkṛti’ (condemnation of non-vaidika practices) wherein he severely handled the cult of the five makāras.

Naturally common people, who could not appreciate or understand the abstruse and subtle philosophy of Śakti, Nāda, Bindu and so forth, seized with avidity upon the apparently easy path of worship of Śakti by the five makāras and the mantras, bījas, cakras and the like taught by the Tantras and it has been seen above (pp. 1073–76) to what depths some persons professing to be gurus, Śāktas and Tāntrikas descended in course of time.

The path of the Tantras was in its higher level one of Upāsanā or Bhakti, though it very often degenerated into magic and moral depravity. The deity worshipped viz. Parameśvarī

[[P1091]]presented three aspects to the upāsaka109 [[( devotee)|(devotee)]] viz. gross (sthūla), subtle (sūkṣma) and para (highest). The first aspect is represented by the form of [[Devi|Devī]] with hands, feet and other limbs, which is fit for worship with the hands and the eyes of the devotee; the second aspect consists of mantras, which are fit for apprehension with the organs of hearing and speech by those who have the good fortune to receive the mantras from [[&|a]] worthy guru. The third aspect (para) is one to be apprehended by the mind of the sādhaka and described as [[all-poryading|all-pervading]] consciousness and the like (in [[Nityaṣodasika|Nityaṣoḍaśikā]] VI. 49-50).

Some modern writers have [[beon|been]] somewhat unjust to the whole [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] literature in labelling it all as black magic or as full of obscenities. The present writer is not one of those who [[some times|sometimes]] hold that what is not understood is either false, absurd or non-existent. He is prepared to believe that the end and aim of a few of the higher minds among Tāntrikas and of some of the works on Tantra was the attainment of high spiritual powers by Yoga practices, the Realization of the Supreme Tattva (Principle) variously designated as Brahma, Viṣṇu, [[śiva|Śiva]] or [[Dovi|Devī]], and Liberation (mokṣa). He is aware that many of these claim to be based on Vedic traditions, teachings and practices and to have further developed the conceptions underlying Vedic teachings and ritual and that even the [[magio|magic]] rites contained in [[Boveral|several]] Tāntrik works had their counterparts, though on a much smaller scale, in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]], the [[Atharyaveda|Atharvaveda]], [[Samavidbina|Sāmavidhāna]] Brāhmaṇa and other Vedic works. The present author, though he has carefully studied many of the Tantras and the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] of [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] with the bhāṣya and commentaries thereon, has to admit that he has had no mystic experience, but he is not [[pre pared|prepared]] to deny that prophets, saints, poets and others might have [[mystio|mystic]] visions and experiences. Man’s psychic powers are vast

[[P1092]]and unknown as propounded in Alexis [[Carrell’s|Carrel’s]] work ‘Man the unknown’ and in ‘Invisible influence’ by Dr. Alexander Cannon (15th impression, Rider and Co., London, 1935). He knows that some of the Tāntrik works made a distinction between the rules of ordinary social life and conventions (samājā-dharma) and the peculiar forms of Tāntrik worship, in which, while it lasts, no [[diatinction|distinction]] is made on the ground of caste or [[Box|sex]] (vide notes 1711 and 1713). It may further be conceded that the Tantra works placed women on a footing of equality with men, gave them an exalted position and that they endeavoured to provide a common platform (as shown in n. 1704 above) for differing and wrangling sects of Vaiṣṇavas, Śaivas and others by putting forward [[Dovi|Devī]] as the object of worship for all; but they had not much success as is shown by the [[fact|facts]] that Vaiṣṇavas and Śaivas still carried on their quarrels and that the Tāntrik texts themselves fell into five classes, viz. Śaiva, Śākta, [[Vaisnava|Vaiṣṇava]], Saura and Gāṇapatya and that there are differing doctrines among the Tāntrikas called Kādimata, Hādimata [[&o.|&c.]]

The matters that distinguish Tāntrik works from other religious literature in Sanskrit are the 110 promise of the attainment of miraculous powers, the Realization of the one Supreme Principle in [[&|a]] short time by means of the Tāntrik sādhanā [[ " method|‘method]] or [[procedure ‘|procedure’]] (vide Intro. to ‘Principles of Tantra’ p. XIV), by their insistence on the worship of [[Devi|Devī]] with makāras alone as yielding the desired results (as in [[Maha. nirvāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] V. 24 ‘[[paīcatattva-vibināyām|pañcatattva-vihīnāyām]] pūjāyām na phalod-

[[P1093]][[bhavah’)|bhavaḥ’)]] and on their peculiar ritual of mantras, [[bijas|bījas]] (syllables meaningless to ordinary men), [[nyāgas,|nyāsas,]] mudrās, cakras, yantras and similar things for attaining their goal. The condemnation poured on Tāntricism is principally due to their insistence on wine, meat and sexual unions as the best and the only means for the effective worship of [[Devi,|Devī,]] their theory that by merely repeating some mantra or mantras over wine, meat and other tattvas, by offering them to [[Devi|Devī]] and by meditation on her, one may drink wine or eat meat, [[whon|when]] in the same breath they say emphatically that partaking of wine and meat is sinful without this ritual. This antagonizes those who are not kaulas and who hold that this persistent teaching is very dangerous for the common run of [[mon|men]] and savours of hypocrisy.

Some of the Tantras practically inculcated what appears to non-tāntrikas unbridled licence. The [[Kaulāvali-nirṇaya|Kaulāvalī-nirṇaya]] (IV. 15 ff) asserts: ‘The [[Sāktas|Śāktas]] have no higher means of happiness and liberation than the fifth tattva (i. e. maithuna); [[&|a]] sādhaka becomes siddha only by the [[practice )|(practice)]] of the fifth [[tattve.|tattva.]] If he resorts only to the first (i. e. wine) he becomes only Bhairava, if only to the 2nd (meat) he becomes [[Brahmi,|Brahmā,]] by the third (fish) he becomes [[& mahabhairava,|a mahābhairava,]] by the 4th ([[mudra|mudrā]]) he becomes foremost among [[sādhakan.179|sādhakas.]] 111 The same Tantra [[goos|goes]] further and frankly says ‘all women are fit for intercourse to a (Śākta) worshipper except the wives of his guru or of those [[sāktas|Śāktas]] who have attained to the status of [[Vira,|Vīra,]] that for those who have reached the stage of [[advaja|advaya]] there is no prohibition [[por|nor]] is anything enjoined. To the pure everything is pure, it is only the hankering that is [[blamable.|blamable.’]] In this connection that work advances certain puerile and obscene arguments (in VIII. 223-225) about illicit or incestuous intercourse that cannot be set out here. That tantra does not stand alone in [[suob|such]] statements. For example, the [[Kālivilāsatantra|Kālīvilāsatantra]] (X. 20-21) allows adulterous intercourse to a ‘[[Sākta|Śākta’]] devotee pro-

[[P1094]]vided it is not carried to the last stage of emission and avers that, if he observes the condition in the proviso, he would become the master of supernatural powers in spite of adultery. It should be noted that the author of this work has unabashed hardihood to make Śiva tell this to [[Pārvati.|Pārvatī.]] About wine that work remarks ‘just as drinking of soma112 is prescribed for brāhmaṇas [[is|in]] solemn Vedic sacrifices, similarly wine should be drunk at proper times (or according to the practice of Kaulas), since it confers enjoyment as well as mokṣa; drinking wine is blamable in the case of those who hanker after benefits or who are egoistic; but in the case of those that are free from egoism there is neither prohibition (of drinking) nor the enjoining of it. One who is free from the fetters of making distinctions should practise drinking wine for the purpose of remembering the meaning of the mantras and for making his mind fixed (on worship) but he who resorts to wine, and other tattvas merely for pleasure is sinful.’ The teachings of works like the [[Kaulāvali-nirnaya|Kaulāvalī-nirṇaya]] about drinking wine and sexual intercourse with all sorts of women as the highest means of [[Sakti|Śakti]] worship by persons professing to be advaitins led to great depravity and sexual immorality and orgies as indicated by the criticism in medieval works cited above (pp. 1073–76) that regarded Tāntrik teachings as execrable, though some medieval Hindu works admitted into religious practices nyāsa, mudrā, yantra and the like, which were deemed to be innocuous and which would be described in this work later on. The mere intention of doing good if certain conditions were fulfilled is not enough excuse, when it is most likely that the means proposed for attaining a high spiritual level and for liberation would have the opposite effect on most people. Taking all things into consideration, the present author is constrained to observe that medieval and recent writers who severely criticized Tāntrik works were on the whole largely justified in their condemnation of Tāntrik practices as enunciated in many Tāntrik works and of the works themselves. For one man that attained super-normal powers, high spiritual level and great mystic experience there must have been hundreds of hypocrites, charlatans, and licentious men who deluded trustful and ignorant men and particularly women.

[[P1095]]Only a few Purāṇas like the [[Devipurāṇa,|Devīpurāṇa,]] the [[· Kalika,|Kālikā,]] the [[Devimahatmya|Devīmāhātmya]] in the [[Markandega|Mārkaṇḍeya]] provide for the employment of some of the condemned makāras (madya, [[mārga,|māṁsa,]] matsya) in the worship of the Great Goddess. From about the 6th or 7th century A.D. [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] began to incorporate the special ceremonial characteristics of the [[Saktas|Śāktas]] and Tāntrikas. Aparārka quotes a passage 113 from the [[Devipurāṇa|Devīpurāṇa]] wherein the qualifications of a Sthāpaka (one who [[performis Dovapratiṣthā)|performs Devapratiṣṭhā)]] are set out, viz. he would be the best [[sthāpaka|Sthāpaka]] for establishing images of [[Devi|Devī]] and the [[Matrs,|Mātṛs,]] who knows the vāma (left or opposite) and the dakṣiṇa (right) path of worship, who has thoroughly mastered the veda relating to the [[Mātrs|Mātṛs]] (the Mother Goddesses), who is [[olever|clever]] in the interpretation of [[Panoarātra|Pañcarātra]] works and is proficient in the Tantras of the [[Matrs|Mātṛs]] &c. The [[Kālika-purāṇa|Kālikā-purāṇa]] devotes many chapters (54 ff) to the [[desoription|description]] of mantras, kavacas, mudrās, nyāsas &c. The [[Bhagavatapurāṇa|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] also and Agni114 372.34 expressly say that the worship of gods and of [[Vignu|Viṣṇu]] also is either [[vaidiki,|vaidikī,]] [[tāntriki|tāntrikī]] or [[mitrā,|miśrā,]] the first and third being for the three higher varṇas and the [[tantriki|tāntrikī]] for [[sūdras.|śūdras.]] The [[Bhagavatapurāṇa|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] refers to the worship of Keśava laid down in the Tantras for him who desires to cast out the knot (bondage or grief) of the heart. It ([[Bhagavata)|Bhāgavata)]] also mentions115 [[Vaidiki|Vaidikī]] and [[Tantriki|Tāntrikī]] [[dikṣā|dīkṣā]] (in XI. 11. 37) and refers to the Tāntrik method of the [[angas,|aṅgas,]] [[upāngas,|upāṅgas,]] [[Āyudhas|āyudhas]] and decorations in the worship of the Lord of [[Laksml. 1774|Lakṣmī.]] 116 Some of the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] and medieval nibandhas, however, fully utilized what Tantras [[bad|had]] to say about mantras, japa, nyāsa, [[mandala,|maṇḍala,]] cakra, yantra and

[[P1096]]similar matters. This will be illustrated later by some examples. Even for such a simple and common topic as the 16 [[upaoiras|upacāras]] of worship, the [[Varsakriya-kaumudi|Varṣakriyā-kaumudī]] (p. 156) and [[Ekādasi-tattva|Ekādaśī-tattva]] (p. 59) quote the [[Prapañasāra-tantra|Prapañcasāra-tantra]] (VI. 41-42).

The Purāṇas and some [[smptis|smṛtis]] prescribe short mantras of five, six, eight, twelve, thirteen and more syllables as very efficacious for securing all objects. A few of them are set out in the note 117 below. Medhātithi [[or|on]] Manu says that the word mantra primarily means a part of the Veda comprising [[Rgveda,|Ṛgveda,]] Yajurveda and [[Sānaveda,|Sāmaveda,]] treated as such by those who have studied the Veda, and that expressions like “Agnaye [[svāhā “|svāhā”]] 118 employed in rites like Vaiśvadeva are called [[mantra.|mantras]] in a secondary sense by way of praise. The vedic conception was that a mantra [[bas|has]] great potency and that it must be correctly repeated to secure the desired result, that a mantra defective [[ad|as]] to [[accept|accent]] or as to a letter or wrongly applied did not convey the meaning intended and that becoming a thunderbolt in the form of a word or words it destroys 119 the [[yajamūna.|yajamāna.]] Vedic mantras are

[[P1097]]of four classes viz. [[Țk|ṛk]] (which is metrical), yajus (which has no restriction as to metre but which must be a sentence), [[&|a]] [[saman|sāman]] (which is sung) and nigada (i. [[o.|e.]] [[praiga,|praiṣa,]] meaning words that are addressed by one person to another calling upon the latter to do a certain act, e. g. the words ‘srucaḥ [[sammrddhi,|sammṛddhi,]] [[proksanir āsādaya’.|prokṣaṇīr-āsādaya’.]] Nigadas are yajus in form but are distinguished from yajus by the fact that the former are loudly uttered, while a yajus is ordinarily recited in a low voice. 120 The most sacred mantra is the [[Gāyatri|Gāyatrī]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 62. 10 ’tat-savitur’ &c.). The [[Atharvavada|Atharvaveda]] (XIX. 71. 1) calls it Vedamātā (the mother of the Veda). The [[Bľahadāraṇyakopaniṣad|Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣad]] (V. 14) contains a grand eulogy of the [[Gāyatri.|Gāyatrī.]] 121 Om is a very sacred [[syllablo, &|syllable, a]] symbol of brahma and may be called in the language of the Tantras a [[bija.|bīja.]] There are only a few syllables such as om, [[phat,|phaṭ,]] [[vaṣat|vaṣaṭ]] in the Vedic literature that on the face of them have no meaning but are like [[bija|bīja]] mantras in the Tantra sense. There is a [[Bijanighaṇķu|Bījanighaṇṭu]] (a dictionary of [[bija|bīja]] mantras) printed in [[’ Tantrik|‘Tāntrik]] texts’ Vol. I. pp. 28–29 (where monosyllabic [[bijas|bījas]] such as [[Hrim, Srim, Krim,|Hrīm, Śrīm, Krīm,]] Hum, [[phat,|phaṭ,]] are set out and described in symbolic words indicated in note 1708 above. It is stated about a dozen times in the Aitareya Brāhmaṇa viz. it is the perfection of sacrifice when it is [[rūpasamddha (|rūpasamṛddha (]]perfect as to its [[form )|form)]] [[i, e.|i. e.]] when the [[fk|ṛk]] verse pointedly refers to the sacrificial act that is being performed. 122 The [[Nirukta.(I.|Nirukta (I.]] 15–16) starts a lengthy discussion on Kautsa’s view that mantras have no sense (or are purposeless). There is a long discussion in the [[Pūrvamlmāmgāsūtra|Pūrvamīmāṁsāsūtra]] (I. 2. 31 ff) on the [[name|same]] lines [[88|as]] in the Nirukta. Jaimini states that there is no difference in the meanings of words employed in the Veda and those employed by people and [[Sabara|Śabara]] adds in his bhāṣya (on P. M. S. I. 2. 32) that mantras are recited in sacrifices only for the purpose of conveying or manifesting the meaning. 123 It is difficult to

[[P1098]]define what [[&|a]] vedic mantra is and it is generally understood, as said by [[Sabara,|Śabara,]] that passages or [[versos|verses]] are mantras that are recognised as such by the learned. 124 The whole Veda is divided into five categories viz. Vidhi (hortatory passages as in ‘Agni-[[hotram jubuyāt’),|hotram juhuyāt’),]] mantras, [[nūmadheya|nāmadheya]] (names such as Udbhid in [[‘Udbhida yajeta’|‘Udbhidā yajeta’]] or [[’ Viśyajit’|‘Viśvajit’]] as in [[‘‘Viśvajitā yajeta’),|‘Viśvajitā yajeta’),]] [[ni ṣedha|niṣedha]] (prohibition as in [[’nānstam vadet’)|‘nānṛtam vadet’)]] and [[artharāda|arthavāda]] (explanatory or laudatory [[pagaagos|passages]] as in ‘Vāyu is a deity that is swiftest’). The Nirukta [[( 20 )|((I. 20))]] embodies the ancient view that the [[søges|sages]] had an intuitive perception of [[Dharina|Dharma]] and they transmitted the mantras by oral instruction to those that [[oame|came]] after them and that had no intuitive perception of Dharma. The mantras and [[stolras|stotras]] were supposed [[oven|even]] in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] to induce the gods to come to the sacrifices and to bestow on those who recited them protection, valiant sons, cattle, wealth, victory and all sorts of things (e. g. vide [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 102. 1-5, II. 24. 15-16, II. 25. 2, III. 31. 14, IX. 20. 7, IX. 72. 9, X. 78. 8, X. 105. 1). It has [[beon|been]] shown above p. 920 (in notes 4167-4168) how the Purāṇas prescribed their own mantras for many religious [[gots|acts]] but those mantras also are significant and not meaningless.

Mantras are the very heart and core of Tantraśāstra [[80|so]] much so that the [[sastra|śāstra]] is often spoken of as [[mantragāstra.|mantraśāstra.]] The theory of [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] writings such as the [[Prapancadāra|Prapañcasāra]] and [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] about mantras may be briefly stated as follows: In the human body there are ten [[nadis,|nāḍīs,]] the three principal being [[Idā|Iḍā]] (on the left side from the left testicle to the left nostril), Suṣumnā (in the middle of the body in the spinal chord) and [[Pingalā|Piṅgalā]] (on the right side from the right [[tosticle|testicle]] to the right nostril). The [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] lies asleep coiled like a snake in the [[Mūlādbāra-cakra.|Mūlādhāra-cakra.]] It is a form of the [[sabda-brahma.|śabda-brahma.]] The [[Day!|Devī]] (or [[Sakti)|Śakti)]] assumes the form of [[Kundalini,|Kuṇḍalinī,]] all gods reside in [[Devi|Devī]] and all mantras are Her forms (Śāradātilaka I. 55-57). It has already been stated how from contact with light ([[Jyotib|Jyotiḥ]]) [[Sakti|Śakti]] becomes conscious and has a desire to create, then it solidifies and appears as a bindu. Through the instrumentality

[[P1099]]of [[Kala|Kāla]] (Time) bindu divides itself into three, gross (i. e. [[bija ),|bīja),]] subtle ([[sukemale.|sūkṣma i. e.]] [[nada|nāda]] which is [[bijabindu)|bījabindu)]] and para (i. [[s.|e.]] bindu which is [[karyabindu).|kāryabindu).]] This last is of the nature of an unmanifest sound and is called [[sabdabrahma 17824|śabdabrahma]] 125 by the [[sagos|sages]] ([[Saradatilaka|Śāradātilaka]] I. 11-12, [[Prapeficasara|Prapañcasāra]] I. 41 ff). [[Sabdabrahma|Śabdabrahma]] exists in all things as consciousness; it exists in the bodies of living human beings in the form of [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] and then appears as letters in prose, poetry and so forth, being carried by [[Vayu|Vāyu]] (air) to the throat, palate, teeth &c. The sounds thus produced are called akṣaras and when written they are spoken of as varṇas (letters of the alphabet, [[matrka,|mātṛkā,]] which are 50 from a to [[ksa).|kṣa).]] The impulse to sound production arising in [[muladhara-cakra|mūlādhāra-cakra]] is called [[‘para.’ (vak),|‘parā’ (vāk),]] it is called [[paśyanti|paśyantī]] when it reaches [[’ svādhiṣthāna-cakra,|svādhiṣṭhāna-cakra,]] [[nadhyamā|madhyamā]] in the heart and [[vaikhari|vaikharī]] in the mouth. Both [[aksara|akṣara]] and varṇa are [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] made articulate in speech and visible in writing. All the mantras (some say they are nine crores) are evolved from the varṇas of the alphabet that are deemed (by [[Tantrikas)|Tāntrikas)]] to be living conscious sound powers. It is the [[bija|bīja]] mantras like [[hrim, śrim, krim|hrīm, śrīm, krīm]] that make visible the form of the [[Devata|Devatā]] ([[Mahanirvana v.|Mahānirvāṇa V.]] 18-19). 126 It is wrong to suppose that mantras are mere letters or words or language. They assume different forms, such as [[bija-mantras,|bīja-mantras,]] kavaca, [[hrdaya|hṛdaya]] &c. The [[bijamantras|bījamantras]] like [[Hrim|Hrīm]] (representing [[Tribhuvanośvari|Tribhuvaneśvarī]] or [[Maya),|Māyā),]] [[Srim|Śrīm]] (representing [[Laksmi),|Lakṣmī),]] [[Krim|Krīm]] (representing [[Kali)|Kālī)]] cannot possibly be called language, since they convey no [[mean ing|meaning]] to ordinary men. They are the Devatā ([[Istadevatā|Iṣṭadevatā]] of the

[[P1100]]sādhaka, the worshipper) imparted to the sādhaka by the qualified guru at the time of [[dikai|dīkṣā]] (initiation). They are not efficacious if merely learnt by reading books. [[Amantra accord ing|A mantra according]] to [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] works is the sound body of [[Sakti|Śakti]] charged with the vibrations of the spiritual personality of the original [[Tantrika soor|Tāntrika seer]] of the mantra and endowed with a perpetual store of power by the [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] seer. What is needed to awaken Power in the disciple and to reap the full effect of the mantra is the touch of the guru, the imagination and concentrated will power of the sādhaka. The deity willed that certain letters uttered by the seer should as [[aound|sound]] or sounds convey such and such a meaning and should have a certain potency. The sounds represented by the letters are forms of [[Śivasakti|Śivaśakti]] i. e. [[$abdabrahma.|Śabdabrahma.]] From this last the whole world proceeds in the form of sounds [[(sabda )|(śabda)]] and the objects (artha) which sounds or words denote. The Devatā, the mantra and the guru are the essentials of [[sūdhanā|sādhanā]] (procedure that leads on to Siddhi, laid down in [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] works); the disciple [[bas|has]] to revolve in his mind that the three are non-different. Mantra is not the same thing as a prayer. For a prayer [[&|a]] person may employ any words that he may choose, but in the case of a mantra definite letters are considered necessary, which are deemed to be the forms in which [[Sakti|Śakti]] manifests Herself to the worshipper. A mantra may be in the form of words that have an obvious meaning or in the form of [[latters|letters]] arranged in a particular order and that have no meaning to the uninitiated. It is recognised in some of the works on this [[sāstra|śāstra]] that thought has creative power, that each person is Śiva, and can attain to higher and higher spiritual levels depending upon his ability to realise himself as Śiva. Thoughts are real, kind thoughts will do good to ourselves and to those around us, evil thoughts and wishes of others may cause us suffering.

[[Tāptrik|Tāntrik]] works have their own [[inantras|mantras]] and they also employ [[Vodic|Vedic]] mantras. For example, the mantra [[’ Jātavedase sunavāma’|‘Jātavedase sunavāma’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 99. 1), though addressed to [[Agai,|Agni,]] is employed in later works for invoking Durgā, the mantra ‘Tryambakam yajāmahe’ (Ṛg. VII. 59. [[12 )|12)]] is addressed to Rudra, but is called [[Mṛtyañjaya-mantra|Mṛtyuñjaya-mantra]] or [[mrtasanjivini|mṛtasañjīvinī]] mantra in [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] works and is prescribed for purifying the mind ([[cittasuddhi)|(cittaśuddhi)]] in the [[Mahānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VIII. 243). Similarly, the [[Gāyatri|Gāyatrī]] mantra (Ṛg. III. 62. 10) is employed by the Tāntrikas. Vide [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] XXI. 1-8 and 16 (latter for dhyāna of [[Gayatrl)|Gāyatrī)]] and [[Prapanoasāra, wbiol.|Prapañcasāra, which]]

[[P1101]][[derates|devotes]] the whole [[ohapter|chapter]] 30 to the explanation of om, the [[vyāhrtis|vyāhṛtis]] and the words of the [[Gayatri|Gāyatrī]] and [[Gayatri-sadhana).|Gāyatrī-sādhana).]] The [[Mahānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] prescribes the performance of Vaidikī sandhyā to be followed by the Tāntrikī sandhyā. The Tāntrikī Gāyatrī is [[’ Ādyāyal|‘Ādyāyai]] vidmahe Parameśvaryai [[dhimahi i|dhīmahi |]] tan-naḥ Kāli [[praco dayāti|praco-dayāt ||’]] (Mahānirvāṇa V. 62-63). Even Śūdra Tāntrikas were to recite this last, while the three higher classes were to repeat the vedic Gāyatrī employing om, śrīm and aim before it respectively. The importance of guru, mantra and [[dovatā|devatā]] is emphatically brought out in the following verse ’the person, who considers guru as a mere mortal, a mantra [[ag|as]] mere letters and images (of [[deities )|deities)]] as mere stones falls into Hell.’127 The Rudrayāmala says ‘If Śiva is angered, Guru can save (the pupil), but if Guru is angered, no one can save (the pupil)’.

The Paraśurāmakalpasūtra, [[Jõānārṇavatantra,|Jñānārṇavatantra,]] the [[Sāradās tilaka|Śāradā-tilaka]] and almost all Tāntrika works say that mantras possess wonderful and inconceivable powers, 128 that a follower of Tantra secures all powers by following the practices of the school of his guru traditionally handed down and by faith, that mantras are the means of securing the fruits desired, that the authoritativeness of Tantraśāstra depends mainly on the faith of the followers of the [[sāstra;|śāstra;]] that the sādhaka should come to feel that guru, mantra, the deity, his own soul, mind and life breaths are all one and then he will possess the knowledge of the Highest Self. Some [[ot|of]] the Tāntrik works contain most exaggerated praises of mantras, particularly the śrīvidyāmantra of sixteen letters; [[0. g.|e. g.]] [[Jñānārpaya|Jñānārṇava]] says ‘Even crores of [[Vajapoyas|Vājapeyas]] and thousands of Aśvamedhas are not equal (in merit) to even the utterance of Śrīvidyā and so also gifts of crores of [[Kapila COws|Kapilā cows]] cannot be compared to one utterance of [[frividyā|Śrīvidyā]] (24th

[[P1102]][[patala,|paṭala,]] verses 74-76). Vide [[Agnipurāpa|Agnipurāṇa]] 125. 51-55 [[tor|for]] mantras employed in killing an enemy, [[obapters|chapters]] 134 and 135 for [[Trat lokyavijayavidyā|Trailokyavijayāvidyā]] and [[Sangrāmavijayavidyā|Saṅgrāmavijayavidyā]] respectively.

Numberless mantras are contained in the Tantras made from different arrangements of the constituent parts of a mantra. The [[Mahānirvāpa|Mahānirvāṇa]] first gives [[&|a]] mantra of [[ton|ten]] syllables as noted below and then by different arrangements of the letters and by addition of certain other syllables and words like ‘Kālike’ it forms twelve mantras, remarks that there are [[croros|crores]] of mantras and that the mantras contained in all Tantras are all mantras of the Great 129 [[Devi.|Devī.]]

The word mantra is explained as derived from ‘man’ to think and ’trai’ or [[’tra’.|‘trā’.]] [[Yaska’s|Yāska’s]] Nirukta (VII. 12) derives it simply from ‘man’. The [[Kularpava|Kulārṇava]] says ‘mantra’ is so called since it saves from all dangers, as the [[rādhaka|sādhaka]] thereby is led to ponder over God of immeasurable refulgence that is the only principle (in the world). A similar derivation is given by [[Ramapūrva tapaniya Upanisad,|Rāmapūrvatāpanīya Upaniṣad,]] by the [[Prapalicasara|Prapañcasāra]] and other Tantras. 130 Numerous varieties of mantras called Kavaca, [[Hṣdaya,|Hṛdaya,]] [[Upabr daya,|Upahṛdaya,]] Netra, Astra, [[Raksa|Rakṣā]] and so forth are specified in the [[Tāatrik|Tāntrik]] texts, one or two of which may be illustrated in the note below and the rest are passed over for reasons of space. 131

[[P1103]]The [[Sāradatilaka|Śāradātilaka]] divides mantras into male, female and [[Deuter;|neuter;]] masculine mantras end in ‘hum’ and [[‘phat’,|‘phaṭ’,]] female mantras in ‘svāhā’ and neuter mantras end with ’namaḥ’. This is stretched further so as to apply to letters viz. the vowels [[FF, ! I|ṛ, ṝ, ḷ, ḹ]] are said to be neither and the rest are said to be not neuter but only short and long (Śāradātilaka, VI. 3 and Rāghavabhaṭṭa thereon). [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] (from chap. VII to [[XXII)|XXIII)]] is devoted to mantras of Sarasvatī, [[Lakṣmi,|Lakṣmī,]] [[Bhuva neśvari,|Bhuvaneśvarī,]] Tvaritā and others, Durgā,132 Tripurā, [[Ganapati,|Gaṇapati,]] [[Candran mas.|Candramas.]] Many of the mantras are to be repeated thousands of times or lakhs of times in order to secure full effect. For example, in X. 105-107 the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] prescribes that a certain mantra should be repeated five lakhs of times and then fifty thousand āhutis of ghee should be made and worship of the [[Devi|Devī]] with the procedure prescribed should be performed. The worshipper then [[BOCUNOS|secures]] whatever he may desire and can make kings sub-servient to his will. Another noticeable feature is that the mantras are treated like the mantras of the Veda and mention is made in the manner of the [[Anukramanis|Anukramaṇīs]] of the [[eage,|sage,]] the metre, the deity and the viniyoga (or purpose for which it is to be used or employed). Though the [[Sāra dātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] is a sober work free from the sexual practices of Vāmamārga, still it does contain mantras for magical purposes such as bringing women under control (IX. 103-104, [[X, 76), 1790|X. 76),]] 133 bringing about the paralysing or the death of an enemy by mantras (XI. 60-124, XXI. [[95 XXII,|95, XXII.]] 1 ff).

The Buddhist Tantras did not lag behind the Hindu Tantras in the matter of the [[potenoy|potency]] of mantras. The Sādhanamālā 134

[[P1104]][[aaserts|asserts]] that there is nothing that cannot be accomplished by mantras if proper procedure is followed. For example, it says that a certain mantra that is a prince among mantras [[conforg|confers]] Buddhahood, what need is there to say about [[othere|other]] siddhis, that by another mantra even Buddhahood which is extremely difficult to attain is like a jujube fruit placed on the palm of the [[band|hand]] and that a certain mantra (of unmeaning [[worde)|words)]] if repeated five times at three periods in the day viz. the morning, noon and evening, makes even a fool (lit. an ass) master of three [[handred|hundred]] works. The Buddhist Tantras also prescribe repetition of mantras for a lakh 135 of times (vide [[Sadhanamālā,|Sādhanamālā,]] vol I, No. 165

[[P1105]]p. 336 and No. 108. p. 221). Some of the mantras embody the doctrines of Mahāyāna with the addition of syllables like om, [[phat,|phaṭ,]] [[svāha|svāhā]] (such as [[‘om sūnyatā-jāānavajrasvabhava tmakoham’|‘om śūnyatā-jñānavajrasvabhāvātmakoham’]] in [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] vol. I. p. 62). The [[Prapanoasāra,|Prapañcasāra,]] which is ascribed to the great advaita teacher [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] and on which Padmapāda is said to have written a commentary (both published by A. Avalon in Tāntrik Texts, vol. XVIII-XIX, 1935), contains a mantra called Trailokya-mohana for the purpose 136 of the six cruel magic rites and (in 34. 33) describes [[&|a]] yantra (diagram) by worship of which a sādhaka can make a woman smitten with passion for him and draw her to [[him,|him.]] This, among other matters such as ungrammatical forms, raises grave doubts as to whether the work was really composed by the first [[Saṅkarācārya.|Śaṅkarācārya.]] It may, however, be stated that the learned [[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] in many places in his commentary on the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] (such as I. 7, 8, 12) quotes the [[Prapancasāra|Prapañcasāra]] (I. 42, 44) as the great ācārya’s work and several later writers do the same. It has to be borne in mind, however, that about 400 works are ascribed to the great advaita [[Acārya|Ācārya]] and that [[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] was separated by at least seven centuries from the great Ācārya and his ascription therefore cannot be accepted with implicit faith without more [[evidence,|evidence.]]

Several Purāṇas are affected by the theory about the power of mantras of the [[Tantrika|Tāntrika]] type. The Garuḍa-purāṇa (I. chap. 7 and 10) employs many monosyllabic unmeaning mantras like hrām, kṣaum, [[brim,|hrīm,]] hum, [[kuh,|huḥ,]] [[śrim,|śrīm,]] [[hrim|hrīm]] and says (I. 23) that ‘om Khakholkāya [[Sūryamurtayo namah’|Sūryamūrtaye namaḥ’]] is the mūlamantra of the Sun and this mantra is employed for sun-worship in an early digest like the Kṛtyakalpataru [[od|on]] vrata (p. 9 note). The [[Bhaviṣya-purana|Bhaviṣya-purāṇa]] (Brāhmaparva 215. 4) gives ‘Khakholkāya [[namah’|namaḥ’]] as the mūlamantra in Sun worship. The Bhaviṣya (Brāhma. 29. 9-15) provides that ‘gam svāhā’ is the mūlamantra of [[Ganapati|Gaṇapati]] worship, [[oites|cites]] the mantras for [[hṣdaya,|hṛdaya,]] [[sikhā,|śikhā,]] kavaca [[&o.|&c.]] and a Gāyatrī suited to [[Ganapati,|Gaṇapati.]] 137 Garuḍa I. 38 contains a long prose mantra of [[Cāmupdā.|Cāmuṇḍā.]] The Agni-

[[P1106]]purāṇa also contains many mantras of the Tāntrik [[typo.|type.]] [[Obap.|Chap.]] 121 verses 15-17 provide 138 for a mantra whereby [[enemeis|enemies]] are brought under control (as noted below) and [[versos|verses]] 17-19 speak of a mantra for paralysing ([[stambhand)|(stambhana)]] of an enemy. Chap. 133–135 of the Agni contain several mantras of the Tāntrik type. Chapter 307 contains several mantras of bringing under control the three worlds.

A mantra called Mahāśvetā is spoken of in the [[Bhaviṣya purāṇa|Bhaviṣya-purāṇa]] which is [[nientioned|mentioned]] in the [[Kr̥tyakalpataru|Kṛtyakalpataru]] on vrata (p. 9) and in [[Ekādaśitattva|Ekādaśītattva]] p. 40 and it is [[’ brām|‘hrām]] [[hrim|hrīm]] [[sah’|saḥ’]] and the japa of that mantra on Sunday with fast is said to yield what one desires. 139

The later medieval digests on [[Dharmaśāstra.|Dharmaśāstra]] adopt the [[Prānapratistha|Prāṇapratiṣṭhā]] mantra [[erolved|evolved]] by the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] (XXIII. 71–76). The [[Dorapratisthā|Devapratiṣṭhā]] tattva 140 (pp. 506-507) quotes [[there|these]] verses from [[Saradātilaka,|Śāradātilaka,]] sets out the mantra and explains the [[versos|verses]] at length. The Divyatattva also (pp. 609-610) does the same. The Vyavahāramayūkha (p. 86) sets out the same mantra for the consecration of the image of Dharma in the ordeal of Dharma without expressly naming the [[Sāradātilaka.|Śāradātilaka.]] The [[Nirnayasindhu|Nirṇayasindhu]] (pp. 349-350) has an elaborate rite of the [[Prānapratisthā|Prāṇapratiṣṭhā]] of images full of Tāntrik elements like the words [[vaṣat, brim, and phat|vaṣaṭ, hrīm, and phaṭ]] and employs the same procedure as the one prescribed by the [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] without expressly mentioning the latter. The [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] appears to follow earlier works like the [[Jayākhyasamhitā|Jayākhyasaṁhitā]] ([[Patala|Paṭala]] XX) and [[Prapaioasāra tantra|Prapañcasāra-tantra]] (35. 1-9 ff).

[[P1107]]Reasons of space [[preolude|preclude]] further illustrations of the use of Tāntrik mantras by medieval digests on [[Dharmasastra.|Dharmaśāstra.]] One matter of contrast between Vedic mantras and some Tāntrik mantras may be noticed. A Vedic mantra, according to Jaimini (I [[%.|2.]] 32), was significant, but the Tantras go so far as to teach the repetition of mantras that have no meaning or that [[oontain|contain]] the letters of the mantra in reversed form. For example, the [[Kālivilāsatantra|Kālīvilāsatantra]] (XXII. 21) says that the reverse of the three letters of the mantra ‘om Durge’, as ‘rgedu om’ yields all desires (‘rgedu om tryakṣaram mantram [[Sarvakāmaphala- *. pradan)’.|sarvakāmaphala-pradam)’.]] The [[Sad-dharmapundarika|Sad-dharmapuṇḍarīka]] 141 (a standard work of Mahāyāna Buddhism) chap. 21 (text edited by Kern and Bunyiu Nanjiu, 1912 and translated in [[8. B. E.|S. B. E.]] vol. XXI. pp. 370-375) contains spells (called [[Dhāranipadāni).|Dhāraṇīpadāni).]] It should not be supposed that spells were peculiar to Hindus or Buddhists. Many ancient peoples believed that words and letters have magic power and that belief led on to another belief that it did not matter even if the letters and words had no sense. E. J. Thomas in ‘History of Buddhist thought’ (reprint of 1953) states that spells similar to Hindu and Buddhist types exist in old English, Old High German and Keltic (p. 186).

The repetition of mantras, Vedic as well as Tāntrik, is called [[‘Purascaraña ‘|‘Puraścaraṇa’]] 142 (which literally means ‘performing or carrying out before’). The [[Mehāniryāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VII. 76-85) sets out [[Various|various]] modes of [[purasoarapa (|puraścaraṇa (]]brief as well as elaborate). One way is to bring together five tattvas on the 14th tithi of a dark fortnight or on a Tuesday or Saturday, then the [[Devi|Devī]] is to be worshipped and the worshipper is to repeat ten thousand [[timeg|times]] with single-mindedness the mantra in [[mahānisā|mahāniśā]] (midnight), then he is to feed persons devoted to brahman and becomes one who has carried out [[purascaraña.|puraścaraṇa.]] Another way is that he should repeat every day one thousand times the mantra beginning on [[&|a]] Tuesday up to (including) the next Tuesday, so that by repeating the mantra in [[al|all]] eight thousand times on eight days,

[[P1108]]he will be deemed to have performed [[purascarana.|puraścaraṇa.]] Sometimes it is prescribed 143 that a mantra such as [[‘Śivaya namah: (this|‘Śivāya namaḥ’ (this]] is five-lettered mantra) or [[‘Om Śivaya namah’|‘Om Śivāya namaḥ’]] (a [[sadaksera|ṣaḍakṣara]] mantra) should be repeated 24 lakhs of times and the [[sadhaka|sādhaka]] should offer into Agni twenty-four thousand [[ūlitis|āhutis]] of [[pāyasas -|pāyasa.]] Then the mantra becomes perfect and confers on the [[sadhaka|sādhaka]] whatever [[be|he]] desires. The [[Kulārpava|Kulārṇava]] states that [[Purasoarapa|Puraścaraṇa]] is so called because by the five-fold [[upīsınū|upāsanā]] the deity desired (as if) moves 144 before the worshipper for conferring on him her favour. A mantra like the Gāyatrī is 145 to be repeated every day 1008 or 108 or 10 times. This requirement is [[n: ‘. kooping|in keeping]] with what the Purāṇas and Dharmaśāstra works say. For example, the [[Naradapurana|Nāradapurāṇa]] (II. 57. 54) provides that a mantra is to be repeated 8, 28, or 108 times. The [[Ekadasitattva180m|Ekādaśītattva]] quotes [[Devipurāṇa|Devīpurāṇa]] for the proposition that the offerings in the [[home|homa]] to planets should be [[108. 28|108, 28]] or 8 according to one’s ability.

[[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] in his commentary on [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] (16. 56) has a very exhaustive and learned note on the details of [[purasoarana|puraścaraṇa]] that are common to all mantras. According to the [[Vayaviya-sambita|Vāyavīya-saṁhitā]] 146 the perfecting of the procedure of the mūlamantra is called [[purascaraṇa,|puraścaraṇa,]] since it has to be practised before the acts in which it is to be employed. The [[Kularpava|Kulārṇava]] 147 provides that there are at least five constituent elements of [[purasoarapa|puraścaraṇa]] viz. pūjā (worship of the desired [[deity )|deity)]] three times a day, japa (muttering of the mantra), [[tarpana,|tarpaṇa,]] homa, and

[[P1109]]dinner to brāhmaṇas; that if any one of these cannot be carried out, then he should perform japa of the mantra twice as many times more as the number prescribed for that constituent, that, in the absence of some constituents, by providing a good dinner to brāhmaṇas everything becomes successful and that if one mantra is perfected by performing the five constituents mentioned above, then he secures siddhi of all mantras. [[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] suggests another way of [[purascaraṇa|puraścaraṇa]] viz. one should be pure, 148 observe a fast and then in an eclipse of the sun or moon should stand in the navel-deep water of a river directly falling into the sea, should recite a mantra with concentration of mind from the time the eclipse begins till its end, thereafter perform homa and the rest in order reducing each to 1/10 of the number of japa recitation, then secure the guru’s contentment (by gifts) and that by this method mantra becomes perfect and the deity becomes favourable. The [[Kaulāvalinirnaya|Kaulāvalīnirṇaya]] describes (14th paṭala, verses 75–260) a terrible sādhanā whereby in a single night a sādhaka secures mantra-siddhi viz. by going to a cemetery or other lonely spot after one watch of the night, securing the corpse of a cāṇḍāla, or of one who is killed by some one with a sword or one bitten by a snake or a young handsome warrior dying in a battle (not killed by himself), he should wash the corpse, offer worship to it and to Durgā and repeat the mantra (‘om Durge Durge [[raksani|rakṣaṇi]] [[svaha’).|svāhā’).]] If he is not frightened by the terrific sights that he may see and after following a very long procedure he secures mantrasiddhi. The [[Tarabhaktisudhārpava|Tārābhaktisudhārṇava]] (taraṅga IX pp 345 ff) describes the ‘śavasādhana-vidhi’ and so does the [[Kulacūdamani-tantra|Kulacūḍāmaṇi-tantra]] (Tāntrik texts, vol. IV.) VI. 19-28.

[[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] further quotes a passage 149 to the effect that if the sādhaka satisfies his guru who is a form of the deity

[[P1110]]worshipped, he would secure perfection of mantra even without [[purasoaraṇa,|puraścaraṇa,]] that [[purascarapa|puraścaraṇa]] is the main seed (cause) of (the perfection of) mantras, that where the number of times [[&|a]] [[mantra.|mantra]] is to be recited is not expressly stated, it should be repeated 8000 times. [[Rāghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] quotes a verse saying that just as a man in the grip of diseases is incapable of performing all actions a mantra devoid of [[purascaraṇa|puraścaraṇa]] is declared to be in a similar condition.

The Agnipurāṇa, [[Kulārpava|Kulārṇava]] and [[Sāradatilaka|Śāradātilaka]] lay down rules about the places where [[purascarana of mantra|puraścaraṇa of a mantra]] is to be practised. The following places are commended 150 for those who are engaged in [[porfecting|perfecting]] a mantra, viz. holy places, river bank, caves, mountain top, ground near a [[tirtha,|tīrtha,]] confluence of rivers, holy forests and parks, the root of a [[llva|bilva]] tree, mountain slopes, temple, seashore, one’s own house or any place where the mind (of the sādhaka) feels happy. Rules were also laid down as to the food on which the sādhaka was to subsist during the days of [[purascarana|puraścaraṇa]] viz. food 151 obtained by begging for alms (for [[brahmacūrin|brahmacārin]] and [[yali),|yati),]] [[havisya|haviṣya]] food (prescribed for vratas), allowed vegetables, fruits, milk, bulbous roots, barley meal. The Mantramahodadhi (25. 66-71) sets out what is [[havisya|haviṣya]] food in śānti and proper food in the other cruel rites. [[Raghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] (on 16. 56) adds many rules from other sources, such as the sādhaka performing [[purascarapa|puraścaraṇa]] should avoid sexual [[intercounte,|intercourse,]] flesh, wine, should not speak with women and [[sūdras,|śūdras,]] nor speak untruth, nor pamper his [[sengos,|senses,]] should carry on the japa from morning till noon, should not allow a break, should repeat the mantra the same number of times every day.

[[P1111]]The [[Jayakhya-gamhita|Jayākhya-saṁhitā]] (19th [[patala,|paṭala,]] verses 13-33) says that, for three years after the sādhaka begins [[purascarada|puraścaraṇa]] of the mantra chosen, various obstacles and disturbances present themselves but if his mind and action are not affected by them, then from the 4th year he comes to be served by disciples that [[gurrender|surrender]] everything to him, that after seven years even proud kings approach him for favour, after the 9th year he notices many wondrous things such as joy, sound dreams, sweet music and fragrance, 152 hears loud vedic recitation, he eats and sleeps little (yet does not become [[loan),|lean),]] that these are signs of his having reached perfection in the mantra. The [[game|same]] work provides that such signs he should not divulge to any one but his own guru and if he divulges those happy signs to others siddhis run away from him (19. 34-37). The [[game|same]] [[Samhita|Saṁhitā]] (15. 186–188) provides that the syllables [[svābā, svādhā, phat,|svāhā, svadhā, phaṭ,]] hum and [[namah|namaḥ]] are to be respectively employed in homa, in rites for manes, destructive activities, creating hatred among friends and for securing [[mokslo|mokṣa.]] All [[tantrik|tāntrik]] works emphasize that mantra must be received from a qualified guru and the sādhanā of the mantra must be done under the guidance of the guru till the disciple himself becomes a siddha. As shown above it was believed that mantras would confer the highest spiritual and supernormal powers, would bring to the sādhaka all desired objects and mokṣa. The [[Kulārṇave|Kulārṇava]] states 153 [[‘It it deolared in the dootrine taught by Śiva that without dikṣā there would be no mokṣa, that diksa cannot be had without an ācārya ( guru) and that mantras would yield no fruit unless a guru instructs (a disciple ) about them’.|‘It is declared in the doctrine taught by Śiva that without dīkṣā there would be no mokṣa, that dīkṣā cannot be had without an ācārya (guru) and that mantras would yield no fruit unless a guru instructs (a disciple) about them’.]]

[[P1112]]It was further provided 154 that there was no siddhi (miraculous powers due to [[mantras )|mantras)]] nor mokṣa for him who had not had [[diksa|dīkṣā]] performed for him, that therefore a man should by all means receive dīkṣā from [[&|a]] guru and that when one is endowed with the ceremony of dīkṣā the distinctions based on caste vanish and a śūdra and a [[brābmana|brāhmaṇa]] cease to be so (when both have undergone dīkṣā). It was held that, if a person were to engage in the japa of a mantra written in a book, he would not secure siddhi and he would surely meet losses at every 155 step.

In the [[Mabānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] ([[IL|II.]] 14-15 and 20) it is said that Vedic mantras yielded desired results in Satya and other yugas, but in Kaliyuga they are like serpents without poison or like dead ones, that in the Kaliyuga the mantras declared in tantra works yield fruits quickly and are commended for employment in all actions such as japa and sacrifices. There is no such path as the one propounded in Tantras that would lead to [[nioksa|mokṣa]] or to happiness in this world and the next. The [[Mahānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] prescribes that [[’ om|‘oṁ]] [[sac-cid-ekam|sac-cid-ekaṁ]] brahma’ is the best of mantras (III. 14), that those who perform the [[upisanā|upāsanā]] of the highest brahma are in need of no other [[sādhani|sādhanā]] and that by the mere perfection of that mantra the individual soul merges in [[brahma,|brahma.]] 156 [[80|So]] So Mokṣa was one of the aims. Another aim was the attainment of miraculous or supernormal powers. The [[Prapa casāra|Prapañcasāra]] [[enume rates|enumerates]] 157 the eight siddhis and states that one who is endowed with these eight is a liberated soul and is [[apoken|spoken]] of as a yogin. The theory of siddhis is an ancient conception and occurs in the [[Apastamba-dharmasūtra.|Āpastamba-dharmasūtra.]] 158 The eight siddhis are named and

[[P1113]]explained in the Yogasūtrabhāṣya as follows 159 : Aṇiman (becoming small like an atom, atomization), laghiman (levitation), mahiman (becoming magnified like a mountain or the sky, magnification), prāpti (all objects becoming near to him such as touching the moon with one’s fingertip, extension), prākāmya (the non-obstruction of his desire such as diving into the earth underground and coming up as if he were in water), vaśitva (mastery of the five elements and their products such as a jar), īśitva (sovereignty over the production, absorption or arrangement of elements and their products), yatra-kāmāvasāyitva (the power to determine things according to his wish or will i. e. he may will that poison should have the effect of nectar and brings about that result). A siddha would be one who has secured these eight siddhis. The Gītā (X. 26) says that the sage Kapila is the great one among siddhas (‘siddhānāṁ kapilo muniḥ’). The Yogasūtra further speaks of siddhis as fivefold viz. proceeding from birth, from drugs, from mantras, from tapas (austerities) and from concentration. There were other objects also sought to be achieved by mantras, such as the six cruel rites and making women to have passion for a man. This shows that not only Tāntrikas, but those who practised yoga believed in the power of mantras to confer supernormal powers on the yogin. The Yogasūtra provides that some of the siddhis (as in III. 37) are obstacles to the attainment of samādhi and they are siddhis only to those who have awakened from trance. 160 [[Yāj|Yāj.]] (III. 202-203) provides that the power to disappear, to enter another body and temporarily to abandon one’s body, to create at one’s will [[objects-these|objects—these]] and other powers are characteristic signs of the attainment of siddhis by Yoga and that when one attains perfection in Yoga one can cast away the mortal coil and be able to become immortal in brahma.

A great deal is said about the six cruel rites in the Tantras such as Prapañcasāra (V), Śāradātilaka (23. 121-145), Śaktisaṅgamatantra (Kālīkhaṇḍa VIII. 102-106), Mantramahodadhi (25th Taraṅga). The Śāradātilaka (23. 137-141) deals with the six methods or arrangements of mantra along with the name of the enemy against whom the six rites were to be practised,

[[P1114]]viz. the arrangements called grathana, vidarbha, sampuṭa, rodhana, yoga and pallava. These are all passed over here. But it appears that even the early Purāṇas were influenced by rites of black magic. For example, the Matsya 161 says ‘in vidveṣaṇa (creating enmity among friends or among those who love one another) and in abhicāra, a triangular kuṇḍa is recommended and homa should be offered in it by priests that wear red flowers and red sandalwood paste, that wear the sacred thread in the nivīta way, that put on red turbans and red garments, the fuel sticks should be smeared with the fresh blood of crows collected in three vessels and should be offered with the left hand holding the bones of a hawk. The priests should have their hair untied on the head and should contemplate the befalling of evil on the enemy; they should recite the mantra ‘durmitriyās-tasmai santu’ and also the syllables ‘hrīm’ and ‘phaṭ’ and having recited over a razor the mantra used in śyenayāga, the priest should cut into pieces an effigy of the enemy with that razor and throw into the fire the pieces of the effigy. This rite yields results only here (in this world), but no good results in the next world and therefore one (who engages in this rite) should then perform a śānti. The Matsya also provides for a rite for bringing a person (or a woman) under control or for uccāṭana (chap. 93. 139-148). It is possible that the Tāntrikas and the Matsya both developed their magic rites of six kinds from the Śyenayāga mentioned in the Brāhmaṇas and Śrautasūtras. The Agnipurāṇa (chap. 138) also deals with these evil rites. The Ahirbudhnya-saṁhitā, though mainly a work of the Pāñcarātra cult, is full of lore on mantras. 162 In chap. 52 (verses

[[P1115]]2-88) it deals with the linguistic occultism of such mantras as ‘om namo Viṣṇave’. It says that mantras have three senses sthūla (gross), sūkṣma (subtle) and highest; chap. 51 explains these three about the Tāra or Tāraka mantra viz. Om.

It may be noted here that the Buddhist Tantras also claimed to show the way to the attainment of various objects, from success in love affairs to liberation and all these could be mostly secured by mantras. The Buddhist Tantra writers of the Vajrayāna school state that there were 84 siddhas, whose names are still cherished 163 and honoured in Nepal and Tibet. The Buddhists mentioned eight siddhis but they were somewhat different from those of the Yogasūtra. The Sādhanamālā mentions them as follows: Khaḍga (a sword over which mantras have been muttered whereby the user of it succeeds in battle), Añjana (collyrium applied to eyes which enables a person to see buried treasure), Pādalepa (ointment applied to the soles of the feet enabling a person to move anywhere undiscovered), Antardhāna (becoming invisible before the very eyes of persons watching him), Rasarasāyana (power of transmitting baser metals into gold or finding out an elixir for immortality), Khecara (being able to fly up in the sky), Bhūcara (going swiftly anywhere on the earth) and Pātālasiddhi (diving underneath the earth 164). As the Buddhist monks were to possess no property, they had a craving for wealth and supposed that by means of certain mantras, the lord of wealth (Kubera) would confer on them riches that would last for ever. 165 They also believed that by mantras they would make

[[P1116]]some of the Hindu gods their servants, viz. that they would be surrounded by many apsarases (heavenly damsels), that Indra would be their umbrella-bearer, Brahmā would be councillor and Hari door-keeper. 166 They wanted to defeat opponents in public discussions and to acquire proficiency in Śāstras without study through the power of mantras alone (vide Sādhanamālā Nos. 151, 155, 256). They were also anxious to cure and avert diseases and remove snake poison. They thought that by mantras they would attain to omniscience and Buddhahood (vide notes 1791-92).

As stated above (notes 1810-11) a mantra had to be received from a guru after initiation (dīkṣā). Therefore, a few words must be said about dīkṣā. The Tāntrikas did not discover any new concept in dīkṣā. From very ancient Vedic times there was upanayana regarded as the beginning of the spiritual birth of a boy and the sacrificer had to undergo a purificatory ceremony before embarking on the performance of a sacrifice, but both were not so elaborate as the dīkṣā described in some of the Tāntrika works. Vide H. of Dh. vol. II. pp. 1135-1140 for Vedic dīkṣā (in Agniṣṭoma), the observances and references to the Brāhmaṇas and the Śrautasūtras. The Tai. S. (VI. 1. 1-3 and VII. 4. 8) refers to dīkṣā and the Ait. Br. (I. 3) mentions the main items in Vedic dīkṣā, such as bath with sacred water on the sacrificer, smearing the face and other parts of the body with butter, applying collyrium to the eyes, purification of the sacrificer’s body twice by the adhvaryu with three bunches of seven darbhas each above the navel and then below it with mantras, entering a pavilion specially prepared for the sacrificer, covering him with a garment that envelopes him as a sack envelopes an embryo, dark antelope skin as an upper garment. The Śat. Br. also refers to the details of dīkṣā and states that thereby the sacrificer becomes one among the gods for the time being (vide III. 1. 2. 10-21, III. 1. 3. 7-28) and for the idea that dīkṣā symbolizes a new birth for the sacrificer. The Atharvaveda says ‘Great truth, formidable moral order, vows of initiation, austerities, prayer (or knowledge) and sacrifice support the earth.’ 167

[[P1117]]Dīkṣā is treated elaborately in some of the Tantras such as Prapañcasāra (V and VI), Kulārṇava (14. 39 ff), Śāradātilaka (4th paṭala), Nityotsava (pp. 4-10), Jñānārṇava (24th paṭala), Viṣṇusaṁhitā (X), Mahānirvāṇa (X. 112-119) and in Liṅgapurāṇa (II. 21). The Nirṇayasāgara Press brought out an edition (in 1935) of a work called Dīkṣāprakāśikā of Viṣṇubhaṭṭa, pupil of Satyānandanātha, composed in śaka 1719 (1797 A. D.). Almost all of them derive 168 the word from the root ‘dā’ (to give) and ‘kṣi’ to destroy, in various ways. The Kulārṇava defines it as ’the good call it dīkṣā because it confers a divine (holy) state and wipes off all sins, thereby freeing a man from the bondage of saṁsāra’; the Śāradātilaka states ‘since it confers divine knowledge and destroys sins it is named dīkṣā by the teachers that know the Tantras.’

The Śaktisaṅgama-tantra regards an eclipse of the sun or the moon (particularly of the moon) as the best time for undergoing initiation for a mantra from a guru and remarks that when performed in an eclipse, the week day, tithi, nakṣatra or month or Yoga or Karaṇa need not be considered. 169 The Kālīvilāsatantra states that if one is fortunate enough to secure Svāti nakṣatra and Friday on the 5th tithi of the dark half of Phālguna and undergoes dīkṣā on that date, that yields the rewards of a crore of ordinary dīkṣās (VI. 3-4). The N. S. (p. 67) quotes the Jñānārṇava to the effect that the dīkṣā as to a mantra should be on the day of a solar or lunar eclipse or within seven days from an eclipse and adds that the principal time is solar eclipse and quotes Kālottara to the effect that, if the time of a solar eclipse is secured for dīkṣā, one need not consider the month, tithi, the week day and the like. N. S. also quotes a passage from Yoginītantra condemning dīkṣā on a lunar eclipse. Vide Muhūrtakalpadruma of Viṭṭhala p. 94 verse 6 (Nirn. ed.) for further details about times and places for dīkṣā.

[[P1118]]The Agnipurāṇa (chapters 27, 81-89 and 304) deals with dīkṣā at great length and is full of Tāntrik mantras and rites, but reasons of space forbid any treatment of the same here. The Jñānārṇava (24. 45-53) provides that at the time of initiation (dīkṣā) the guru has to instruct the disciple or novice as regards the six cakras (Ādhāra up to Ājñā) together with the number of petals in each, the colour of each, the letters of the alphabet assigned to each.

Late Dharmaśāstra works relied for the initiation into mantras on tantra works. For example, the Dharmasindhu (p. 32) expressly says so. There is a difference between Dīkṣā and upadeśa, which latter means the communication of only a mantra in a solar or lunar eclipse, at a tīrtha, Siddhakṣetra (sacred region once inhabited by a siddha) or in a Śiva temple. Raghunandana in Dīkṣātattva (vol. II. pp. 645-659) gives a lengthy treatment of dīkṣā at the beginning of which he remarks that in the Śāradātilaka and other works numerous topics of dīkṣā are described but as in his time all those were not being followed he attempts only a brief treatment.

The Mahānirvāṇa states (X. 201-202) that the guru for giving dīkṣā when the disciple is a Śākta, Śaiva, Vaiṣṇava, Saura or a Gāṇapatya, should be of the same sect, but a kaula is a good guru for every one. The Mahānirvāṇa (X. 112) provides that a person does not become a Kaula by merely drinking wine, but he becomes so only after abhiṣeka. Then (in verses 113-193 of 10th ullāsa) the Mahānirvāṇa provides an elaborate procedure of complete abhiṣeka somewhat resembling Christian Baptism. There is first on the day previous to the day of abhiṣeka worship of Gaṇeśa, then of eight Śaktis (Brāhmī &c.), lokapālas and their weapons. Then the next day (i. e. the day of abhiṣeka) after a bath the neophyte donates sesame and gold for the removal of all sins and approaches a guru with the prayer to perform abhiṣeka. Then the guru draws a sarvatobhadra maṇḍala on an altar, sanctifies the five tattvas, places an auspicious jar and fills it with wine or with holy water. The principal part consists in the guru’s sprinkling with water the disciple to the accompaniment of 21 mantras (X. 160-180) invoking 170 the gods Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva, the Mātṛs, various Śaktis, the avatāras, the numerous forms of Devī, the dikpālas,

[[P1119]]the nine planets, nakṣatras, yogas, weekdays, karaṇas, seas, sacred rivers, nāgas, trees &c. Then the guru gives the disciple a new name ending in Ānandanātha, who honours the guru and other Kaulas present. This ceremony may go on for 9, 7, 5, 3 nights or one night. Vide Tantrarājatantra II. 58-72, Jñānasiddhi (XVII) for similar procedure of abhiṣeka. Vide the Introduction to the Sekoddeśaṭīkā, a Buddhist Tāntrik work (in G. O. S.) edited by Mario E. Carelli, for its resemblance to the Christian rite of Baptism. The Ahirbudhnya-saṁhitā (chap. 39) provides a procedure of Mahābhiṣeka as one remedy against all diseases, as one destroying all enemies and for the attainment of all desired objects.

There were four kinds of dīkṣās, Kriyāvatī, Varṇamayī, Kalāvatī and Vedhamayī and there were elaborate rules about vāstuyāga, construction of maṇḍapa, kuṇḍas and sthaṇḍila, which are all passed over for reasons of space.

[[In JRAS for 1906 pp. 355-362 B, C, Majumdar endeavours to show tbat the two hymns to Dorgā are late interpolations in the Mahā. bbārata, probably derived from practices of god-Aryan Sūdras in Oriya speaking bill tribes of Sambalpar. But he forgets that apart from other sources Kalidasa (not later than about 400 A, D.) speaks of Parvati as Umā, Aparnā, Dargā, Gauri, Bbavāni and Candi in his several works and also that Kālidāsa refers to the Ardbanārīśvara form of Śiva. In the last verse of the Sakuntala Kalidāsa speaks of Śiva as ‘parigata-faktih’ and thereby suggests that at least tbe germs of the later Sakti worsbip were not unknown in his times. Therefore, the worsbip of Durgā in her several aspects is older than 300 A. D. by at least a century or more; vide pp. 185-186 above.|In JRAS for 1906 pp. 355–362 B. C. Majumdar endeavours to show that the two hymns to Durgā are late interpolations in the Mahābhārata, probably derived from practices of non-Aryan Śūdras in Oriya-speaking hill tribes of Sambalpur. But he forgets that apart from other sources Kālidāsa (not later than about 400 A. D.) speaks of Pārvatī as Umā, Aparṇā, Durgā, Gaurī, Bhavānī and Caṇḍī in his several works and also that Kālidāsa refers to the Ardhanārīśvara form of Śiva. In the last verse of the Śākuntala Kālidāsa speaks of Śiva as ‘parigata-śaktiḥ’ and thereby suggests that at least the germs of the later Śakti worship were not unknown in his times. Therefore, the worship of Durgā in her several aspects is older than 300 A. D. by at least a century or more; vide pp. 185–186 above.]] ↩︎

  33. [[Dr. Bhattacharya in Iatro, to Gabyasa māja p. XXXIV holds that Asanga is probably the author of the Gobyasamāja and therefore that work belongs to the 3rd or 4th century A, D, One has only to read the Maba. yānasātrālaikāra of Asanga ed, by Sylvain Levi and compare its refined and correct Sanskrit with the rather barbarous Sanskrit of the Gohyasamāja to come to the conclusion that the latter is not Asanga’s work. [[वीरसाधनकर्माणि पञ्चतस्वोदितानि च। मधे मांस तथा मत्स्यहनानैथुनमेव च। पतानि पश्च तस्यानि खया प्रोक्तानि शङ्कररा महानिर्वाण I. 37; साधकs are of three kind. पा, वीर and दिग्य. vide शक्तिसङ्गमतन्त्र, कालीखण्ड VI. 21, महानिर्वाण I.51 and 55. IV. 18-19, कोलावलीनिर्णय VII. 186. कुलाचारं विना देवि शक्तिमन्त्री न सिद्धियः । तस्माकुलाचाररतः साधयेच्छक्तिसाधनम्। मयं मांस तथा मत्स्यं मुदा मैथुनमेव च । शक्तिपूजा: विधावाये पञ्चतत्त्वं प्रकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण V.21-22. आये is vocative of आधा applied to शक्ति, the spouse of शिव. The कोलावलीनिर्णय states चणिका पूजयेद्यस्तु विना पशमकारकैः। चत्वारि तस्य नश्यन्ति आयुर्विद्या यशोधनम् ॥ मयं मांसं…मैथुनमेव च।… मकारपश्चकं देवि देवताप्रीतिदायकम् ॥…विना पञ्चोपचारं हि देवीपूजां करोति यः। योगिनीनो भवेब्रक्ष्यः पापं चैव पदे पदे॥ IV. 24-28; besides, कौलावलीनिर्णय II. 101-105 are moreatriking: संस्थाप्य वामभागेतु शक्ति स्वामिपरायणाम्।…विना शक्त्या या पूजा विफला मात्र संशयः। तस्माच्यक्तियुतो पीरो भवेश्च यत्नपूर्वकम् । या शक्ति सा महादेवी हररूपस्त साधकः अयोग्यचिन्तनाम्चैव देवस्वमुपजायते।…शक्तिविनापि पूजायां माधिकारी भवेचना.|वीरसाधनकर्माणि पञ्चतत्त्वोदितानि च। मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यमुद्रामैथुनमेव च। एतानि पञ्च तत्त्वानि त्वया प्रोक्तानि शङ्कर ॥ महानिर्वाण I. 57; साधकs are of three kinds पशु, वीर and दिव्य. Vide शक्तिसङ्गमतन्त्र, कालीखण्ड VI. 21, महानिर्वाण I. 51 and 55, IV. 18-19, कौलावलीनिर्णय VII. 186. कुलाचारं विना देवि शक्तिमन्त्रो न सिद्धिदः । तस्मात्कुलाचाररतः साधयेच्छक्तिसाधनम् । मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यं मुद्रां मैथुनमेव च । शक्तिपूजा-विधावाद्ये पञ्चतत्त्वं प्रकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण० V. 21-22. आद्ये is vocative of आद्या applied to शक्ति, the spouse of शिव. The कौलावलीनिर्णय states चण्डिकां पूजयेद्यस्तु विना पञ्चमकारकैः। चत्वारि तस्य नश्यन्ति आयुर्विद्या यशोधनम् ॥ मद्यं मांसं…मैथुनमेव च।… मकारपञ्चकं देवि देवताप्रीतिदायकम् ॥…विना पञ्चोपचारं हि देवीपूजां करोति यः। योगिनीनां भवेद्भक्ष्यः पापं चैव पदे पदे॥ IV. 24-28; besides, कौलावलीनिर्णय II. 101-105 are more striking: संस्थाप्य वामभागे तु शक्तिं स्वामिपरायणाम् ।…विना शक्त्या तु या पूजा विफला नात्र संशयः। तस्माच्छक्तियुतो वीरो भवेच्च यत्नपूर्वकम् । या शक्तिः सा महादेवी हररूपस्तु साधकः । अन्योन्यचिन्तनाच्चैव देवत्वमुपजायते।…शक्तिं विनापि पूजायां नाधिकारी भवेत्क्वचित् ॥]] ↩︎

  39. श्रीकाल्युपासका ये च तत्फलं परिकीर्तितम् । [[तेषां समूहोदेवेशि कुलं सङ्कीर्तित भया॥|तेषां समूहो देवेशि कुलं सङ्कीर्तितं मया ॥]] [[शक्तिसहम,|शक्तिसङ्गम,]] कालीखण्ड chap. 3. 32; [[मयं मांस तथा मत्स्य मुद्रा मैथुनमेव च। एभिरेव कृता पूजा कुलाचारः प्रकीर्तितः । शक्तिसङ्गमा,|मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यं मुद्रा मैथुनमेव च । एभिरेव कृता पूजा कुलाचारः प्रकीर्तितः ॥ शक्तिसङ्गम०,]] ताराखण्ड, 36th पटल, verses 18-20; कुलं गोत्रं समाख्यातं तच्च [[शक्तिशिवोद्भवम्।|शक्तिशिवोद्भवम् ।]] येन मोक्ष इति [[ज्ञान कोलिका सोभि. धीयते॥|ज्ञानं कौलिकः सोऽभि-धीयते ॥]] [[अकुल|अकुलं]] शिव इत्युक्तं कुलं शक्तिः प्रकीर्तिता। [[कुलाकुलानुसन्धानानिपुणाः कौलिका मिये।|कुलाकुलानुसन्धाननिपुणाः कौलिकाः प्रिये ॥]] कुलार्णव 17. 26-27. No. 994 of 1891-95 folio 13 b) states [[मध…मैथुनमेव च। भाग्यहीना (नः1) न लग्यन्ते मकाराः पञ्च दुर्लभाः”.|‘मद्यं…मैथुनमेव च । भाग्यहीना(नः ?) न लभ्यन्ते मकाराः पञ्च दुर्लभाः॥’.]] ↩︎

  40. एकः परमात्मा। ईश्वराः सप्त। असंख्या जीवाः । [[ब्रह्मविष्णुशिवसूर्यगणेशशक्ति. भैरवाश्वेश्वराः।|ब्रह्मविष्णुशिवसूर्यगणेशशक्ति-भैरवाश्चेश्वराः।]] पारानन्दे मते त्रयो मार्गाः। दक्षिणः । वामः। [[उत्तः।|उत्तरः ।]] तथैव [[गाथाइदाहरन्ति।|गाथामुदाहरन्ति ।]] दक्षिणादुत्तमं धाम [[घामादुत्तरमुत्तमम् ।|वामादुत्तरमुत्तमम् ।]] उत्तरादुत्तमं [[किंचिव|किंचिन्न]] [[ब्रह्माण्डमण्डले।|ब्रह्माण्डमण्डले ॥]] [[वामाचारी विषकारो मध्यमोसमभेदेन।|वामाचारो द्विपकारो मध्यमोत्तमभेदेन ।]] [[मयमैथुनमुदाभिर्युक्तोऽसाघुत्तमः|मद्यमैथुनमुद्राभिर्युक्तोऽसावुत्तमः]] स्मृत इति। [[मद्यमांसमस्य सुवामैथुनयुक्तो|मद्यमांसमत्स्य-मुद्रामैथुनयुक्तो]] मध्यमः। पारानन्दसूत्र (G.O.S.) [[Pp|pp]] 1-3, 13; compare कुलार्णवतन्त्र [[1.7-8|II. 7-8]] ‘वैष्णवादुत्तमं शैवं [[शैवान|शैवाद]] [[वक्षिणमुत्तमम् ।|दक्षिणमुत्तमम् ।]] दक्षिणादुत्तमं [[वाम घामात|वामं वामात्]] सिद्धान्तमुत्तमम् । [[सिद्धान्तादुत्तम कौलं कोलात्परतरं नाहि..|सिद्धान्तादुत्तमं कौलं कौलात्परतरं नहि ॥’.]] वामाचार was possibly so called because woman [[(urat)|(वामा)]] played an important part in [[It|it]] or it was secretly practised (which is a crooked way). ↩︎

[[स्वेच्छामतुमती शक्ति साक्षात् ब्रह्म न संशयः। तस्माता पूजयेडक्रया पचालङ्कारः भोजनैरिति रियो देवाः खियः प्राणाः श्रिय एव हि भूषणम् । श्रीणां निन्दा न कर्तव्या मचता: क्रोधयेदपि । इति । देवान् गुसन्ममम्यर्च्य वेदतन्त्रोक्तवर्मना। देवं स्मरन् पिबन् मचे वेश्या गच्छन दोषभाक् ॥ इति । सेवेदात्ममुखार्थ यो मद्यादिकमशानतः। स पाति नरकं घोरं मात्र कार्या विचारणा यः शास्त्रविधि …पर गतिम् ॥ इति। यावत चलते हटियावत चलते मनः। तावत्पानं प्रकुर्वीत पशुपानमितःपरम् । इति। जीवन्मुक्तः पिवेदेवमन्यथा पतितो भवेत् । इति। पारानन्द. pp. 16-17 sutras 64, 65, 74-76, 80-81. Extravagant praise is bestowed on women in several tantras such as in Saktisaagama-tantra, Kalithanda 3. 142-144 and Tarakhanda 13.43-50 and in कोलावलीनिर्णयx.88. The hall verse ‘त्रियो…भूषणम्! occurs in शक्तिसङ्ग्मतन्त्र, ताराखण्ड 23.10. The verse यः शात्र is भगवद्वीता 16. 23. For यावा…परम् compare कुलार्णवतन्त्र VII. 97-98, The qurfa states that every woman is born in the kula of the great Mother and so one should not beat a womad even with a dower even if sho be guilty of a hundred misdeeds, one should not siad the faults of women and should make known only their good points (XI. 64-65). Vido dlso कोलावलीनिर्णय X.66-69,|स्वेच्छानुमती शक्तिः साक्षाद् ब्रह्म न संशयः। तस्मात्तां पूजयेद्भक्त्या वस्त्रालङ्कार-भोजनैरिति ॥ स्त्रियो देवाः स्त्रियः प्राणाः स्त्रिय एव हि भूषणम् । स्त्रीणां निन्दा न कर्तव्या न च ताः क्रोधयेदपि ॥ इति। देवान् गुरून्नमस्कृत्य वेदतन्त्रोक्तवर्त्मना। देवं स्मरन् पिबन् मद्यं वेश्यां गच्छन्न दोषभाक् ॥ इति। सेवेतात्मसुखार्थं यो मद्यादिकमशान्तधीः। स याति नरकं घोरं नात्र कार्या विचारणा॥ यः शास्त्रविधि…परां गतिम् ॥ इति। यावन्न चलते दृष्टिर्यावन्न चलते मनः। तावत्पानं प्रकुर्वीत पशुपानमितःपरम् । इति । जीवन्मुक्तः पिबेदेवमन्यथा पतितो भवेत् ॥ इति । पारानन्द० pp. 16-17 sūtras 64, 65, 74-76, 80-81. Extravagant praise is bestowed on women in several tantras such as in Śaktisaṅgama-tantra, Kālīkhaṇḍa 3. 142-144 and Tārākhaṇḍa 13. 43-50 and in कौलावलीनिर्णय X. 88. The कुलार्णव states that every woman is born in the kula of the great Mother and so one should not beat a woman even with a flower even if she be guilty of a hundred misdeeds, one should not find the faults of women and should make known only their good points (XI. 64-65).   107. शेषतत्त्वं महेशानि निर्वीर्ये प्रबले कलौ । स्वकीया केवलं ज्ञेया सर्वदोषविवर्जिता ॥ अथवा स्वयम्भावि कुसुमं प्राणवल्लभे । कथितं तत्प्रतिनिधौ कुसीदं परिकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण० VI. 14–15; अत्र means ‘in offering शेषतत्त्व’ (i. e. the 5th). The com. explains ‘कुसीदं रक्तचन्दनम्’. Women that could be śaktis were of three classes, स्वीया (one’s own wife), परकीया (wife of another) and साधारणी (a woman who is a veśyā). ↩︎

  110. Sir [[Jobo|John]] Woodroffe remarks (in the [[latrodaction|Introduction]] to his [[. Priaci ples|‘Principles]] of Tantra’ part 2, pp. XII-XIV) that the one topic that appears to [[diferentiate|differentiate]] Tantras from other religious works is the [[constitaent|constituent]] parts of its ritual such as the mantras, [[bijas, modrās|bījas, mudrās]], yantras, [[bhutasuddhi|bhūtaśuddhi]] and that it is mainly by these items rather than by anything else that [[tho tāatrik|the tāntrik]] character of a work is established. Vide also [[the Sāktas.|‘the Śāktas’]] by E. [[A,|A.]] Payne p. 137 for a similar view. Sir[[, Jobo| John]] Woodroffe, while reviewing [[Payao’s|Payne’s]] work (in JRAS for 1935 at p. 387), himself agreed that what distinguished the Śākta ritual is the [[maptra|mantra]] and magical sections and that part of it which deals with the secret ritual, that, while there is ordinarily [[do thoga|no bhoga]] (enjoyment) where there is yoga, in the Śākta doctrine a man may [[hava borb|have both]] yoga and bhoga and that this is a [[distiact|distinct]] and profound characteristic of that doctrine. Even the Buddhist [[Vajrayapa Tantra porport|Vajrayāna Tantras purport]] to aim at the [[attaiament|attainment]] of bodhi (vide [[Gahyasamāja|Guhyasamāja]] p. 154, [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] I. p. 225 and II. [[P.|p.]] 421) and [[ARE 14 # T: HATGIT: H r vats i a पशान्ति पर बोधिजन्मनी हैव साधका|ज्ञानसिद्धि I. 4 ‘ये तु सत्त्वाः समाख्याताः सर्वसङ्कल्पवर्जिताः । ते स्पृशन्ति परां बोधिं जन्मनीहैव साधकाः ॥’]] ↩︎

  135. [[3 horario geht maraf va guidata i nieara acha gente i|ओं मणिधारे हूं । लक्षजापेनार्या अग्रत उपतिष्ठति । यदिच्छति तत्सर्वं ददाति ।]] विना मण्डलकस्नानोपवासेन केवलं जापमात्रेण सिध्यति सर्व कार्य च साधयति । साधनमाला vol. I. p. 221. [[SITUT|आर्या]] here means Goddess [[Tāra.|Tārā.]] The most famous [[maotra|mantra]] among Buddhists is [[‘Oma Manipadme hūm’|‘Om Maṇipadme hūm’]] where ‘maṇipadme’ is vocative (and probably refers to Tārā deity, who has a jewel [[lotas).|lotus).]] Vide Dr. [[F, W.|F. W.]] Thomas in JRAS for 1906 p. 464. It is often rendered as [[‘Hail, the jewel lotas’.|‘Hail, the jewel lotus’.]] While these pages were [[passiog|passing]] through the press the present [[autbor|author]] received a work called ‘Foundations of Tibetan mysticism’ according to the Esoteric [[Teacbi ngs|Teachings]] of the Great Mantra ‘Om [[Maṣi|Maṇi]] Padme Hūm’ by Lama Anagarika Govinda ([[pob.|pub.]] by Rider and [[Co,|Co.]] London, 1959). It is impossible to deal with this work in this note. He states that the Mantra [[“Om’|‘Om’]] &c. is dedicated to [[Avalokitesvara|Avalokiteśvara]] (a fine [[pbotographic|photographic]] plate of whom occurs as the Frontispiece). None who is not a confirmed Tibetan Buddhist scholar or [[mook|monk]] will accept [[bis|his]] interpretations of the words of the mantra. On [[P.|p.]] 27 [[be|he]] states that the mantra is pronounced in Tibet as ‘Om [[Mani|Maṇi]] Peme Hūm’ and that the complete formula is ‘Om… Hūm, [[Hrih’|Hrīḥ’]] (p. 230); on pp. 84 [[f,|ff.]] he scouts the idea that tāntrism is a Hinduistic reaction taken over by later Buddhist schools. He offers different esoteric [[meanlags|meanings]] of the words of the mantra, e. g. [[op|on]] p. 130 he says ‘om is the ascent towards universality, Hūm is the descent of the states of [[aniversality|universality]] into the depth of the [[buman beart’:|human heart’;]] on p. 131 ‘Om is the infinite, but [[Hum|Hūm]] is the [[sofinite|infinite]] in the finite, the eternal in the temporal’ &c.; on p. 230 ‘In the Om, we [[experi epce tbe|experience the]] Dharmakāya and the mystery of [[tbe upiversal|the universal]] body, in [[mani’|‘maṇi’]] the Sambhogakāya, in the Padma we experience the [[Nirmāṇakaya,|Nirmāṇakāya,]] [[ia|in]] the [[Hum|Hūm]] we experience the Vajrakāya as synthesis of the transcendental body of [[tbe|the]] three mysteries; [[io the Hrih,|in the Hrīḥ,]] we dedicate the totality of our transformed personality to the service of [[Amitabba;|Amitābha;]] on p. 256 ’thus [[Om.. Hum|Om…Hūm]] embodies the [[bappy|happy]] tidings of liberation, of the love [[towarde|towards]] all living beings and of the way that leads to [[figal|final]] realization.’ The present [[autbor|author]] is [[constraised|constrained]] to say that by similar arbitrary interpretations [[tbe|the]] words of any mantra may be [[mado|made]] to yield similar [[soppos.|senses.]] ↩︎

  142. [[Puraścaraña|Puraścaraṇa]] of a mantra has several constituent elements viz. dhyāna (meditation on the form of the deity to be worshipped), worship, japa of the mantra, homa, [[tarpapa,|tarpaṇa,]] abhiṣeka and feeding brāhmaṇas. ‘In a brief [[purascarana|puraścaraṇa]] the first three alone are included. [[Tarpana|Tarpaṇa]] means satiating with water the deity and the pitṛs. ↩︎

  152. The Yogasūtra III. 36 and [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] thereon state that some of the powers developed are that the yoga expert hears divine music and receives the impression of fragrant scents. In ‘Bengal Lancer’ F. Yeats-Brown (London, 1930) narrates (pp. 246–247) how he had the experience that the room in which he and his American friends were sitting was filled by a yogic teacher who wore nothing but his dhoti with the [[perfame|perfume]] of [[attar|aṭṭar]] of roses, then with the scent of violets, [[mask,|musk,]] sandalwood, with nothing else with him except cotton wool on which he focussed a magnifying glass. The [[Sv. Up. II, 13|Śv. Up. II. 13]] details the first [[sigas|signs]] of the effectiveness of yoga practices [[‘लवत्वमारोग्यमलोलपत्वं वर्णप्रसावः स्वरसौष्ठवं च । गन्धः शुभो मूत्रपुरीषमल्पं योगमवृचिं gurat tartallo,|‘लघुत्वमारोग्यमलोलुपत्वं वर्णप्रसादः स्वरसौष्ठवं च । गन्धः शुभो मूत्रपुरीषमल्पं योगप्रवृत्तिं प्रथमां वदन्ति ॥’.]] ↩︎

  157. अणिमा महिमा च तथा गरिमा लघिमोशिता वशित्वं च । प्राप्तिः प्राकाम्यं चेत्यष्टैश्वर्याणि योगयुक्तस्य ॥ अष्टैश्वर्यसमेतो जीवन्मुक्तः प्रवक्ष्यते योगी । प्रपञ्चसार 19. 62-63. [[दद्याच्च दिव्यभावं क्षिणुयाद् दुरितान्यतो भवेद्दीक्षा । प्रपञ्चसार V. 2; दिव्यभाव- प्रदानाच्च क्षालनात्कल्मषस्य च । दीक्षेति कथिता सद्भिर्भवबन्धविमोचनात् ॥ कुलार्णव 17. 51 (here क्ष is said to represent क्षालन and not क्षय as in others); दिव्यज्ञानं यतो दद्यात्कुर्यात्पापस्य संक्षयम् । तस्माद्दीक्षेति संप्रोक्ता देशिकैस्तन्त्रवेदिभिः ॥ शारदा० IV. 2.|दद्याच्च दिव्यभावं क्षिणुयाद् दुरितान्यतो भवेद्दीक्षा । प्रपञ्चसार V. 2; दिव्यभावप्रदानाच्च क्षालनात्कल्मषस्य च । दीक्षेति कथिता सद्भिर्भवबन्धविमोचनात् ॥ कुलार्णव 17. 51 (here क्ष is said to represent क्षालन and not क्षय as in others); दिव्यज्ञानं यतो दद्यात्कुर्यात्पापस्य संक्षयम् । तस्माद्दीक्षेति संप्रोक्ता देशिकैस्तन्त्रवेदिभिः ॥ शारदा० IV. 2.]] ↩︎

[[चन्द्रसूर्यग्रहे दीक्षा सर्वदीक्षाशुभा मता । चन्द्रग्रहे शक्तिदीक्षा सर्वदीक्षोत्तमोत्तमा । न वार-तिथि-ऋक्षादि न मासनियमस्तथा । न योगकरणं वापि न विचारं समाचरेत् । शक्तिसङ्गम, ताराखण्ड 17. 36-38; मन्त्रदीक्षाप्रकारस्तन्त्रे द्रष्टव्यः । दीक्षाग्रहणमुपदेशस्याप्युपलक्षणम् । युगे युगे तु दीक्षासीदुपदेशः कलौ युगे । यद्यर्कसूर्यग्रहे तीर्थे सिद्धक्षेत्रे शिवालये । मन्त्रमात्रप्रकथन- मुपदेशः स उच्यते । धर्मसिन्धु p. 32.|चन्द्रसूर्यग्रहे दीक्षा सर्वदीक्षाशुभा मता । चन्द्रग्रहे शक्तिदीक्षा सर्वदीक्षोत्तमोत्तमा । न वार-तिथि-ऋक्षादि न मासनियमस्तथा । न योगकरणं वापि न विचारं समाचरेत् । शक्तिसङ्गम, ताराखण्ड 17. 36-38; मन्त्रदीक्षाप्रकारस्तन्त्रे द्रष्टव्यः । दीक्षाग्रहणमुपदेशस्याप्युपलक्षणम् । युगे युगे तु दीक्षासीदुपदेशः कलौ युगे । यद्यर्कसूर्यग्रहे तीर्थे सिद्धक्षेत्रे शिवालये । मन्त्रमात्रप्रकथनमुपदेशः स उच्यते । धर्मसिन्धु p. 32.]] ↩︎
